7. acetyl hexapeptide-1 (Melitane) 5% leave-on lotion at a concealed retroauricular test zone, replacing the prostaglandin arm entirely (H051) Novel candidate — TOP PICK

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1567 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (leave-on lotion, concealed retroauricular or mid-occipital test zone) · origin: evolution (round 4) · theme: Direction-of-effect as the run's real…

Mechanism
Both parents wanted the same thing: push melanin output in follicles that still contain functional bulb melanocytes, via the cAMP-MITF-tyrosinase axis. They reached it through FP-receptor agonism with a prostaglandin, and inherited three problems that are properties of the molecule rather than of the protocol - prescription-only access against an explicitly OTC-preferring run, epidermal hyperpigmentation that is the drug working correctly in the wrong compartment, and a periocular tail risk (iris pigmentation, periorbital fat atrophy) made worse by the hairline siting. This evolution reaches the same MITF switch from the physiological upstream end instead: acetyl hexapeptide-1 is an alpha-MSH biomimetic acting at MC1R, the canonical receptor whose cAMP output drives MITF and tyrosinase, reported to raise melanin synthesis and melanin transfer to keratinocytes and to shift hair-bulb cells from a low- to a high-pigmented state. Because it acts through the pigmentary unit's own receptor at cosmetic concentrations rather than by broad prostaglandin signalling in every cell it contacts, the diffuse epidermal-staining liability that dominated H036 largely dissolves - and with it the entire unquantified morning-wash-off argument, which the reviewer showed was the hypothesis's only novel content and rested on an unsupported partitioning assumption.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 supplies the verified alpha-MSH to MC1R to cAMP to MITF framework that this agent engages directly rather than by analogy. PMID 30222197 shows for the same peptide class that MC1R agonism raises melanin production with elevated MC1-R, TRP-1 and Melan-A and reduced ASIP in vitro and in ex vivo human hairs - and its 15-volunteer arm measured MC1-R protein on plucked hairs at three months as target engagement, not hair colour, so it is cited here as receptor-level support and nothing more. For acetyl hexapeptide-1 specifically the human data are one uncontrolled case report of Melitane 5% given with oral hair supplements (PMID 31831925), which is anecdote grade and confounded by the co-intervention; the rest is supplier data. PMID 36853923 supplies the constraint the H036 reviewer insisted on: greying is melanocyte and MelSC loss, so a fully white follicle has no target and only partially depigmented units can respond. The two operational demands from that review are executed. First, relocation: the test zone moves from the hairline and temple - the most photo-exposed, least concealable margin, and the one nearest the eye - to a retroauricular or mid-occipital 2 cm square that is genuinely hidden and nowhere near the periocular region. Second, an enrolment criterion: trichoscopy at 20x, and a zone qualifies only if it is salt-and-pepper with a majority of pigmented shafts present, excluding uniformly white zones where non-response is guaranteed by biology and would be misread as drug failure. PMID 33382172 is noted as a caution rather than support: MC1R proved dispensable for acute stress-induced greying in mice, so MC1R agonism should not be expected to rescue the stress-driven arm of the disease. (refs: PMID 33320376, PMID 30222197, PMID 31831925, PMID 36853923, PMID 33382172, PMID 12219263, PMID 15854125)
Safety
Cosmetic-grade OTC peptide lotion; no prescriber, no off-label prescribing, no cost barrier at scalp scale - this is the access fix, and it is the single largest improvement over both parents. Negligible systemic absorption of a lipid-modified hexapeptide; realistic worst case is local irritation or contact sensitisation, so patch-test three days first. The specific harms that made the prostaglandin parents uncomfortable are removed rather than mitigated: no documented iris pigmentation, no periorbital fat atrophy, no eyelash hypertrichosis - the class effects catalogued for latanoprost in PMID 12219263 and reproduced as follicular melanogenesis in the murine work of PMID 15854125. Residual and honestly stated risk: a melanogenesis stimulant applied to skin can in principle darken the treated skin, so the stop rule from the parents is retained - discontinue on visible skin darkening, perifollicular blotching, or persistent erythema. Concealed siting means any such change is hidden and reversible.
Novelty rationale
The material change is deleting the drug, not defending it. H036's entire novelty was a contact-time-limited wash-off scheme invented to contain prostaglandin staining; the reviewer showed the epidermis is the reservoir the follicle loads through, so washing at hour eight removes surface residue rather than partitioned drug, and the claimed selectivity was never estimated. Rather than quantify an assumption that is probably wrong, this version removes the liability at its source by switching to an MC1R biomimetic that reaches the same MITF switch, and the wash-off step disappears with it. It simultaneously fixes the access failure that both parent reviewers flagged - prescription-only, expensive, off-label - by moving to an OTC cosmetic active, and it executes the two concrete demands from the H036 review: relocation to a genuinely concealed and non-periocular zone, and a trichoscopic enrolment criterion that excludes fully white zones where non-response is preordained. The melatonin arm that H027 bolted on remains deleted so the melanogenesis mechanism is tested cleanly.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 3/10, safety 8/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict revise. Deleting the prostaglandin is right - the access, epidermal-staining and periocular objections were properties of the molecule - but what is left is largely a duplicate of H050 with weaker evidence and a weaker design, and the two should not both proceed. (1) Redundancy: H050 and H051 are both OTC alpha-MSH-biomimetic peptides acting on MC1R-cAMP-MITF, differing mainly in supplier and in where the test square sits. Run one MC1R arm, and it should be the one with follicle-derived-cell catalase/H2O2 data, i.e. H050. (2) Evidence transfer is the real weakness. PMID 30222197 is a study of palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20, a different molecule; 'for the same peptide class' is doing heavy lifting, and class-level extrapolation between a palmitoylated tetrapeptide and an acetylated hexapeptide is exactly the kind of inference this run has been penalising. Strip it and the molecule-specific human evidence is PMID 31831925 alone - a single 14-year-old on Melitane 5% plus oral hair supplements, reporting 'boosting improvement in hair color' with no objective measure, no control and an obvious co-intervention. That is the thinnest human anchor in the batch. The remaining support is supplier data, correctly labelled but not independent. (3) Design regression versus its sibling: there is no vehicle-control side and no blinded grading here. H050 specifies paired squares, randomised sides, identical vehicle and blinded band scoring; H051 specifies a concealed zone and a trichoscopic enrolment gate but leaves the outcome to be judged on a single treated patch. For a slowly progressive, subjectively scored, high-expectation cosmetic endpoint that is not adequate - add the vehicle square and the blinded reader, or the result is uninterpretable. (4) The relocation, though well-motivated, fights the disease's distribution: premature canities starts temporally and frontally (PMID 28584374: temporal 35.2%, frontal 18.3%; PMID 36386090: vertex 42.5%), so a retroauricular/mid-occipital square that also satisfies the 'majority pigmented, salt-and-pepper' enrolment criterion may simply not exist in early-stage subjects, or will contain too few grey shafts to power a band score. Reconcile concealment with where the grey actually is, or state the recruitment yield you expect. (5) Good marks for the two honest constraints: PMID 36853923 to exclude fully white zones where non-response is preordained, and PMID 33382172 as a scope limit that MC1R agonism should not be expected to rescue the stress arm. Revise: merge into the H050 arm or, if kept separate, add vehicle control and blinding and drop the cross-molecule evidence transfer.

  • OpenTargets: error — no chembl_id available
  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

8. palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 - gating ex vivo package run BEFORE any human trial: independent replication of the melanogenic effect in organ-cultured human anagen follicles, plus a follicular pharmacokinetic dose-finding study asking whether the peptide reaches the bulb at all (H079) Novel candidate — TOP PICK

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1566 · confidence 3/5 · route: topical, tested ex vivo (Franz-cell human scalp skin and microdissected human anagen follicle organ culture; no human subjects) · origin: evolution (round 6) · theme: The only surviving molecular candidates…

Mechanism
This evolution does not propose a new agent or a new mechanism; it inserts the experiment that has to come first and that nobody in six rounds has specified. Two unexamined assumptions sit under every version of the MC1R arm. The first is pharmacokinetic: the published human application was a 10 ppm (0.001%) leave-on lotion, while the in vitro melanogenic responses occur around 1e-7 M on cells in a dish, and the target - the bulb melanocyte of an anagen terminal follicle - sits roughly 1 mm below the skin surface behind the stratum corneum and the follicular infundibulum. A palmitoylated tetrapeptide is an amphiphile with no published follicular penetration data at any concentration, so the run has been arguing about trial design for an agent that may never reach its receptor. The second is evidentiary: the entire melanogenic dataset is one supplier-authored paper, and the cheapest possible replication is not a nine-month clinical trial but a bench repeat by a laboratory with no commercial interest. This hypothesis merges both into one gating package. Arm A, penetration: excised human scalp skin with terminal follicles mounted in Franz cells, dosed with 0.001%, 0.1%, 1% and 2% peptide in the intended vehicle for 12 h, followed by differential stripping and cyanoacrylate follicular casting to separate surface, infundibular and deep-follicular compartments, with LC-MS/MS quantitation of intact peptide in microdissected bulbs and, critically, an infundibular-contamination control - a surfactant wash step and a parallel set of bulbs microdissected from undosed follicles spiked at the point of dissection to bound carry-over. The pre-specified question is whether any tolerable topical concentration produces a bulb concentration within an order of magnitude of the in vitro active range. Arm B, independent replication and target engagement: microdissected human anagen follicles in organ culture exposed to the same dose series, read out for melanin content of the newly formed shaft, TYR/TYRP1/DCT/MLANA transcript and MC1R protein, ASIP, and Melan-A - i.e. the exact readouts of the supplier paper, repeated in a different laboratory with vehicle controls and blinded assay.
Evidence
PMID 30222197 is the entire evidence base and this hypothesis exists because of what it does and does not contain. It reports catalase induction and a ~30% intracellular H2O2 reduction in Human Follicle Dermal Papilla Cells, activation of melanogenesis in vitro and in ex vivo microdissected human hairs, and a 15-male-volunteer study whose measured endpoint was MC1-R protein on plucked hairs after three months at 10 ppm - a pharmacodynamic biomarker with no placebo. It reports no penetration data, no bulb concentration, and no colour endpoint. The reviewers of both parents landed on the same two points independently: the concentration used in humans is 10 ppm with no demonstrated follicular pharmacokinetics to a target ~1 mm deep, and there is no independent replication of any of it. PMID 41948976 is the reason to take the delivery question seriously rather than assume it away: investigators reversing hair greying by transdermal delivery of active tyrosinase found it necessary to build a penetration model and to optimise nanocapsule size and surface potential specifically because follicular melanocytes are deeply localised and macromolecule delivery is inefficient - and they validated permeation in porcine and human skin models before claiming repigmentation. That is the standard this arm has not met. The organ-culture arm is feasible on established methodology within this corpus: PMID 33598985 demonstrates ex vivo human hair follicle culture used to read melanocyte-lineage responses to H2O2 and noradrenaline, which is the same experimental system proposed here. PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 define the compartment that must be reached (the anagen bulb pigmentary unit), PMID 31316609 and PMID 34700322 define the transcriptional deficit the target-engagement readout should move, and PMID 19237503 supplies the peroxide environment the follicles sit in. Falsification is explicit and cheap: if no tolerable concentration puts intact peptide into the bulb near the in vitro active range, or if an independent laboratory cannot reproduce the melanogenic response in organ-cultured human follicles, the human protocol is cancelled and the MC1R arm dies for a fraction of the cost of finding that out over nine months in 40 people. (refs: PMID 30222197, PMID 41948976, PMID 33598985, PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887, PMID 31316609, PMID 34700322, PMID 19237503)
Safety
No human dosing at all: excised skin and microdissected follicles from consented surgical discard tissue, so the safety profile is an ethics-and-tissue-provenance matter rather than a pharmacological one. The dose escalation to 1-2% is on ex vivo tissue, which is where an irritation or vehicle-tolerance ceiling should be probed before a leave-on human product is specified. The audit found no depigmenting or leukotrichia signal for this molecule or its palmitic-acid-plus-peptide hydrolysis products, so the bench work carries no wrong-direction hazard. The one methodological safety issue is scientific rather than clinical and is designed for: plucked or microdissected bulbs drag infundibular contents, so a lotion at high concentration sitting in the infundibulum can masquerade as intracellular delivery - the same flaw the H066 reviewer identified in the methionine kill test - which is why a surfactant wash, a spike-at-dissection carry-over control and intact-peptide (not fragment) quantitation are mandatory rather than optional.
Novelty rationale
This is not a reworded parent: it deletes the human trial from the proposal entirely and replaces it with the two experiments that determine whether that trial is worth running. Neither parent contains any penetration experiment, any bulb concentration, or any independent replication step - H040 argued mechanism and H050 argued protocol, and both took for granted that a topical lipopeptide reaches a bulb melanocyte 1 mm down. Making the go/no-go depend on a measured bulb concentration converts the strongest recurring criticism of the arm (10 ppm with no follicular PK) from an unanswerable objection into a number. The independent-replication arm attacks the other recurring criticism at its root and at the cheapest possible level: the contra-audit and two reviewers all noted that every melanogenic datum traces to the ingredient house that sells the active, and a blinded organ-culture repeat in a disinterested laboratory is the smallest experiment that can either break that dependence or confirm it. It also imports a methodological lesson from a different arm - the infundibular-contamination control the H066 reviewer showed was missing from the methionine assay - into this one before the same mistake is made twice.
Reviews

No reviews.

9. L-methionine - human topical protocol cancelled and the arm reduced to a single ex vivo human anagen follicle organ-culture test with medium-delivered methionine under peroxide challenge, carrying a mandatory eumelanin/pheomelanin and homocysteine contra-readout and a hard concentration ceiling (H082) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1564 · confidence 2/5 · route: ex vivo (medium-delivered in microdissected human anagen hair follicle organ culture; the topical human protocol is cancelled) · origin: evolution (round 6) · theme: Protocol and design engineering was…

Mechanism
The parent had already conceded the chemistry - sacrificial protection of tyrosinase Met374 by free methionine is a pure concentration-ratio competition for the same oxidant, stoichiometric and without catalytic turnover against a continuously regenerated millimolar peroxide pool - and had restructured itself into a two-week topical loading study asking whether topical delivery can raise bulb free methionine above what plasma already supplies. Its reviewer then dismantled that kill test on three counts that are all correct: 10% w/v lotion under occlusion on the same square from which bulbs are plucked guarantees infundibular carry-over that dwarfs any intracellular signal, so a positive is uninterpretable; free intracellular methionine is transporter-buffered and consumed by protein synthesis, so a null does not exclude delivery either; and whole-bulb free methionine versus plasma is the wrong comparator, since the mechanism turns on the free-Met to protein-Met ratio at Met374 inside the melanosome. A test whose positive is confounded and whose negative is uninformative should not be run. This version therefore deletes the human experiment entirely and moves the question into microdissected human anagen follicles in organ culture, where methionine is supplied in the medium at a defined concentration - which removes penetration, occlusion and infundibular contamination from the experiment in one step and lets the question be asked in its proper form. Design: follicles from the same donor allocated to medium methionine at plasma concentration, 5x and 20x plasma, with and without a controlled H2O2 challenge modelled on the peroxide burden of the grey follicle, read out at 6 days for shaft melanin content, tyrosinase activity, and - the point the parent could never reach - whether excess methionine protects pigment production under oxidative challenge at all. The prediction remains falsifying: if supraphysiological medium methionine does not protect melanogenesis under peroxide challenge in intact human follicles, the sacrificial mechanism is dead irrespective of any formulation, because the delivery barrier has been removed and the drug still did nothing.
Evidence
PMID 19237503 remains the only direct support and its true weight is stated: it reports that under in vitro conditions Met oxidation can be prevented by L-methionine, a single clause in a 2009 paper with no concentration reported, i.e. a cuvette result at an unstated ratio. That is thin ground for a nine-month human programme and adequate ground for one organ-culture experiment, which is exactly the re-scoping performed here. The same paper supplies the lesion (millimolar H2O2 in grey shafts, near-absent catalase and MSRA/B, Met-S=O at tyrosinase Met374 limiting enzyme function) and PMID 24033376 the wider oxidative account. PMID 33598985 establishes that the proposed system exists and works: ex vivo human hair follicle organ culture used with H2O2 and noradrenaline to read melanocyte-lineage outcomes. PMID 32549695 is retained as context only, since it measures serum MDA/GSH/SOD - systemic redox, not follicular, and not this molecule. PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 place the readout in the anagen bulb pigmentary unit. The contra-evidence is the other half of the re-scoping and is represented in the design rather than in a caveat. The audit graded L-methionine 'possible', not clean: the molecule itself has no depigmenting or tyrosinase-inhibiting activity and is not sold or patented as a whitening agent, and the only direct data run the intended way - but its two principal metabolic fates do not. Methionine is the direct precursor of homocysteine, which inhibits tyrosinase by chelating active-site copper and kills melanocytes through ROS and the PERK-eIF2alpha-CHOP pathway, and raised homocysteine is repeatedly reported in vitiligo and, in this corpus, alongside low B12 and folate in premature canities (PMID 28761260); and transsulfuration to cysteine diverts dopaquinone to cysteinyl-DOPA, suppressing eumelanin in favour of pheomelanin, with thiols reviewed as clinical skin-lightening agents. Those primary sources are external to this corpus and are not listed as source_refs. Because the parent's regimen was deliberate high local flooding - 5-10% w/v under occlusion, precisely the condition that would drive local transsulfuration - the contra becomes a design requirement: medium concentration is capped at 20x plasma, and every run measures the eumelanin/pheomelanin ratio of the newly formed shaft by HPLC (PTCA and 4-AHP) plus medium homocysteine. A rise in pheomelanin fraction or homocysteine at any concentration is a stopping result even if total melanin is unchanged, because a shift from eumelanin to pheomelanin lightens hair while looking like preserved pigment on a crude melanin assay. (refs: PMID 19237503, PMID 24033376, PMID 33598985, PMID 32549695, PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887, PMID 28761260)
Safety
No human exposure: the arm is now bench-only on consented surgical discard tissue, which is itself the safety improvement, because the parent proposed occluding 10% w/v methionine on human scalp overnight for two weeks on the strength of one unquantified cuvette sentence. The dose ceiling is a scientific safety measure too - flooding at arbitrary concentration is the regimen most likely to generate the depigmenting metabolites, so open-ended escalation is prohibited rather than left to judgement. The pheomelanin and homocysteine readouts are mandatory and pre-declared as stopping criteria, not exploratory, which is the only way a 'possible' wrong-direction risk gets converted into data instead of a footnote. If the organ-culture test is positive and the contra-readouts are clean, the next step is a further ex vivo dose-response and a formulation study - explicitly NOT the cancelled human occlusion protocol, which would have to be redesigned from scratch with the infundibular-contamination controls its reviewer specified.
Novelty rationale
The material change is that the arm shrinks again and, this time, leaves human subjects altogether - and it does so for a reason the parent could not have given, namely that its own kill test was shown to be uninterpretable in both directions. Supplying methionine in culture medium is not a formulation tweak; it deletes the penetration question, the occlusion, and the infundibular carry-over that made the parent's plucked-bulb assay unreadable, and it replaces the wrong comparator (whole-bulb free Met versus plasma) with the question the mechanism actually makes: does excess methionine protect melanogenesis in an intact human follicle under peroxide challenge. That is a different experiment with a different failure mode, not a reworded one. Second, the contra-evidence is built into the readout rather than acknowledged: no prior version of this arm measured anything downstream of methionine, and the eumelanin/pheomelanin ratio plus medium homocysteine convert the audit's metabolite concern into a pre-declared stopping rule, with a concentration ceiling that specifically forbids the flooding regimen that would generate those metabolites. Third, the novelty claim is corrected downward, as its reviewer demanded: L-methionine for canities on MSRA/Met374 logic is already an explicit published suggestion in the premature-greying literature, so nothing here is claimed as a new repurposing idea - the contribution is a decisive, cheap experiment that either revives a well-worn proposal or ends it.
Reviews

No reviews.

10. vitamin B12 (cobalamin) (H003) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1564 · confidence 4/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
Vitamin B12 deficiency is the best-documented reversible nutritional association with premature canities. B12 is required for DNA synthesis in rapidly dividing cells, including the transit-amplifying melanocyte progeny generated from melanocyte stem cells each anagen; deficiency (or pernicious anemia) impairs this regeneration and is reversibly associated with early greying. Correction restores normal melanocyte lineage turnover and, in reported cases, repigmentation.
Evidence
The dossier's clinical literature consistently associates low B12 with premature greying, and case literature describes repigmentation after deficiency correction. PMID 36853923 provides the mechanistic frame: greying reflects failure of melanocyte stem cell regeneration, which B12 repletion supports. Blind supplementation without confirmed deficiency is unproven. (refs: PMID 36853923)
Safety
Extremely safe even at high oral doses; water-soluble, no known toxicity ceiling at supplement doses (1000 mcg/day). Consumer access: OTC tablets/sublingual cobalamin anywhere; best practice is a blood test first (B12, and pernicious-anemia workup if low), then daily oral supplementation or periodic injection via a GP if malabsorption is found. Repigmentation, when it occurs, appears over months.
Novelty rationale
Not novel as an association, but it is the highest-certainty actionable entry in the universe; included because deficiency-correction is the only intervention tier with documented human repigmentation, and it is frequently missed as a first-line workup step rather than treated as a 'repurposing' candidate.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 4/10, safety 10/10, novelty 2/10 — verdict revise. This is not a repurposing hypothesis — it is standard diagnostic workup (check B12, treat pernicious anemia) repackaged as a candidate, and the hypothesis's own novelty rationale concedes this. Worse, the evidence_summary overreaches its citation: PMID 36853923 (verified in corpus) is a narrative review of MelSC biology and says nothing about B12 supporting MelSC regeneration; the mechanistic sentence linking B12 repletion to 'melanocyte stem cell regeneration' is an inference presented as literature support. The B12-canities association and repigmentation-after-correction case reports are real but association/case-report grade, and the dossier itself states blind supplementation is unproven. Keep only as an explicitly conditional, deficiency-confirmed workup step, not as a scored intervention candidate.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 8 alopecia trial(s), max phase 4, statuses: COMPLETED, RECRUITING, UNKNOWN
  • OpenTargets: error — no chembl_id available
  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

11. a baseline laboratory screen (B12, ferritin, TSH with anti-TPO) repositioned as a mandatory eligibility and stratification step for every topical arm in this run, with selenium deleted and copper demoted from intervention to a zinc-intake screening question (H067) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1561 · confidence 3/5 · route: oral (test-gated repletion, functioning as a pre-enrolment screen rather than a scored intervention) · origin: evolution (round 5)

Mechanism
All three parents were told the same thing by three different reviewers: this is standard dermatological workup for premature canities dressed up as a repurposing candidate, and it should be retained as a screening step attached to the other arms rather than scored as an intervention expected to move pigment. This evolution stops resisting that and rebuilds the tier around the role it can actually fill. In a nine-month vehicle-controlled topical trial, an untreated B12 deficiency, an untreated iron deficiency or an unrecognised thyroid disorder is not a competing therapy - it is an uncontrolled covariate that can move the pigment endpoint, and correcting or excluding it before randomisation is how the topical arms become interpretable. So the deliverable is an entry criterion and a stratification variable, not a treatment claim: screen, exclude or correct and re-screen before enrolment, and stratify randomisation on whether a correctable abnormality was found. Two components are cut on the evidence rather than retained for completeness. Selenium is deleted outright - the H028 reviewer noted it is absent from the association layer entirely, rides on glutathione-peroxidase biochemistry alone, and that selenosis itself causes hair loss, making it the first thing to cut. Copper is demoted from a scored arm to a single screening question about chronic high-dose zinc supplementation, which is the common real cause of acquired copper deficiency; it is no longer proposed as therapy, because the premise it depended on - that bulb tyrosinase is cofactor-limited in ordinary canities - is not supported by anything in this corpus.
Evidence
The reason copper is demoted is stated as evidence, not as deference. PMID 11092760 is a Menkes-fibroblast overexpression system with wholly non-functional ATP7A; it shows that a transporter-null cell cannot activate recombinant tyrosinase, which says nothing about whether copper supply is rate-limiting across the physiological range in an otherwise normal follicle. The competing evidence points the other way at three independent points: PMID 19237503 locates the defect in oxidative damage to the assembled holoenzyme (Met374 sulfoxidation limiting kinetics), PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 describe the lesion as declining melanogenic output, defective melanosome transfer to matrix keratinocytes and melanocyte apoptosis rather than an apo-enzyme state, and PMID 41948976 achieved repigmentation by transdermally delivering active tyrosinase holoenzyme itself in mouse, porcine and human skin models - i.e. what was missing was enzyme activity, not the metal. The apo-enzyme phenotype the copper hypothesis predicts has never been demonstrated in canities. The human copper data are also too weak to gate on: in PMID 21979243 cases averaged 90.7 versus 105.3 microgram/dL with SDs of 37 and 50, both means inside the normal reference range at P = 0.048, and that same study found serum iron significantly higher in cases while PMID 30783336 found it lower - opposite directions in two studies of the same question, which reads as cohort or analytical artefact, and PMID 30783336 itself correlates severity with iron and calcium but not with copper and calls the mechanism unknown. What survives is used only as eligibility rationale, at association grade: PMID 28584374 found B12 deficiency and hypothyroidism significantly associated with premature canities in 71 retrospective cases while ferritin and anaemia were not; PMID 36386090 found low ferritin and raised anti-TPO significantly associated in 40 cases and gives the vertex-predominant distribution used for siting test squares; PMID 28761260 found lower B12 and folate in premature canities. The mis-citations both parent reviewers caught are not repeated: PMID 36853923 is a melanocyte-stem-cell review that says nothing about any micronutrient and is not cited for repletion, and PMID 33320376 is not cited for tyrosine's substrate role. Honest ceiling, stated plainly: no interventional trial shows repletion returns pigment, most premature-canities patients have normal labs so the modal result is a null screen, and the value claimed here is trial validity, not therapy. (refs: PMID 28584374, PMID 36386090, PMID 28761260, PMID 21979243, PMID 30783336, PMID 11092760, PMID 19237503, PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887, PMID 41948976)
Safety
Nothing is dosed blind and, more importantly, less is dosed at all. Selenium is removed, so selenosis - a real toxicity that itself causes hair loss and nail dystrophy - is off the table rather than bounded. Copper supplementation is removed, which eliminates the run's most genuinely dangerous supplement: a narrow therapeutic window, competition with zinc, and hepatic injury in chronic excess, all in service of a mechanism the corpus does not support. B12 is effectively unlimited in safety margin at oral supplement doses. Iron is given only for a confirmed low ferritin, at 325 mg ferrous sulfate every other day with vitamin C, rechecked at three months, and not given empirically. Any thyroid abnormality routes to a physician and is an exclusion from the cosmetic protocol until treated, not a self-treatment item. Access is a routine GP or private pathology panel plus ordinary OTC supplements. Anyone whose screen is normal - the majority - takes nothing.
Novelty rationale
The material change is a change of role plus two deletions, which is the salvage all three reviewers specified and which none of the parents accepted. H003 and H028 both insisted on being scored as interventions while conceding they were workup; here the tier stops competing with the topical arms and becomes their eligibility and stratification instrument, which is a different object with a different success criterion - trial interpretability rather than repigmentation. H053's copper arm is not revised, it is retired as therapy, and the reason is spelled out with the evidence that defeats it: a transporter-null overexpression model is not a model of marginal dietary copper, the two human case-control series are within-reference-range and internally contradictory on iron, and the corpus's own repigmentation result came from delivering active tyrosinase holoenzyme rather than its cofactor. Copper survives only as the zinc-intake question that actually identifies acquired deficiency. Selenium is cut on its reviewer's explicit advice. Both mis-citations the reviewers caught are removed rather than reworded, and the association-layer claims that had been asserted from the dossier are now anchored to the specific human studies that report them. The result is smaller, cheaper, safer and makes every other hypothesis in the run more interpretable, which is the only claim it now makes.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 6/10, safety 8/10, novelty 2/10 — verdict revise. The most intellectually honest item in the batch and the one that stops pretending to be a drug - but that is also the objection. As written it is no longer a hypothesis; it is a protocol appendix, and it should be moved into the trial design for H064/H066/H068 rather than occupying a hypothesis slot and consuming review effort. Judged on its own terms it holds up well: the copper demotion is argued from evidence rather than deference and the argument is correct on the primary sources. I verified PMID 11092760 is a Menkes fibroblast/ATP7A-null overexpression system, which indeed says nothing about marginal dietary copper in a normal follicle; PMID 21979243 does report copper 90.7 +/- 37.4 vs 105.3 +/- 50.2 ug/dL at P = 0.048 with both means inside the reference range, AND reports serum iron significantly HIGHER in cases (108.3 vs 88.8, P = 0.008), while PMID 30783336 reports iron reduced and negatively correlated with severity - so the stated internal contradiction is real and correctly characterised. PMID 41948976 does deliver active tyrosinase holoenzyme transdermally and achieve repigmentation, which is a fair rebuttal to a cofactor-limitation model. The selenium deletion is right. Weaknesses: (1) The residual iron limb is the one place the reasoning goes soft. Having just cited a study showing iron HIGHER in cases, the protocol still repletes low ferritin with 325 mg ferrous sulfate alternate-day - defensible clinically, but the hypothesis should state that this limb is being run for general health and trial hygiene, not on any pigment rationale, because its own evidence points both ways. (2) 'Stratify randomisation on whether a correctable abnormality was found' is not implementable in a within-subject paired-square design, which is what every topical arm in this run uses - side is randomised within a subject, so a subject-level covariate cannot be a stratification factor for it. It can be a covariate or an exclusion, not a stratifier. That sentence needs rewriting or the claim of improved interpretability is cosmetic. (3) Novelty is honestly conceded as near zero, and I agree: this is textbook workup for premature canities.
    contra-evidence (possible): Searches run outside the bundle: (a) 'iron overload supplementation oxidative stress hair greying; excess iron ferritin premature canities harm' - excess free iron generates free radicals via Fenton chemistry and high ferritin is described as increasing oxidative stress at the follicle, which is precisely the millimolar-H2O2 lesion of PMID 19237503 that the rest of this run is trying to reduce. So empirical iron dosing has a mechanistically coherent path to making greying worse; the hypothesis's test-gated, recheck-at-3-months design largely defuses this, which is why I score it 'possible' rather than 'documented'. (b) In-corpus opposite-direction check: PMID 21979243 found serum iron significantly HIGHER in premature-canities cases than controls - a direct in-corpus datum pointing against iron repletion as beneficial, which the hypothesis to its credit reports rather than hides. (c) 'B12 supplementation hypopigmentation / depigmentation' - the documented direction is the reverse (B12 deficiency associated with greying and with reversible hyperpigmentation; repletion is not reported to lighten hair); no whitening product is built on B12, ferritin or TSH testing. (d) Commercial-use check: the components here are a pathology panel and generic OTC supplements; there is no cosmetic depigmentation product built on any of them. (e) Thyroid limb: correction of hypothyroidism is not reported to cause greying. Conclusion: no documented opposite-direction effect for the screen itself; the residual risk sits entirely in the iron limb and is dose-and-indication dependent.

12. baseline laboratory screen (B12, ferritin, TSH with anti-TPO) as a covariate-and-exclusion eligibility gate for the topical arms - selenium and copper permanently deleted, the iron limb explicitly declared to have no pigment rationale, and the B12 evidence restated in both directions (H083) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1559 · confidence 3/5 · route: oral (test-confirmed repletion only), functioning as a pre-enrolment screen and model covariate rather than a scored intervention · origin: evolution (round 6) · theme: Protocol and design engineering was…

Mechanism
The parent had already accepted the correct role for this tier - not an intervention expected to move pigment, but an eligibility and confounder-control instrument that makes the topical arms interpretable - and had deleted selenium and demoted copper to a zinc-intake question. Its reviewer then found one implementable error and one soft limb, and both are fixed here rather than defended. The implementable error: 'stratify randomisation on whether a correctable abnormality was found' cannot be done in a within-subject paired-square design, because side is randomised within a subject and a subject-level attribute cannot stratify a within-subject contrast. It is therefore restated as what it can be - a pre-specified subject-level covariate in the mixed-effects model, and an exclusion criterion for abnormalities that are corrected during the study window, since a mid-trial correction changes both squares simultaneously and would move the endpoint symmetrically rather than differentially. The soft limb: iron. Having cited a corpus study that found serum iron significantly HIGHER in premature-canities cases than controls (PMID 21979243) while another found it lower and negatively correlated with severity (PMID 30783336), the protocol cannot claim a pigment rationale for repleting low ferritin. It is retained as ordinary clinical care and trial hygiene - confirmed low ferritin treated at 325 mg ferrous sulfate on alternate days with vitamin C, rechecked at three months, never given empirically - and is explicitly declared to carry no pigment expectation, with the added reason that free iron drives Fenton chemistry into the very peroxide lesion the topical arms are trying to relieve, so empirical dosing has a coherent route to making greying worse. Copper stays retired as therapy for the reasons the parent gave and selenium stays deleted, now with a second reason from the contra-audit: organoselenium compounds and selenium-enriched ferments are published anti-melanogenic leads and marketed whitening actives, so a selenium arm in a pigment trial carries a wrong-direction class exposure even though nothing shows that repleting a deficient human lightens hair.
Evidence
Everything claimed is association grade and is used only as eligibility rationale. PMID 28584374 found B12 deficiency and hypothyroidism significantly associated with premature canities in 71 retrospective cases while ferritin and anaemia were not, and supplies the temporal (35.2%) and frontal (18.3%) distribution used for siting. PMID 36386090 found low ferritin and raised anti-TPO significantly associated in 40 cases and gives the vertex-predominant distribution (42.5%). PMID 28761260 found lower B12 and folate in premature canities. PMID 21979243 and PMID 30783336 are the internally contradictory trace-element pair described above, and PMID 30783336 correlates severity with iron and calcium but not copper and calls the mechanism unknown. The copper retirement is argued from evidence and is unchanged: PMID 11092760 is a Menkes-fibroblast ATP7A-null overexpression system, which shows a transporter-null cell cannot activate recombinant tyrosinase and says nothing about marginal dietary copper in a normal follicle; PMID 19237503 puts the lesion at oxidative damage to assembled holoenzyme (Met374 sulfoxidation), which copper cannot repair and can worsen via Fenton chemistry; PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 describe declining melanogenic output, defective melanosome transfer and melanocyte apoptosis rather than an apo-enzyme state; and PMID 41948976 achieved repigmentation by delivering active tyrosinase holoenzyme transdermally in mouse, porcine and human skin models - investigators who could have supplied the cofactor supplied the enzyme. NEW in this version, and required by the contra-audit: the B12 evidence is stated in both directions instead of as uniformly pro-pigment. In the skin compartment, B12 repletion is documented to REDUCE pigmentation - hypocobalaminaemia causes reversible cutaneous and nail hyperpigmentation that resolves on cyanocobalamin therapy, and inducing B12 deficiency in human epidermal melanocytes raised melanin content and tyrosinase activity, meaning the replete state is the lower-pigment state at that cell. Those primary reports (Noppakun & Swasdikul 1986; Rzepka et al. Int J Mol Sci 2018) are outside this run's corpus and are not listed as source_refs, but the direction must be on the record. In hair the reported direction is the opposite - the same 1986 case describes white hair alongside skin hyperpigmentation, with both normalising after intramuscular cyanocobalamin - and no report exists of B12 repletion lightening hair, so the screen survives; what does not survive is the parent's implicit assumption that B12's melanocyte effect is uniformly pro-pigment. The honest ceiling is unchanged: no interventional trial shows repletion returns pigment, most premature-canities patients have normal labs so the modal result is a null screen, and the value claimed is trial validity, not therapy. (refs: PMID 28584374, PMID 36386090, PMID 28761260, PMID 21979243, PMID 30783336, PMID 11092760, PMID 19237503, PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887, PMID 41948976)
Safety
Less is dosed than in any ancestor of this tier. Selenium is gone, so selenosis - which itself causes hair loss and nail dystrophy - is off the table, and the anti-melanogenic organoselenium class exposure goes with it. Copper supplementation is gone, eliminating the run's most dangerous supplement: narrow therapeutic window, competition with zinc, hepatic injury in chronic excess, in service of a mechanism this corpus does not support. B12 has an effectively unlimited safety margin at oral supplement doses and no reported poliosis or depigmentation as an adverse effect. Iron is given only for confirmed low ferritin, alternate-day, rechecked at three months, and never empirically - the Fenton argument above is the reason that rule is a safety rule and not merely a stewardship one. Any thyroid abnormality routes to a physician and excludes the subject from the cosmetic protocol until treated and stable, rather than being self-treated. Access is a routine pathology panel plus ordinary OTC supplements; the majority, whose screen is normal, take nothing. One addition: the screen now also records use of oral melatonin and of any oral supplement containing curcuminoids or thiols, as covariates alongside the topical exclusion list, since the round-6 audit established that the retired arms' agents antagonise the surviving one.
Novelty rationale
Novelty is near zero and is conceded outright - this is textbook workup for premature canities - so the only defensible contributions are corrections, and three are made that the parent did not contain. (1) The stratification claim, which its reviewer showed is unimplementable in the within-subject paired-square design every surviving topical arm uses, is replaced by a pre-specified model covariate plus an exclusion for mid-trial corrections; without that fix the parent's central claim of improved interpretability was cosmetic. (2) The iron limb stops borrowing a pigment rationale it cannot support and is redeclared as clinical hygiene, with the Fenton argument stated as the reason empirical dosing is prohibited - a hypothesis that cites iron being higher in cases and then repletes iron for pigment is arguing against itself. (3) The B12 direction is corrected: every prior version treated B12 repletion as unambiguously pro-pigment, and the documented skin-compartment finding is the reverse, so the write-up now carries both directions and rests the hair claim on the hair-specific reports alone. Selenium's deletion gains a second, independent justification from the contra-audit's organoselenium whitening literature, which matters because the parent had deleted it only on weak-association grounds and it could otherwise have been reinstated by a later round. The result is smaller, safer and makes the surviving topical arms interpretable, which is the only claim it makes.

No reviews.

13. topical melatonin + tetrahydrocurcumin leave-on scalp serum, run as a hemi-scalp (split-scalp) controlled protocol with incident-grey-hair counting (H035) Novel candidate androgen

ChEMBL: CHEMBL45 · Elo 1556 · confidence 3/5 · route: topical (leave-on, hemi-scalp controlled) · origin: evolution (round 3) · theme: The redox arm, which led…

Mechanism
The redox arm of the parents is retained but its target is re-specified to fix the quantitative objection that sank pseudocatalase. The claim is no longer that a nightly topical scavenger neutralises the millimolar peroxide pool already present in an established white follicle — that flux is unreachable by any leave-on cosmetic. The claim is narrower and physically defensible: still-pigmented follicles in a greying scalp sit under a lower, chronic oxidative load, and the vulnerable event is the early-anagen window in which bulge melanocyte stem cells (MelSCs) commit to regenerating the pigmentary unit. Lipophilic melatonin partitions into sebum and the infundibulum and quenches radicals locally, while tetrahydrocurcumin (colourless, cosmetic-grade, skin-compatible) supplies Nrf2-mediated induction of the follicle's own catalase/GPx capacity. The endpoint therefore shifts from repigmenting white hairs to reducing the rate at which new hairs emerge unpigmented — a prevention endpoint the pharmacology can actually reach.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 shows H2O2 and noradrenaline induce ectopic bulge pigmentation and differentiation-marker induction in ex vivo human follicles — a surrogate for the earliest greying steps, which is precisely why the reframed endpoint (protecting still-pigmented follicles at anagen onset) is the right one. PMID 36853923 frames MelSC loss as the central driver. Material resolution of H026's review: the reviewer's decisive demand was a control, because a slow, small progression-slowing effect is uninterpretable without one. This version therefore specifies a within-subject hemi-scalp design — serum applied to one side of the part-line only, untreated contralateral zone as internal control, standardised fixed-distance photographs plus trichoscopic counts of pigmented versus unpigmented emerging hairs in matched 1 cm2 zones at baseline, 6 and 12 months. Intervention evidence remains empty for both agents: no greying trial, no follicular concentration data, no demonstration of raised follicular catalase in human skin. That is stated as the evidence ceiling, not hedged around. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Both components are already cosmetic-grade and OTC: topical melatonin scalp lotions (0.0033–0.1%) are sold with negligible systemic absorption, and tetrahydrocurcumin is an established colourless cosmetic antioxidant that avoids curcumin's yellow staining — a non-trivial point on white hair. Worst realistic harm is local irritation or contact dermatitis; patch-test the forearm for 3 days first. No systemic exposure, no drug interactions, no prescriber needed, which is exactly the run's access constraint. Explicit failure rule: if the treated hemi-scalp shows no divergence in incident-unpigmented-hair count from the untreated side at 12 months, stop — do not extend on faith.
Novelty rationale
Materially changes H026 on the two axes its reviewer flagged. First, the uncontrolled 6-month photo readout is replaced by a within-subject hemi-scalp design with trichoscopic counting of emerging pigmented versus unpigmented hairs — the reviewer stated plainly that without this the readout is uninterpretable. Second, and more substantively, the mechanistic claim is retargeted: instead of implying a topical scavenger can clear a millimolar peroxide burden (the pseudocatalase failure mode the reviewer named), the hypothesis now claims only protection of still-functional follicles during the anagen-onset window, converting the promise from repigmentation to measurable incidence reduction. H001's standalone melatonin arm is subsumed rather than repeated, since single-agent scavenging leaves the enzymatic deficit untouched.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 3/10, safety 9/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict revise. The retargeting from repigmentation to incidence-reduction is the right move and honestly argued, but the hypothesis has quietly swapped one unmeasurable claim for another. The weakest link is the endpoint's power, not its logic: incident unpigmented hairs in a matched 1 cm2 zone accumulate at a few percent per year in early canities, so a 12-month hemi-scalp count with hand-placed trichoscopy will be swamped by zone-registration error, seasonal telogen synchrony, and the fact that a hair emerging from a follicle at month 3 was committed before treatment began. No sample size, no per-hair segmentation criterion, no blinded grader is specified — a within-subject control fixes bias, not noise. Second, the hemi-scalp design is contaminated by construction: a leave-on lipophilic serum on a sebum-continuous scalp does not respect a part-line, and melatonin's putative benefit is exactly the kind of low-dose diffusible effect that would cross. A shaved or clipped mid-scalp square with a physical separation and a vehicle-treated (not untreated) control is the minimum; 'untreated contralateral' also leaves the applicator-massage confounder unblinded. Third, the two-agent construction is unearned: neither component has a follicular concentration measurement, and stacking melatonin's scavenging on tetrahydrocurcumin's Nrf2 claim means a null result cannot attribute failure and a positive result cannot attribute success. Nrf2 induction in follicular keratinocytes is inferred entirely from other tissues; the run's own dossier (Wood et al. 2009) locates the catalase deficit in the melanocyte/bulb compartment, which a sebum-partitioning molecule reaches worst. Citations are accurate — PMID 33598985 is genuinely a methods paper on ectopic MelSC pigmentation ex vivo, PMID 36853923 genuinely reviews MelSC loss — but neither touches either agent, so intervention evidence is literally zero and the hypothesis says so. Safety is near-perfect and access is real. Novelty is low: topical melatonin scalp lotions and cosmetic antioxidant serums are already marketed for exactly this; the genuinely new content here is a study design, not a therapeutic idea, and a design is not a hypothesis.

14. lab-guided micronutrient correction protocol (ferritin, selenium, B12, vitamin D, thyroid function; dietary protein/tyrosine sufficiency) (H028) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1553 · confidence 3/5 · route: oral (test-guided repletion only) · origin: evolution (round 2)

Mechanism
Premature greying is associated with a panel of correctable deficits — low ferritin, vitamin B12, vitamin D, thyroid dysfunction, and marginal selenium or protein intake. Rather than blind supplementation with any single nutrient (the flaw common to all three parents), this evolution is a mandatory screening-and-correction protocol: a baseline lab panel (ferritin, B12, vitamin D, TSH, selenium status plus dietary assessment) identifies confirmed deficits, and only those are corrected. Iron repletion for confirmed low ferritin supports the rapid proliferative turnover of melanocyte progeny each anagen; selenium repletion only for confirmed low status restores glutathione-peroxidase activity, the enzymatic peroxide defense that remains when follicular catalase has collapsed; tyrosine sufficiency is addressed only where protein intake is documented as marginal, since tyrosinase substrate is rarely limiting otherwise.
Evidence
The dossier documents the B12/ferritin/vitamin D/thyroid association layer for premature canities; PMID 36853923 supplies the failed-regeneration framework that repletion supports at the proliferative level; PMID 33598985 grounds the peroxide axis relevant to selenium/GPx; PMID 33320376 grounds tyrosine's substrate role. This resolves all three parent critiques exactly as their reviewers demanded: H009 is demoted from a standalone candidate to one item in a mandatory lab-screening bundle alongside B12/vitamin D/thyroid; H023 is reframed strictly as test-and-correct for confirmed low selenium; H019 is reframed as targeted repletion in documented marginal-protein states only. Stated honestly: the associations are dossier-grade with uncertain causality, and no interventional trial shows repletion restores pigment. (refs: PMID 36853923, PMID 33598985, PMID 33320376)
Safety
Nothing is dosed blind — the specific safety fix all three reviewers required. Selenium, whose toxicity (selenosis) itself causes hair loss and nail dystrophy, is bounded to 50–100 mcg/day only after confirming low status, with dietary intake counted and the ~400 mcg/day ceiling respected. Iron is dosed only after a low ferritin result (325 mg every other day with vitamin C), with recheck at ~3 months. Thyroid abnormalities route to a physician, not self-treatment. Consumer access: a GP-ordered panel is cheap and covers most items; repletion uses standard OTC supplements at corrective doses.
Novelty rationale
Converts three weak standalone supplement hypotheses — each individually criticized as an association dressed up as an intervention — into a single defensible screening-and-correction protocol, which is precisely the salvage all three reviewers specified. The novelty is procedural, not pharmacological: a closed-loop test-correct-recheck protocol rather than supplement-aisle guesswork.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 4/10, safety 9/10, novelty 4/10 — verdict revise. PMID check: 36853923, 33598985, and 33320376 all exist, but they are decoration rather than support -- they ground MelSC/melanogenesis biology, and none of them says anything about micronutrient repletion affecting greying. Citing 33320376 (a UV/MC1R/MITF review) for 'tyrosine's substrate role' is a stretch; that claim is general biochemistry, not in the excerpt. The actual support is the dossier's association layer (B12/ferritin/vitamin D/thyroid), which the hypothesis states honestly. Weakest link: this is not a repurposing hypothesis at all -- it is standard-of-care workup for premature canities (any dermatology textbook recommends exactly this panel) dressed as a candidate. Association does not imply reversibility: no interventional trial shows pigment return after repletion, and most premature-canities patients have normal labs, so the protocol's modal outcome is 'nothing found, nothing to correct.' Note also that selenium is not in the dossier's deficiency association list at all; its inclusion rides on GPx biochemistry alone, and selenosis itself causes hair loss -- the test-and-bounded-dose design mitigates this, but the selenium arm should be the first thing cut. Safety design is genuinely the best in this batch (nothing dosed blind, thyroid routed to a physician, selenium ceiling respected). Salvageable only if reframed as a mandatory baseline workup adjunct to every other candidate in the run, not as a standalone intervention expected to move pigment.

15. palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 (Greyverse) (H040) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1548 · confidence 3/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 4) · theme: Direction-of-effect as the run's real…

Mechanism
Palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 (PTP20) is a lipidated biomimetic of alpha-melanocyte-stimulating hormone that agonises MC1R on follicular melanocytes. MC1R is Gs-coupled: agonism raises cAMP, activates PKA/CREB, and induces MITF, the master transcription factor for the melanogenic cassette (TYR, TRP-1/TYRP1, TRP-2/DCT, Melan-A/MLANA). This is the canonical 'push' arm of pigmentation and is exactly the axis that is transcriptionally collapsed in premature canities, where pigmentation-associated genes are globally downregulated in grey follicles (PMID 31316609, PMID 34700322). Uniquely among the candidates here, PTP20 also engages the redox arm of the dossier's core mechanism: in HFDPC and ex vivo micro-dissected human follicles it raised catalase expression and enzymatic activity and cut intracellular H2O2 by ~30% (PMID 30222197), directly opposing the millimolar follicular peroxide accumulation with blunted catalase and methionine-sulfoxide repair that is the best-documented driver of greying (PMID 19237503, PMID 24033376). So a single leave-on active plausibly both stimulates melanin synthesis in surviving bulb melanocytes and lowers the oxidative burden that pushes melanocyte stem cells into ectopic differentiation (PMID 33598985).
Evidence
PMID 30222197 is the strongest human-endpoint evidence in this entire candidate universe: in vitro/ex vivo upregulation of MC1R, catalase, TRP-1, TRP-2, Melan-A and Melan-A/ASIP modulation, SIRT1 activation, ~30% reduction in intracellular H2O2, plus a clinical study in 15 male volunteers with premature canities. PMID 37034550 is a single case report (25-year-old female, Greyverse 2% solution 1 mL twice daily, plus oral biotin/calcium pantothenate) reporting >90% conversion of grey to black hair at 5 months. Both must be graded harshly: the 15-volunteer study is supplier-developed, small, and has no described placebo arm or blinded assessor; the case report is n=1, uncontrolled, confounded by concurrent oral supplements, and reports an effect size (>90% reversal in 5 months) that is biologically implausible against the dossier's constraint that pigment change requires new anagen growth in follicles with viable MelSCs. Supporting mechanism only: MC1R/MITF pigmentation control (PMID 33320376, PMID 25219681); note the discordant finding that MC1R is dispensable for acute stress-induced greying in mice (PMID 33382172), which bounds the mechanism to melanin-synthesis rescue rather than MelSC rescue. (refs: PMID 30222197, PMID 37034550, PMID 31316609, PMID 34700322, PMID 19237503, PMID 24033376, PMID 33598985, PMID 33320376, PMID 25219681, PMID 33382172)
Safety
Cosmetic-grade leave-on scalp solution already on the consumer market, so the OTC/easy-access constraint is fully satisfied. Peptide is large, palmitoylated and applied topically at low percentage; systemic absorption and systemic melanocortin effects (the flushing/nausea/systemic tanning seen with injected alpha-MSH analogues such as afamelanotide) are not expected at these doses and none are reported. Main realistic risks are irritant or allergic contact dermatitis from the vehicle and, theoretically, patchy or off-target hyperpigmentation of scalp skin. Because MC1R agonism drives melanocyte proliferation and melanogenesis, avoid applying over pigmented naevi and exclude anyone with a personal history of melanoma; this is a precaution, not an observed harm.
Novelty rationale
This is deliberately NOT a novelty play, and it must not be scored as one. Palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 is a marketed anti-greying cosmetic active sold explicitly for this indication — proposing it is repurposing nothing. Its value here is entirely as the evidence-anchored comparator the previous three rounds structurally could not see: every other hypothesis in this run (topical melatonin, sulforaphane, prostaglandin analogues, micronutrient repletion, beta-blockade) rests on mechanism plus indirect or animal data, while this one has the only human canities-endpoint data in the universe, however weak. It should be treated as the active control against which more novel candidates must earn their slot, and as the realistic vehicle/base into which novel actives could be added. Honest caveat repeated: the supporting programme is sponsored by the ingredient supplier, small, single-arm, and largely outside peer-reviewed independent replication.
Reviews
  • plausibility 7/10, evidence 5/10, safety 8/10, novelty 2/10 — verdict revise. The mechanism is the one genuinely coherent story in this batch — MC1R is Gs-coupled, cAMP/PKA/CREB drives MITF, and MITF drives TYR/TYRP1/DCT/MLANA — and the dossier's core lesion (global downregulation of pigmentation genes in grey follicles, PMID 31316609; reduced melanogenesis markers, PMID 34700322) is exactly a transcriptional deficit. But the central evidence claim is overstated and I checked it. PMID 30222197's human component is 15 male volunteers in whom the measured endpoint after 3 months of a 10 ppm lotion was MC1R PROTEIN EXPRESSION on plucked hairs — not hair colour, not white-hair density, not a graying severity score. This is a pharmacodynamic biomarker study, not a canities-endpoint clinical study, and the hypothesis's claim that it is 'the only human canities-endpoint data in the universe' does not survive reading the abstract. Second, the redox arm is mis-located: catalase induction and the ~30% H2O2 reduction were measured in Human Follicle Dermal Papilla Cells, a fibroblast-lineage population, not in melanocytes or MelSCs. Invoking that as antidote to the millimolar intrafollicular peroxide of PMID 19237503 / PMID 24033376 is an extrapolation across cell type and across three orders of magnitude of concentration. Third, the concentration is 10 ppm (0.001%) — a leave-on lotion at that dose has no demonstrated follicular pharmacokinetics to the bulb melanocyte, which sits ~1 mm deep. Fourth, PMID 37034550 (n=1, >90% grey-to-black in 5 months, on concurrent oral biotin/pantothenate) is not weak evidence, it is anti-evidence: an effect that large in that time is incompatible with the anagen-cycle constraint (PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887) and should raise the prior on assessment or photographic artefact rather than raise confidence in the peptide. Fifth, PMID 33382172 shows MC1R is dispensable for stress-induced greying, correctly bounding this to melanin-synthesis rescue in follicles that still have melanocytes — which is fine, but it means the hypothesis cannot address the MelSC-exhaustion arm of premature canities at all, and the hypothesis quietly borrows PMID 33598985 (ectopic MelSC differentiation) as if it could. Sponsorship is not disqualifying but it is the whole evidence base: supplier-developed, single-arm, no blinded assessor, no independent replication. Safety is the least of the problems — a palmitoylated tetrapeptide at 10 ppm has no credible systemic melanocortin exposure and the afamelanotide analogy does not apply; the naevus/melanoma-history precaution is sensible and cheap. Novelty is effectively zero and the hypothesis says so, which is to its credit. Salvageable as the run's active comparator, not as a lead: rewrite the evidence_summary to state that no controlled repigmentation endpoint exists for this active, and drop the 'strongest human-endpoint evidence' framing entirely.
