  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

16. topical L-methionine (high-concentration aqueous-glycol scalp lotion under short overnight occlusion) as a sacrificial substrate protecting tyrosinase Met374 from sulfoxidation, replacing the Nrf2 arm (H052) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1548 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (leave-on lotion under short overnight occlusion, vehicle-controlled clipped squares) · origin: evolution (round 4) · theme: Protocol and design engineering was…

Mechanism
H038's strategy was to induce the follicle's own antioxidant battery with sulforaphane, and its reviewer identified two problems that could not be argued away: the human target-engagement data sit in epidermal keratinocytes hundreds of microns from the bulb and bulge, and the load-bearing citation for that engagement is outside this run's verified corpus. This evolution keeps the parents' commitment to fixing the enzymatic side of the redox lesion but sharpens a diffuse claim ('raise the Nrf2 battery') into one named molecular event with a corpus-verified in vitro rescue. Wood et al. showed that grey follicles lose both catalase and methionine sulfoxide reductase A and B, so accumulated H2O2 oxidises methionine residues with no repair pathway left - and specifically converts Met374 in the tyrosinase active site to methionine sulfoxide, which limits enzyme function and is proposed as a direct cause of colour loss. Crucially the same work reports that under in vitro conditions Met oxidation can be prevented by L-methionine. The proposal is therefore stoichiometric protection rather than enzyme induction: flood the follicle with free L-methionine so that peroxide-driven sulfoxidation consumes free substrate instead of the tyrosinase active site, in a follicle that has irreversibly lost the reductase needed to undo the damage. This does not require any transcriptional response, any Nrf2 signalling, or any assumption about gene induction in a compartment nobody has sampled.
Evidence
PMID 19237503 is the whole basis and is corpus-verified: millimolar H2O2 in grey shafts by in vivo FT-Raman, almost absent catalase and MSRA/B protein by immunofluorescence and Western blot, functional loss of Met-S=O repair throughout the grey follicle including the shaft, Met374 sulfoxidation limiting tyrosinase kinetics, and prevention of Met oxidation by L-methionine in vitro. That paper's own finding that the deficit spans the entire follicle rather than one compartment partly answers the compartmental objection that sank the sulforaphane arm, because the target is not restricted to the bulge. PMID 33598985 and PMID 36853923 supply the peroxide-driven MelSC lesion and the loss framework. PMID 32549695 adds independent human evidence that redox imbalance tracks greying severity in premature canities (raised MDA, reduced glutathione and SOD, correlated with severity). The evidence ceiling is stated without hedging: the L-methionine rescue is a cuvette result, no one has applied methionine to a human scalp for pigmentation, there is no follicular concentration measurement, and there is no greying endpoint of any grade. The known and probably decisive weakness is delivery - L-methionine is small but hydrophilic, and hydrophilicity is exactly what disqualified the N-acetylcysteine arm earlier in this lineage. Rather than assert penetration, the hypothesis makes it the first thing to test and specifies the mitigation: high concentration (5-10% w/v in a water/propylene-glycol vehicle, which favours the transfollicular route over the lipid corneum), a clipped square, and a 6-8 hour occlusive overnight cap to sustain hydration and gradient. If nothing changes, the interpretation is unambiguous - either the molecule does not reach the bulb or the sacrificial mechanism does not operate in vivo - which is a cleaner failure than the parents could produce. Design is inherited from H035 with its reviewer's corrections: two separated clipped squares, identical vehicle on the control side, randomised sides, and blinded per-hair scoring for proximal pigmented banding at 6 and 9 months. (refs: PMID 19237503, PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923, PMID 32549695)
Safety
L-methionine is an essential dietary amino acid sold OTC as a food-grade supplement powder for a few cents per gram; a topical solution can be made from it or bought as a cosmetic ingredient. Topical exposure carries no systemic risk at these doses - the concerns attached to methionine are about chronic high oral loading and homocysteine, which does not apply to a scalp lotion. Realistic worst case is local irritation from the glycol vehicle or maceration and folliculitis under an occlusive cap, so occlusion is capped at one overnight period per application, the cap is discarded or washed between uses, and use stops on any pustules or persistent erythema. Patch-test three days first. No prescriber, no interactions, fully compatible with the run's OTC constraint. Anyone with active scalp dermatitis should not occlude.
Novelty rationale
Two material changes. First, the active is replaced because the parent's superiority claim rested on a citation the reviewer could not verify: sulforaphane's distinguishing evidence was outside the corpus, its formulation stability was described with an adjective rather than a specification, and its human data live in the wrong compartment. The caffeine remnant that the same reviewer said should be deleted outright is deleted, not demoted. Second, and more substantively, the mechanism is sharpened from an inferential claim about transcriptional induction of an antioxidant battery in an unsampled compartment to a specific chemical protection of one named residue - Met374 of tyrosinase - for which the corpus contains a direct in vitro demonstration that L-methionine prevents the oxidation. Sacrificial methionine as a topical anti-greying strategy does not appear in the marketed cosmetic landscape or elsewhere in this run, and unlike scavenging against a millimolar peroxide pool it does not require out-competing that flux - only protecting one active site within it. The delivery weakness is named as the gating question rather than buried.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 8/10, novelty 6/10 — verdict revise. The citation is honest - PMID 19237503 really does report that 'under in vitro conditions, Met oxidation can be prevented by L-methionine' - but the hypothesis leans on that one clause far harder than it will bear, and its central chemical argument is wrong. It asserts that sacrificial protection 'does not require out-competing that flux - only protecting one active site within it'. That is exactly backwards: free methionine and Met374 compete for the same oxidant through the same slow second-order reaction, so the degree of protection is set entirely by the concentration ratio of free Met to protein Met at the site. It is a competition argument, and the hypothesis's chief claimed advantage over scavenging is therefore illusory. Worse, methionine reacts with H2O2 slowly and stoichiometrically - one molecule consumed per oxidation, no turnover - against a millimolar, continuously regenerated peroxide pool, so protection would require sustained high intracellular millimolar free Met in the bulb melanocyte. Second fatal-if-unanswered problem: the follicle is already perfused with plasma methionine at physiological concentrations and every one of these patients eats methionine daily, yet they grey anyway. The hypothesis needs to explain why endogenous free methionine fails and topical delivery would not - it never attempts this, and no follicular concentration measurement exists (it admits as much). Third, compartment: Met374 sits in tyrosinase within the melanosomal/secretory pathway of the bulb melanocyte. Topical L-methionine must cross the stratum corneum or the follicular infundibulum, reach the bulb, enter the cell (it will be taken up by amino-acid transporters and largely consumed by protein synthesis, not left free as a scavenger), and reach the melanosome lumen. Citing 'the deficit spans the whole follicle' does not answer this - the lesion being everywhere does not make the drug everywhere. Fourth, delivery is conceded to be the probable killer and is the same hydrophilicity objection that sank the NAC arm; a 5-10% aqueous/glycol lotion under an 6-8 h occlusive cap is a reasonable mitigation but is asserted, not modelled. Fifth, PMID 32549695 measures serum MDA/GSH/SOD - systemic redox, not follicular - so it supports the general redox story and not this molecule. The one thing I credit: the failure mode is genuinely interpretable, the safety profile is near-perfect, and the idea is not in the marketed landscape. Revise only if the proposal adds a penetration/quantitation step as a gating pre-experiment (measure Met in plucked bulbs or in an ex vivo follicle model before running a 9-month human protocol) and drops the 'no need to out-compete the flux' claim, which is not defensible.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 0 alopecia trial(s), max phase 0, statuses: n/a
  • OpenTargets: error — no chembl_id available
  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

17. timolol 0.1% compounded scalp lotion used intermittently and only during defined acute high-stress episodes, on a small concealed test zone, after cardiopulmonary screening (event-triggered, not chronic, beta-blockade) (H039) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL499 · Elo 1548 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (intermittent, event-triggered leave-on lotion on a small test zone) · origin: evolution (round 3) · theme: The stress-axis arm is inverted…

Mechanism
The sympathetic axis remains the most original mechanism in the run: noradrenaline released at the follicle drives melanocyte stem cells out of the bulge into ectopic differentiation, and beta-blockade would interrupt that signal. What the reviewer rejected was not the mechanism but the exposure schedule — chronically beta-blocking the entire scalp of a healthy young person for a cosmetic endpoint on an unproven human premise. That specific inversion is fixed by matching the dosing to the biology: the ex vivo and animal data describe an acute, surge-driven insult, not a tonic one, so continuous blockade is pharmacologically unnecessary as well as unsafe. Use is therefore event-triggered — short courses of a few days to a few weeks bracketing an identifiable acute stressor (bereavement, surgery, an acute illness, an exam or deadline period) — applied to a small concealed zone rather than the whole scalp, cutting cumulative systemic dose by one to two orders of magnitude relative to daily whole-scalp use.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 is now cited with the accuracy its reviewer demanded: it is a methods paper demonstrating that noradrenaline induces ectopic bulge pigmentation and differentiation-marker induction in ex vivo human follicles as a surrogate assay for early greying steps — it does not demonstrate MelSC depletion ex vivo, and the parent's wording overstated this. PMID 36853923 supports MelSC loss as the central driver. The dossier's Nature 2020 mouse data establish the sympathetic chain in animals; the human contribution rests on one small retrospective mapping study and is stated plainly as unproven. Because the premise is weak, the correct response is to shrink the exposure until the risk matches the evidence rather than to build a chronic regimen on it — and to state that a human demonstration that the stress axis matters in canities remains the true gating experiment. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
The risk/benefit inversion the reviewer identified is addressed by exposure reduction rather than reassurance. Absolute exclusions before any use: asthma or any bronchospastic disease, bradycardia, AV block or conduction disease, decompensated heart failure, and concurrent rate-limiting calcium-channel blockers; screening is by prescriber, not self-assessment. Dose caps: 0.1% strength, a small concealed test zone only (never whole-scalp), and a hard cumulative ceiling of a few weeks per stress episode with washout between courses — this is deliberately not a daily maintenance product. Because ophthalmic timolol is meaningfully systemically absorbed, users are told to check resting pulse during each course and stop on symptomatic bradycardia, wheeze, or exercise intolerance. The paradoxical beta-blocker alopecia signal is handled by stopping on any increase in shedding, not by monitoring alone. Prescription-only via a cheap generic plus routine compounding; anyone unwilling to be screened should not use it. Honest bottom line: this remains the weakest risk/benefit case in the run and is offered as a bounded, falsifiable probe of the stress axis, not as a consumer recommendation.
Novelty rationale
The material change is the dosing schedule and exposed area, which is what turned the reviewer's verdict to reject: chronic whole-scalp blockade of healthy people becomes short, event-triggered courses on a concealed zone with a cumulative-exposure ceiling, washout periods, and pulse self-monitoring — a schedule that also matches the acute, surge-driven biology the source data actually describe, so it is a pharmacological correction and not merely a safety concession. Concentration drops to the bottom of the parent's range, the PMID 33598985 attribution is corrected from claimed MelSC 'depletion' to differentiation-marker induction in a surrogate assay, and the hypothesis now names the human proof-of-premise study as the real gate before any further formulation work. Event-triggered follicular beta-blockade timed to acute stressors has not been proposed for canities.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 3/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict reject. This is the most intellectually honest write-up in the batch and it still should not proceed. The fatal problem is that the event-triggered schedule is not a pharmacological correction, it is a rationalisation: the Nature 2020 mouse work describes MelSC depletion as effectively irreversible once triggered, so a blocker started when the user notices a stressor is started after the surge that matters, and stressors like bereavement or acute illness are by definition not pre-scheduled. Only the exam-deadline case is anticipatable, and that is the weakest stressor in the list. Second, the receptor pharmacology does not line up: the murine mechanism is noradrenergic drive on MelSCs reported through beta-2 adrenergic signalling, while timolol is a non-selective beta-blocker whose systemic liability is precisely beta-2 blockade in the airway — so the version that engages the target is the version that causes the bronchospasm the exclusion list is built around. Choosing timolol because it happens to exist as an off-label topical is availability-driven, not target-driven. Third, the risk mitigation is arithmetic that does not survive contact with the use case: reducing area and duration cuts cumulative dose, but the excluded conditions (undiagnosed asthma, occult conduction disease) are exactly the ones a young cosmetic user has not been screened for, and the protocol offloads that to a prescriber who has no indication to write for. Fourth, the endpoint is unmeasurable at the stated scale — a small concealed zone treated for a few weeks, read against a greying process with a multi-year timescale, cannot produce an interpretable signal even if the mechanism is real, so the exposure is incurred for no obtainable information. Credit where due: the PMID 33598985 attribution is now correct (differentiation-marker induction in a surrogate assay, not MelSC depletion), the human premise is named as unproven, and the hypothesis identifies its own gating experiment. That gating experiment — a human study showing the stress axis operates in canities — is what should be pursued; dosing beta-blockers on healthy scalps ahead of it inverts the order. Highest novelty in the batch, worst risk/benefit, and it says so itself.

18. topical rhynchophylline (Uncaria rhynchophylla alkaloid) scalp lotion as a beta-2-pathway-selective, non-cardioactive replacement for compounded timolol (H054) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1547 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (leave-on lotion on a small clipped test zone) · origin: evolution (round 4) · theme: The stress-axis arm is inverted…

Mechanism
The parent's reviewer rejected it on two grounds that are both properties of the drug rather than the schedule. First, the murine mechanism runs through beta-2 adrenergic receptors on melanocyte stem cells, while timolol is non-selective - so the receptor that carries the benefit is the same one whose blockade causes bronchospasm, meaning target engagement and systemic hazard are inseparable. Second, timolol was chosen because it happened to exist as a topical, which is availability-driven rather than target-driven. This evolution keeps the mechanism, which is still the most original in the run - acute sympathetic burst release of noradrenaline drives quiescent MelSCs to proliferate, differentiate, migrate and deplete permanently - and swaps in a molecule found by screening specifically for beta-2 pathway selectivity. Rhynchophylline was identified by computer-aided screening for stable binding at the beta-2 adrenergic receptor active site, inhibited noradrenaline-beta-2 signalling in cells, and cut stress-induced greying in a mouse model from 28.5% to 8.2%, with transcriptomics implicating disrupted intracellular calcium balance and apoptosis downstream of noradrenaline. Applied topically to a small scalp zone at cosmetic concentrations, the cardiopulmonary exposure that made the parent unacceptable does not arise, because the compound is not a systemically active beta-blocker at the doses involved and is not being used to blockade the sinus node or the airway.
Evidence
PMID 31969699 is the corpus-verified foundation for the axis - adrenalectomy, denervation, chemogenetic and melanocyte-stem-cell-specific adrenergic receptor knockout experiments show acute stress depletes MelSCs through sympathetic noradrenaline rather than adrenal hormones, and that transient suppression of MelSC proliferation prevents the greying. PMID 38036925 is the intervention evidence and is stated at its true weight: an in silico plus in vitro plus mouse study, systemic administration, a stress model rather than spontaneous canities, and no human data of any kind. PMID 33598985 shows noradrenaline inducing ectopic bulge pigmentation and differentiation markers in ex vivo human follicles, which is the closest human-tissue link and is described as a surrogate assay rather than as MelSC depletion. PMID 33382172 is cited to keep the mechanism honest and to bound it: MC1R is dispensable for acute stress-induced greying in mice, so the stress arm is genuinely separate from the melanocortin arm and neither substitutes for the other. The parent's reviewer made a further point that is accepted rather than argued with: because MelSC depletion is effectively irreversible once triggered and real-life stressors are not pre-scheduled, an agent used reactively arrives after the surge. This version therefore drops the event-triggered pretence and is framed as continuous low-dose topical prophylaxis in people entering a foreseeably prolonged high-stress period - which is only defensible at all because the safety cost has fallen to that of a cosmetic botanical. The honest position remains that the human premise is unproven and that a human demonstration the stress axis operates in canities is still the gating experiment; the difference is that this candidate can be held while waiting for it, whereas the parent could not. (refs: PMID 31969699, PMID 38036925, PMID 33598985, PMID 33382172)
Safety
The rejection-grade risk profile of the parent is removed rather than mitigated. No asthma or bronchospasm exclusion, no bradycardia, AV block, heart-failure or calcium-channel-blocker interaction list, no pulse self-monitoring, no prescriber screening for occult conduction disease in a healthy young cosmetic user, and no compounding pharmacy - the objection that a prescriber has no indication to write for simply disappears. Uncaria rhynchophylla is available as a herbal preparation and rhynchophylline as a research and cosmetic ingredient; topical use on a small clipped scalp zone at low percentage keeps systemic exposure minimal. The real and stated risks are the ones that attach to any botanical: variable and often unlabelled alkaloid content between suppliers, adulteration, and contact sensitisation, so a characterised single-compound preparation with a stated assay is preferred over crude extract, and a three-day forearm patch test comes first. Oral use is explicitly not proposed - Uncaria alkaloids have central and cardiovascular activity systemically, which is precisely what is being avoided. Not for use in pregnancy given the absence of any human data.
Novelty rationale
The material change is the molecule and therefore the entire risk/benefit sign, which is what earned the parent a reject. The parent tried to make a non-selective cardioactive beta-blocker acceptable by shrinking the dose and area; the reviewer showed that is arithmetic rather than pharmacology, since the beta-2 blockade that engages the target is the beta-2 blockade that causes the harm, and that timolol was picked for availability rather than for the receptor. Here the beta-2 pathway is targeted by a compound selected for exactly that in a published screen, with a mouse anti-greying result on the same axis, and with no cardiopulmonary exclusion list attached. The unworkable event-triggered schedule is also abandoned rather than defended, since MelSC depletion is irreversible once triggered and bereavement and acute illness are not scheduled events. What remains is the run's only stress-axis candidate whose safety profile is compatible with a cosmetic indication - and the first proposal anywhere in this run to attack canities through beta-2 signalling with a non-beta-blocker.
Reviews
  • plausibility 3/10, evidence 3/10, safety 4/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict reject. Reject. The molecule swap fixes the parent's timolol problem on paper and creates three new ones that are each independently disqualifying for a cosmetic indication. (1) The selectivity claim - the entire basis for calling this 'beta-2-pathway-selective, non-cardioactive' - comes from PMID 38036925, which arrived at rhynchophylline by computer-aided screening plus docking/MD, then showed cellular inhibition of NA-beta2AR signalling and a mouse stress-greying reduction from 28.5% to 8.2% under SYSTEMIC administration. In silico binding is not a selectivity dataset. Rhynchophylline is in fact a promiscuous alkaloid with documented calcium-channel, NMDA and cardiovascular/CNS activity - which the safety notes themselves concede when ruling out oral use. You cannot claim a compound is target-selective and in the same paragraph exclude the oral route because its systemic pharmacology is dangerous; that is an admission that the selectivity is dose- and route-dependent, not molecular. (2) Route mismatch: every efficacy datum is systemic dosing in mice. There is no topical PK, no evidence rhynchophylline reaches the bulge at receptor-occupying concentration through human scalp, and no human exposure data of any kind. 'Cosmetic concentrations on a small clipped zone' is asserted safety by arithmetic - precisely the reasoning the parent was rejected for - only now with a botanical alkaloid of unlabelled, supplier-variable content, applied continuously for months. That is a worse-characterised exposure than the prescription drug it replaces, not a better one. (3) The proposal is untestable as framed. Having correctly conceded that MelSC depletion is irreversible and that stressors are unschedulable, it becomes continuous prophylaxis - an endpoint of greying that does not happen. In an individual or a small cosmetic trial that is unmeasurable; it would need a large randomised cohort followed for years against a heritable, slow background rate. There is no proposed design that could return an interpretable result, which is a harder failure than a null. (4) The human premise remains unestablished: PMID 31969699 is mouse (elegant, but mouse), and PMID 33598985 is explicitly a surrogate ex vivo assay of ectopic bulge pigmentation after noradrenaline, not demonstration that sympathetic tone drives human canities. Citing PMID 33382172 to bound the mechanism is good practice and I credit it. The axis is the most original in the run and deserves to stay alive as a mechanism lead, but this candidate is an uncharacterised herbal alkaloid, on an unvalidated route, for a non-event endpoint, in a run with zero tolerance for systemic effects. It should be replaced by a molecule with real topical PK and a measurable endpoint, not revised.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 0 alopecia trial(s), max phase 0, statuses: n/a
  • OpenTargets: error — no chembl_id available
  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

19. mandatory baseline workup and deficiency-correction adjunct (ferritin, B12, vitamin D, TSH/thyroid antibodies) — an eligibility gate for every other candidate, not a standalone intervention (H037) Novel candidate — TOP PICK

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1542 · confidence 3/5 · route: oral (test-confirmed repletion only; positioned as a baseline adjunct, not a treatment) · origin: evolution (round 3) · theme: Direction-of-effect as the run's real…

Mechanism
New or rapidly progressive premature greying can be the visible surface of a correctable systemic problem: iron deficiency, B12 deficiency or pernicious anaemia, vitamin D insufficiency, or thyroid dysfunction. Where such a deficit exists, the mechanistic rationale is proliferative — each anagen requires MelSCs to generate a wave of transit-amplifying melanocyte progeny, and substrate or hormonal insufficiency degrades that regeneration. Where no deficit exists, and this is the modal case, there is nothing to correct and no basis for supplementation. The candidate is therefore reframed exactly as both reviewers instructed: it functions as a screening gate run before and alongside any pharmacological trial, because an uncorrected deficiency or thyroid disorder confounds the readout of every other candidate in this run and would make a null result uninterpretable.
Evidence
The dossier's association layer (B12, ferritin, vitamin D, thyroid, smoking) is the actual support and is cited as association-grade with uncertain causality; repigmentation after deficiency correction is case-report grade. PMID 36853923 is cited only for the failed-MelSC-regeneration framework and is explicitly not claimed to say anything about micronutrient repletion — correcting H003's reviewer-identified overreach, where a MelSC review was used to prop up a B12 mechanism it never discusses. Two elements of H028 are deliberately deleted rather than defended: the selenium arm, which its reviewer said should be the first thing cut because it appears nowhere in the dossier's association list and rides on GPx biochemistry alone while selenosis itself causes hair loss; and the tyrosine/protein arm, whose citation of PMID 33320376 for a substrate role was a stretch and whose deficiency is vanishingly rare in the target population. What remains is only the four tests with a documented canities association. (refs: PMID 36853923)
Safety
The safest item in the run and now safer still: nothing is dosed blind, the two riskiest arms are gone, and the highest-value action it triggers is often not a supplement at all but a smoking-cessation conversation and a thyroid referral. Iron only after a documented low ferritin (e.g. 325 mg ferrous sulfate every other day with vitamin C, recheck at 3 months), B12 orally at 1000 mcg/day after a low result with a pernicious-anaemia workup if indicated, vitamin D per standard repletion guidance, thyroid abnormalities to a physician rather than self-treatment. Access: one inexpensive GP-ordered panel; corrective agents are OTC. Realistic expectation stated up front — most people screen normal and should proceed straight to a mechanistic candidate.
Novelty rationale
The material change is a demotion, which is what both reviews demanded and what H028 stopped short of doing: it is no longer offered as an intervention expected to move pigment, but as a mandatory eligibility and confounder-control gate that every other hypothesis in this run must pass through, with any pigment benefit treated as incidental to correcting disease. Two arms (selenium, tyrosine/protein) are deleted outright rather than re-hedged, and the H003 citation overreach linking B12 to MelSC regeneration is retracted in text. Repositioning a workup as a trial-design prerequisite rather than a competitor is a procedural change with real consequences: it removes a weak candidate from the scoring pool while making every remaining candidate's readout interpretable.
Reviews
  • plausibility 7/10, evidence 5/10, safety 9/10, novelty 2/10 — verdict accept. Demoting this from intervention to eligibility gate is the correct call and it is executed with unusual discipline: the selenium and tyrosine arms are deleted rather than re-hedged, and the H003 citation overreach — using a MelSC review to support a B12 mechanism it never discusses — is explicitly retracted, which is exactly the behaviour a reviewer wants to see. PMID 36853923 is now used only for the failed-regeneration framework, which is what it actually supports. The honest statement that most people screen normal and should proceed straight to a mechanistic candidate is the single most useful sentence in the batch. That said, the remaining rationale is weaker than the confident framing implies. The four retained tests are association-grade with substantial reverse-causation and confounding risk — B12 and ferritin deficiency and hypothyroidism are common in the same demographics that report premature greying, and repigmentation on repletion is case-report level at best, essentially all of it in B12/pernicious anaemia. Vitamin D's inclusion is the weakest of the four and rides largely on studies of undifferentiated hair complaints. More importantly for its stated purpose, the confounder-control argument is asserted rather than shown: no evidence is offered that an uncorrected low ferritin actually changes the rate of incident unpigmented hairs over a 12-month window, so mandating the gate for every other candidate imposes cost and delay on the basis of a plausible but untested assumption. The design consequence also cuts the other way — screening out deficient subjects may remove the very people most likely to respond to anything, biasing every downstream candidate toward the null. Novelty is essentially nil; 'check B12, ferritin, TSH in new-onset premature greying' is standard dermatological practice and appears in the run's own dossier, so this earns its place as good trial hygiene, not as a repurposing idea, and it should not compete for ranking against mechanistic candidates. Accept as an adjunct, on the explicit condition that it is never scored or presented as a treatment.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 0 alopecia trial(s), max phase 0, statuses: n/a
  • OpenTargets: error — no chembl_id available
  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

20. topical sulforaphane (stabilised broccoli-sprout glucoraphanin/myrosinase scalp lotion) as the Nrf2 arm, replacing oral curcumin; caffeine tonic retained only as an optional near-zero-expectation vehicle adjunct (H038) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1539 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (leave-on scalp lotion) · origin: evolution (round 3)

Mechanism
The strategic idea from H022 survives — restore the follicle's own peroxide-clearing enzymes (catalase, glutathione peroxidase, and the wider Nrf2 battery) rather than scavenging stoichiometrically against a documented catalase collapse — but the molecule is swapped for one whose exposure problem is solved. Curcumin's oral bioavailability is dismal and its in-vitro Nrf2 data are micromolar and PAINS-shadowed, so tissue-level target engagement was never plausible. Sulforaphane, generated from glucoraphanin by myrosinase, is the best-characterised Nrf2 inducer available OTC, is small and lipophilic enough to penetrate skin, and is applied directly to the target organ, removing the first-pass and distribution problem entirely. The delivery argument the reviewer demanded is thus answered by changing both the compound and the route rather than by asserting that curcumin gets where it needs to go.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 documents the peroxide-driven ectopic MelSC differentiation and depletion this arm targets; the dossier's Wood et al. FASEB J 2009 data document the catalase and methionine-sulfoxide-reductase collapse in grey follicles, which is the enzymatic deficit Nrf2 induction addresses. Crucially, and unlike curcumin, sulforaphane has published human topical pharmacodynamics: broccoli-sprout extract applied to human skin induces Nrf2-target gene products in situ (Talalay and colleagues, human topical skin studies — cited from outside this run's verified PMID corpus and therefore not asserted as corpus-verified here). That converts 'Nrf2 activator in dishes' into 'Nrf2 activator with human skin target-engagement evidence', which was the reviewer's exact objection. What remains unproven is the last step: no data show scalp application raises catalase or GPx inside the bulge, and there are zero greying endpoints for sulforaphane. H007's caffeine cAMP/MC1R claim is dropped as pharmacologically unsupportable at achievable follicular concentrations, per its reviewer; caffeine survives only as an optional, penetration-friendly tonic base with claims downgraded to 'no expected pigment effect'. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Broccoli-sprout extract preparations are food-derived and sold OTC; topical use is cosmetic-grade with local irritation and, rarely, contact sensitisation as the realistic worst case — patch-test first. Isothiocyanates can smell sulfurous and can bleach or irritate at high concentration, so a low-concentration commercial cosmetic preparation is preferred over home-made sprout macerates, which have unpredictable myrosinase activity and microbial load. No systemic exposure at cosmetic topical doses, no anticoagulant interaction, and none of the GI upset or antiplatelet caution that the oral high-dose curcumin parent carried. Access: OTC cosmetic serums and supplement-grade extracts are widely available online.
Novelty rationale
This is a compound substitution, not a rewording: the parent's active is replaced because its exposure problem was fatal, and the replacement was chosen specifically for having human topical skin target-engagement data — the single piece of evidence H022's reviewer said was missing. Route changes oral to topical, which removes bioavailability as the rate-limiting objection. From H007, the shaky cAMP/MC1R melanogenesis claim is deleted rather than defended and caffeine is demoted from candidate to optional vehicle with explicitly downgraded expectations, exactly the salvage its reviewer specified. Nrf2 induction by a topically delivered isothiocyanate has not been proposed for canities and is mechanistically upstream of every scavenger in this run.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 3/10, safety 8/10, novelty 6/10 — verdict revise. Swapping oral curcumin for topical sulforaphane is a genuine improvement — it removes the bioavailability objection that killed the parent, targets upstream enzyme induction rather than stoichiometric scavenging, and the Nrf2 rationale maps onto the Wood et al. catalase/MSRA collapse better than any other redox candidate in the run. But the load-bearing evidence is self-admittedly outside the verified corpus. The entire claim to superiority over the parent is 'sulforaphane has published human topical skin target-engagement data (Talalay)', and that citation is flagged as unverified here — which means the one fact that distinguishes this from H035 cannot be checked, and the hypothesis is honest about it but still leans on it. The two PMIDs that are verified (33598985, 36853923) establish the peroxide/MelSC problem, not the solution; neither mentions sulforaphane, Nrf2, or any topical. So evidence for the intervention is zero, exactly as for the parent. The deeper gap is compartmental. The published human topical work induces NQO1 and related markers in epidermal keratinocytes; the deficit being targeted is in the bulb melanocyte and bulge MelSC, several hundred microns down and behind a different barrier. Demonstrating Nrf2 target-gene induction in epidermis is not evidence of induction in the bulge, and the hypothesis concedes this in one line while its framing treats the objection as answered. Formulation is a second unaddressed problem: sulforaphane is notoriously unstable in aqueous cosmetic vehicles and degrades over weeks, and the glucoraphanin/myrosinase two-component approach needs an activation step with real variability — 'stabilised' is used as an adjective, not a specification, and the hypothesis correctly warns off home macerates without naming a product with characterised sulforaphane content. There is also no dose, no application frequency, no endpoint, and no control design here, which is a conspicuous regression relative to H035's hemi-scalp protocol in the same batch. Safety is good and access is genuinely OTC, satisfying the run constraint. Novelty is moderate and real — topical isothiocyanate Nrf2 induction has not been proposed for canities — but the caffeine remnant should be deleted outright rather than retained as an 'optional vehicle adjunct', since keeping a demoted agent in the candidate reintroduces the attribution problem for no expected benefit.

21. topical melatonin as a single agent, gated behind a two-part bench test (bulb target engagement by LC-MS/MS, plus protection in the noradrenaline/H2O2-challenged ex vivo human follicle assay) before any human pigment trial (H065) Novel candidate androgen

ChEMBL: CHEMBL45 · Elo 1538 · confidence 3/5 · route: topical (leave-on lotion; preceded by plucked-bulb LC-MS/MS target-engagement assay and ex vivo human follicle challenge assay) · origin: evolution (round 5) · theme: The redox arm, which led…

Mechanism
This lineage has accumulated partners it never earned. H013 bolted N-acetylcysteine onto melatonin and its reviewer pointed out that swapping oral for topical NAC relocates the delivery problem rather than solving it - NAC is hydrophilic and nothing shows it reaches the pigmentary unit - and asked why the arm existed at all except to satisfy a merge brief. H035 then replaced NAC with tetrahydrocurcumin, whose Nrf2 catalase induction is inferred entirely from non-follicular tissue, and its reviewer made the same objection in the same words: a two-agent stack with no follicular measurement for either component means a null cannot attribute failure and a positive cannot attribute success. Both partners are deleted here, leaving the one component with real skin data: lipophilic melatonin, which partitions into sebum and the follicular infundibulum and is already sold as an OTC scalp lotion. But single-agent melatonin unmodified is just H001, so the material change is that the hypothesis stops asserting the step every reviewer in this lineage has flagged - that the drug reaches the target at an active concentration - and makes it the experiment. Stage 0a: nightly 0.1% melatonin lotion to one clipped square for four weeks, vehicle to a separated square, then plucked anagen bulbs from each are assayed for melatonin by LC-MS/MS. If bulb melatonin does not rise measurably above the vehicle side, the entire topical-antioxidant branch of this run is falsified for this vehicle and should be abandoned rather than reformulated on faith. Stage 0b runs in parallel on the bench and is where the retired stress axis is recycled: the ex vivo human follicle assay that quantifies ectopic bulge pigmentation after hydrogen peroxide or noradrenaline is a published, quantitative, human-tissue readout, and melatonin is added to the culture medium to ask directly whether it blunts either insult. Only if bulb exposure is demonstrable and ex vivo protection is seen does the human 9-month vehicle-controlled banding trial run at all.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 is doing new work here rather than being cited as background: it is a methods paper describing a quantifiable ex vivo human hair follicle assay in which hydrogen peroxide, ionising radiation and noradrenaline each induce ectopic pigmentation of bulge melanocyte stem cells with induction of differentiated melanocytic markers, explicitly offered by its authors as a surrogate model for early human greying. That is precisely a screening assay, and using it as a go/no-go gate is the change. PMID 19237503 defines the insult being tested (millimolar H2O2 in grey shafts, near-absent catalase and MSRA/B, Met374 sulfoxidation). PMID 31969699 supplies the noradrenaline arm's rationale - sympathetic hyperactivation depletes MelSCs through adrenergic signalling, shown by adrenalectomy, denervation and melanocyte-stem-cell-specific receptor knockout - and is stated at its true weight as mouse work. PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 impose the honest scope limit that the parents lacked: because human greying begins as declining melanogenesis in the anagen bulb and only becomes irreversible once bulge stem cells are depleted, an antioxidant acting at the bulge protects against the late, irreversible transition and should never have been sold as a repigmentation agent - a prevention claim, tested in people who still have pigment to lose. PMID 36853923 frames MelSC loss. Intervention evidence for melatonin in greying remains exactly zero and is stated as such; that is why the gates exist. On the stress axis, this hypothesis records why rhynchophylline (PMID 38036925) is not carried forward: its selectivity rests on in silico docking, every efficacy datum is systemic dosing in mice, there is no topical PK and no human exposure of any kind, and its own proponents excluded the oral route because the alkaloid is systemically active - so the axis survives here only as a bench challenge condition applied to an agent that is already an OTC topical, not as a reason to put an uncharacterised botanical alkaloid on a scalp for months. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 19237503, PMID 31969699, PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887, PMID 36853923, PMID 38036925)
Safety
Topical melatonin scalp lotions (0.0033-0.1%) are sold OTC with negligible systemic absorption and no meaningful adverse-effect signal; worst realistic harm is local irritation or contact dermatitis, patch-tested on the forearm for three days first. Stage 0b involves no human dosing at all - it is donor follicle organ culture - so the entire riskiest question (does the mechanism operate in human tissue) is answered before anyone applies anything for nine months. Plucking 15-20 anagen bulbs per square for Stage 0a is a trivial, transient discomfort and is the only invasive step. The deleted arms remove risks rather than manage them: no NAC odour and irritancy, no curcuminoid staining question on white hair, and explicitly no beta-adrenergic or alkaloid exposure. No prescriber, no interactions, fully OTC. Stop rules are hard and pre-declared: no bulb-level melatonin above vehicle at four weeks, or no attenuation of ectopic bulge pigmentation ex vivo, and the human trial does not run.
Novelty rationale
The material change is subtraction plus falsifiability, in a lineage that has so far only added agents. Both bolt-on partners are deleted - NAC because its reviewer showed topical delivery relocates rather than solves its hydrophilicity problem, tetrahydrocurcumin because its catalase claim is inferred from tissue nobody sampled - which resolves the attribution objection raised against H035 and H013 by making the regimen single-agent. The genuinely new content is that the unmeasured premise every reviewer in this lineage identified as the weakest link, that topical melatonin reaches the pigmentary unit at an active concentration, is converted from an assertion into a cheap four-week plucked-bulb assay that can kill the whole branch before a nine-month protocol is funded; H001's accept-verdict review named exactly this gap and no descendant has addressed it until now. Second, the published ex vivo human follicle model is repurposed from a citation into a decision instrument, which also salvages something usable from the rejected stress axis: the noradrenaline challenge is retained as a bench condition while rhynchophylline, an uncharacterised herbal alkaloid with no topical PK and a non-event endpoint, is dropped outright rather than revised. Third, the therapeutic claim is downgraded to what the human biology supports - delaying the bulge-depletion step that makes greying irreversible - rather than repigmentation, which no leave-on scavenger against a millimolar peroxide pool can deliver.
Reviews
  • plausibility 3/10, evidence 2/10, safety 7/10, novelty 2/10 — verdict reject. This is the tetrahydrocurcumin error repeating with a different molecule, and the hypothesis's own structure hides it. Melatonin is not a neutral antioxidant with respect to pigment: it is the archetypal vertebrate skin-LIGHTENING hormone, named for exactly that action, and it inhibits melanogenesis and tyrosinase in mammalian systems including hair follicles (see contra_evidence). Every gate in this proposal is built to test delivery and to test protection from oxidative/adrenergic insult; not one gate tests whether melatonin suppresses pigment. Worse, Stage 0b is actively confounded in the direction that would produce a false go-decision. The PMID 33598985 readout is ectopic PIGMENTATION of bulge MelSCs and induction of differentiated melanocytic markers. A drug that inhibits melanogenesis and MITF-driven differentiation will reduce that readout whether or not it protects a single stem cell, so 'attenuation of ectopic bulge pigmentation' is exactly the measurement melatonin can pass for the wrong reason. The gate is not a decision instrument, it is a trap. Secondary problems: the subtraction of NAC and tetrahydrocurcumin is correct and well argued, but what remains is H001 with two assays bolted on, and the novelty claim - 'converts an assertion into an experiment' - is procedure, not science. The 0.1% Stage 0a dose is 30x the top of the OTC range the safety notes themselves cite (0.0033-0.1% is quoted as the sold range and 0.1% sits at its ceiling), which is fine, but plucked-bulb LC-MS/MS after a leave-on lotion has the same infundibular-contamination problem as H066 and no wash-out or tracer control is specified. The rhynchophylline exclusion reasoning (PMID 38036925: in silico selectivity, systemic mouse dosing, no topical PK) is sound and correctly cited. Finally, note that the run's own corpus contains essentially no melatonin literature at all - one incidental mention in 542 records - which means this hypothesis was never grounded in retrieved evidence and its central pharmacological liability was invisible to anyone working from the bundle.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run outside the bundle, and they returned a documented opposite-direction effect. (a) 'melatonin inhibits melanogenesis tyrosinase skin whitening depigmenting agent' - melatonin and its metabolites inhibit melanogenesis by reducing tyrosinase activity and inhibit melanocyte proliferation via MT1/MT2 membrane receptors (Sevilla et al., J Pineal Res 2022, 'Revisiting the role of melatonin in human melanocyte physiology'); melatonin inhibits senescence-associated melanin pigmentation through the p53-TYR pathway in human primary melanocytes and in C57BL/6J mouse skin after UVB (PMC10163137); melatonin is discussed as a candidate depigmenting/anti-hyperpigmentation agent. The same literature notes melatonin was 'first discovered through its potent skin-lightening effects in amphibians'. (b) 'melatonin implants mink fox coat colour winter whitening depigmentation hair' - the mammalian hair-follicle evidence is worse for the hypothesis than the skin evidence: melatonin implantation induced development of WHITE winter hair in normal brown weasels, i.e. an implanted melatonin dose changed pelage from pigmented to unpigmented; melatonin inhibits in vitro melanogenesis in Siberian hamster hair follicles; melatonin implants are a 40-year commercial veterinary product used in mink, fox and other fur species specifically to drive the seasonal pelage programme. So the commercial use of melatonin in hair biology is coat-cycle manipulation with documented depigmenting outcomes, not repigmentation. (c) 'melatonin effects on mammalian melanocytes and melanoma' (Mubashshir et al., Exp Dermatol 2023) - same direction. (d) Corpus grep for 'melatonin' across all 542 records: exactly one hit, an unrelated vitiligo review (PMID 28575807); there is no in-corpus melatonin pigment evidence in either direction. Nothing I found documents melatonin restoring hair pigment in any species. This is opposite-direction evidence in a mammalian hair follicle, from a marketed product, and it must sink the arm.

22. copper repletion strictly gated on a low measured serum copper, as the one deficiency correction with a named enzymatic mechanism in melanogenesis (with zinc status co-monitored) (H053) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL55643 · Elo 1532 · confidence 2/5 · route: oral (deficiency-gated) · origin: evolution (round 4)

Mechanism
The parent was a generic nutritional candidate whose reviewer's decisive objection was that it is diagnostic workup dressed as a repurposing hypothesis, and that its mechanistic sentence linking B12 repletion to melanocyte stem cell regeneration was an inference presented as literature support. This evolution keeps only the part of the nutritional tier that has a specific, named enzymology rather than a general association. Tyrosinase is the rate-limiting enzyme of melanin synthesis and is a copper metalloenzyme: it is loaded with copper inside the secretory pathway by the ATP7A (Menkes) transporter, and tyrosinase expressed in Menkes fibroblasts lacking functional ATP7A is catalytically inactive until the transporter is restored, with activation abolished again by chelating medium copper. That is a mechanistic chain from systemic copper availability to pigment output that no other micronutrient in this universe can draw, and it predicts a specific phenotype - pigment loss with intact melanocytes and intact tyrosinase protein, i.e. an apo-enzyme problem rather than a cell-loss problem. Repletion is therefore proposed as cofactor restoration, not as generic nourishment, and only where the cofactor is measurably short.
Evidence
PMID 11092760 supplies the enzymology: ATP7A-dependent copper delivery is required for tyrosinase activity and melanogenesis in a cell system. Two independent human case-control series find lower serum copper in premature canities - PMID 21979243 reports significantly lower serum copper in 66 patients versus 66 controls (90.7 versus 105.3 microgram/dL, P = 0.048) with no zinc difference and, notably, iron running in the opposite direction to expectation, while PMID 30783336 reports reduced serum iron, copper and calcium in 60 patients versus 30 controls with severity correlating to iron and calcium. Neither is interventional and both are association grade; PMID 30783336 explicitly calls the mechanism unknown. PMID 33320376 places tyrosinase within the MITF-controlled melanogenic program. The other micronutrient associations are retained only as screening, not as candidates, and are cited so the panel is evidence-anchored rather than asserted: B12 and folate and biotin lower in premature canities (PMID 28761260), B12 deficiency and hypothyroidism significantly associated in 71 retrospective cases while ferritin and anaemia were not (PMID 28584374), and low ferritin plus raised anti-TPO significantly associated in a 40-case study (PMID 36386090). The citation overreach the parent's reviewer caught is corrected: PMID 36853923 is a MelSC review that says nothing about any micronutrient and is not used to support repletion here. Honest ceiling: no trial shows copper repletion repigments hair, the associations may be reverse-causal or confounded by diet and socioeconomics, and this arm can only help the subset who are actually deficient. (refs: PMID 11092760, PMID 21979243, PMID 30783336, PMID 33320376, PMID 28761260, PMID 28584374, PMID 36386090)
Safety
This is the one place in the run where blind supplementation is genuinely dangerous rather than merely unproven, and the hypothesis is built around that. Copper has a narrow therapeutic window, competes with zinc for absorption, and chronic excess causes GI toxicity and hepatic injury; high-dose zinc supplementation is itself a common cause of acquired copper deficiency, so zinc intake is reviewed at the same time. Hard gate: serum copper (with caeruloplasmin) measured first, supplementation only if genuinely low, at conservative supplement doses (roughly 1-2 mg elemental copper daily, at or near the RDA rather than megadose), with a repeat level at 8-12 weeks and cessation once normalised. Anyone with liver disease or Wilson disease is excluded outright. Access is straightforward - a GP or private pathology panel plus an OTC copper supplement - but the test is not optional, and taking copper without a measured deficiency should be treated as a harm, not as a neutral experiment. Any repigmentation would take months of new anagen growth.
Novelty rationale
Materially different from the parent on the axis its reviewer attacked. The generic B12-and-friends panel is demoted to a screening step, and the scored intervention narrows to the single micronutrient with a named enzymatic mechanism in the pigment pathway - copper as the tyrosinase cofactor loaded by ATP7A - rather than an association in search of a mechanism. The parent's unsupported mechanistic sentence and its mis-citation of a MelSC review are removed and replaced with the actual corpus-verified copper-and-canities case-control data and the transporter enzymology. It also inverts the safety framing: where the parent leaned on B12's effectively unlimited safety margin, this version treats a mandatory blood test as a hard eligibility gate because the candidate is genuinely toxic in excess, and states the zinc interaction explicitly. It is deliberately scoped as the deficiency tier's strongest single entry rather than as a general supplement recommendation.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 4/10, safety 7/10, novelty 2/10 — verdict revise. The citations are accurately represented - including the creditable concession that PMID 30783336 correlates severity with iron and calcium but NOT with copper, and that it calls the mechanism unknown - yet the hypothesis is defeated by its own numbers and by the premise it never tests. (1) Is bulb tyrosinase cofactor-limited at all? PMID 11092760 does not show that. It is a Menkes-fibroblast overexpression system with a wholly non-functional ATP7A, showing recombinant tyrosinase is inactive until the transporter is restored and inactivated again by chelating the medium. That is a transporter-null genetic model, not a model of marginal dietary copper in an otherwise normal follicle, and it says nothing about whether copper supply is rate-limiting anywhere on the physiological range. The competing evidence points elsewhere: PMID 19237503 locates the lesion in oxidative damage to the assembled holoenzyme (Met374 sulfoxidation limiting kinetics), and PMID 41948976 gets repigmentation by delivering active tyrosinase enzyme itself - both indicate the constraint is enzyme output/integrity, not cofactor availability. The apo-enzyme phenotype this hypothesis predicts has not been demonstrated in canities. (2) The gate destroys the evidence base. In PMID 21979243 the cases averaged 90.7 vs 105.3 microgram/dL - both squarely inside the normal reference range, P = 0.048 with SDs of 37 and 50, i.e. near-total overlap and a result that would not survive multiplicity correction. That study also found serum iron significantly HIGHER in cases, the opposite direction to PMID 30783336, which is a strong hint of analytical or cohort artefact rather than biology. So the hard gate 'supplement only if genuinely low' would exclude essentially every patient in the two studies used to justify the hypothesis. It cannot both require true deficiency and cite low-normal group means as support. (3) Serum copper is a poor status marker in any case: ~90% is bound to caeruloplasmin, an acute-phase reactant that rises with inflammation, oestrogen and pregnancy and falls otherwise, so a single serum value plus caeruloplasmin will misclassify in both directions. If the gate is load-bearing it needs a defensible one (repeat measures, exclude inflammation, consider erythrocyte SOD or a zinc-intake history), and the far commoner real cause of acquired copper deficiency - chronic high-dose zinc - deserves to be the screening question, not a footnote. (4) Novelty is near zero: 'measure copper, correct it if low, watch the zinc interaction, exclude Wilson' is standard clinical practice and is already in the dossier's deficiency-correction tier. Narrowing the parent's B12-and-friends panel to one micronutrient sharpens the mechanism story but does not make it a repurposing hypothesis. (5) Expected yield is small even if right: it can only help a rare truly deficient subset, no trial shows repletion repigments hair, and any effect needs months of new anagen. Retain as a screening step in the protocol - which is where its real value is - not as a scored intervention arm. Revise at best.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: error — HTTPStatusError: Client error '400 Bad Request' for url 'https://clinicaltrials.gov/api/v2/studies?query.term=copper+repletion+strictly+gated+on+a+low+measured+serum+copper%2C+as+the+one+deficiency+correction+with+a+named+enzymatic+mechanism+in+melanogenesis+%28with+zinc+status+co-monitored%29+AND+alopecia&pageSize=10&fields=NCTId%2CBriefTitle%2COverallStatus%2CPhase' For more information check: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTTP/Status/400
  • OpenTargets: error — AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'get'
  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

23. adenosine (H034) Known hair-related use —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL477 · Elo 1532 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 3)

Mechanism
Adenosine A2A/A2B receptors on skin cells are Gs-coupled, raising intracellular cAMP — the second messenger that drives MITF and melanogenesis downstream of MC1R (the canonical tanning pathway). Receptor-mediated Gs stimulation is a different upstream lever than caffeine's phosphodiesterase inhibition (H007) for reaching the same cAMP-MITF switch. Adenosine also prolongs anagen (its established cosmetic hair effect), giving the follicular pigmentary unit more regeneration cycles before melanocyte stem cell attrition.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 describes the alpha-MSH -> MC1R -> cAMP -> MITF melanogenesis axis that A2-receptor agonism engages. Adenosine's anagen-prolonging effect is documented in the cosmetic hair literature but its melanogenesis application is untested; this is mechanism-level plausibility at the dossier's honest evidence ceiling. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
Best-in-class cosmetic fit: adenosine scalp lotions (0.75%) are sold OTC as anti-hair-loss products in Japan and online internationally, with negligible systemic absorption and no meaningful adverse-effect signal. Consumer access: purchase an adenosine hair lotion online or from Asian-beauty retailers and apply to the scalp daily; any pigment effect would require months of new anagen growth and only in follicles with viable melanocytes.
Novelty rationale
Flagged prior hair-related use: adenosine is an established anti-hair-loss cosmetic active, but its use as a cAMP-driven melanogenesis support for greying is not an established indication and differs from incumbent caffeine (H007) by acting through a defined Gs-coupled receptor rather than PDE inhibition.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 9/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict revise. Mechanistically the most coherent of the four — A2A/A2B Gs-coupled cAMP elevation feeding the MITF melanogenesis switch is biochemically sound and the differentiation from caffeine's PDE inhibition (H007) is real — but the load-bearing premise is unproven: that functional A2 receptors on FOLLICULAR melanocytes/McSCs drive melanogenesis in vivo. Adenosine signaling in skin is documented mainly in keratinocytes and dermal papilla (its proven cosmetic effect is anagen prolongation via growth-factor induction in dermal papilla, i.e., hair GROWTH, not pigmentation), and adenosine receptor agonism has been reported to modulate melanogenesis in both directions in cell models, so the sign of the effect is not established. Cited PMID 33320376 is verified in the corpus and does describe the alpha-MSH -> MC1R -> MITF tanning axis, but MC1R-driven cAMP in melanocytes does not entail that A2 agonism in the follicle reaches the same switch at cosmetic concentrations. The 'more anagen cycles before McSC attrition' argument is speculative — anagen prolongation has no demonstrated effect on McSC exhaustion rate. Safety and access are best-in-class (OTC 0.75% scalp lotions, negligible systemic exposure), which fits the run's OTC constraint perfectly. Salvageable as a low-risk trial, but the hypothesis should cite actual adenosine-receptor/melanogenesis data (or concede none exists) and drop the unevidenced McSC-preservation claim.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 10 alopecia trial(s), max phase 4, statuses: COMPLETED, ENROLLING_BY_INVITATION, RECRUITING, TERMINATED, UNKNOWN, WITHDRAWN
  • OpenTargets: MoA targets ADORA1, ADORA2A, ADORA2B, ADORA3; AGA-associated target overlap: none
  • Novelty: 121 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 0.008)

24. melatonin (H001) Known hair-related use androgen

ChEMBL: CHEMBL45 · Elo 1531 · confidence 3/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 1) · theme: The redox arm, which led…

Mechanism
Topical melatonin is a potent lipophilic antioxidant and radical scavenger that accumulates in skin and hair follicles. Grey follicles accumulate millimolar hydrogen peroxide with low catalase, and ex vivo human hair follicles exposed to H2O2 show ectopic differentiation and depletion of melanocyte stem cells (MelSCs). By lowering follicular ROS burden at the bulge/bulb, melatonin could slow MelSC exhaustion and protect surviving melanocytes, preserving pigment output in subsequent anagen cycles.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 demonstrates that redox stress (hydrogen peroxide) drives ectopic pigmentation and depletion of MelSCs in isolated human hair follicles — the exact lesion melatonin's antioxidant pharmacology targets. PMID 36853923 confirms MelSC loss in the bulge as a main driver of hair graying. Direct anti-greying trials of melatonin do not exist; this is mechanism-level plausibility only. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Excellent cosmetic-indication fit: topical melatonin solutions (0.0033%–0.1%) are already sold OTC as anti-hair-loss lotions (e.g., in the EU) with negligible systemic absorption and no meaningful adverse-effect signal. Oral melatonin (1–3 mg at night) is also OTC in most countries but systemic dosing is unnecessary for a follicular target. Consumer access: buy a topical melatonin scalp lotion online/in pharmacies and apply nightly; any pigment effect needs months of new anagen growth.
Novelty rationale
Melatonin is known in hair research only for androgenetic alopecia growth effects; its use specifically as an anti-greying antioxidant protecting the MelSC niche is not an established indication and is rarely proposed.
Reviews
  • plausibility 7/10, evidence 5/10, safety 9/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict accept. Mechanism is coherent and the cited PMIDs check out: 33598985 really is the ex vivo human follicle study showing redox/H2O2-driven ectopic MelSC differentiation and depletion, and 36853923 is a review supporting MelSC loss as a driver of greying. Weakest link: the evidence is entirely mechanism-level — no study shows melatonin reaches the bulge MelSC niche at effective antioxidant concentrations, and the dossier itself notes antioxidant topicals generally lack direct evidence. Also, millimolar H2O2 accumulation in grey follicles may be a consequence of melanocyte loss rather than the initiating lesion, so melatonin is at best progression-slowing for follicles that still have MelSCs, not a repigmentation agent. That framing is honest in the hypothesis, and the safety/access profile (existing OTC topical scalp lotions) is the best of the set.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 1 alopecia trial(s), max phase 0, statuses: UNKNOWN
  • OpenTargets: MoA targets MTNR1A, MTNR1B; AGA-associated target overlap: none
  • Novelty: 97 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 0.010)

25. topical melatonin + topical N-acetylcysteine (leave-on scalp serum) (H013) Novel candidate androgen

ChEMBL: CHEMBL45 · Elo 1517 · confidence 3/5 · route: topical (leave-on) · origin: evolution (round 1) · theme: The redox arm, which led…

Mechanism
Grey follicles accumulate millimolar hydrogen peroxide with collapsed catalase and methionine-sulfoxide repair, and ex vivo human follicles show this redox burden drives ectopic differentiation and depletion of melanocyte stem cells (MelSCs). This evolution merges two complementary, follicle-deliverable antioxidant mechanisms into one leave-on topical regimen: melatonin, a lipophilic radical scavenger that accumulates in skin and follicles, plus N-acetylcysteine (NAC), a cysteine prodrug that replenishes intracellular glutathione — applied topically so the glutathione precursor reaches the follicular pigmentary unit directly instead of relying on oral bioavailability. Jointly they lower the per-anagen ROS burden at the bulge/bulb, slowing MelSC exhaustion in follicles that still retain stem cells.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 demonstrates that H2O2/redox stress reproduces the greying lesion (ectopic MelSC differentiation and depletion) in isolated human hair follicles — the exact target both agents address; PMID 36853923 frames MelSC loss as the central driver of greying. Material change versus parents: H002's oral systemic dosing (flagged by its reviewer as violating the cosmetic-indication constraint and as an unbridged systemic-to-follicle gap) is replaced by direct topical delivery; H001's reviewer concern that no study shows bulge delivery is narrowed by specifying a leave-on lipophilic serum vehicle. Evidence ceiling is unchanged and stated honestly: mechanism-level plausibility only, no greying trial data exist for either agent. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Both components are cosmetic-grade: topical melatonin lotions (0.0033%–0.1%) are already sold OTC for scalp use with negligible absorption, and topical NAC is used in dermatologic/cosmetic formulations with only local irritation risk — no chronic systemic exposure, resolving the run-constraint violation raised against oral NAC. Consumer access: combine an OTC topical melatonin scalp lotion with a compounded or cosmetic NAC-containing leave-on serum applied nightly to greying areas. Framed explicitly as progression-slowing for follicles with surviving MelSCs, not repigmentation; assess with standardized photos over 6+ months of new anagen growth.
Novelty rationale
Not a rewording of either parent: the delivery route of H002 is materially changed (oral to topical, the specific salvage its reviewer demanded) and two single-mechanism parents are merged into one coherent dual-antioxidant follicle regimen — radical scavenging plus glutathione repletion at the same lesion. No existing consumer product pairs these mechanisms for greying; the anti-greying market's oral catalase products are mechanistically inferior (digested protein).
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 4/10, safety 8/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict revise. The redox target is real and both citations check out: 33598985 verifies as ex vivo human follicle H2O2-driven MelSC ectopic differentiation/depletion, and 36853923 verifies as a MelSC-loss-in-greying review. The weakest link is the one the hypothesis claims to have fixed: delivery. Swapping oral for topical NAC does not bridge the delivery gap, it relocates it — NAC is hydrophilic, and no cited evidence shows it penetrates to the bulge/pigmentary unit or raises follicular glutathione at cosmetic doses; 'leave-on lipophilic serum vehicle' is asserted, not demonstrated. Melatonin is the stronger half (lipophilic, accumulates in skin, some human scalp-tolerability data), which raises the obvious question of why the NAC arm is there at all other than to satisfy the merge brief. Claiming superiority over oral catalase products sets a very low bar. Evidence ceiling is honestly stated but remains mechanism-plausibility only with zero greying intervention data for either agent. To proceed, the formulation claim needs substantiation (penetration model or porcine/follicle assay) or the NAC arm should be dropped in favor of the single defensible component.

26. topical melatonin + topical tetrahydrocurcumin (colorless curcumin derivative; leave-on lipophilic scalp serum) (H026) Novel candidate androgen

ChEMBL: CHEMBL45 · Elo 1517 · confidence 3/5 · route: topical (leave-on) · origin: evolution (round 2) · theme: The redox arm, which led…

Mechanism
Grey follicles accumulate millimolar hydrogen peroxide with collapsed catalase and impaired methionine-sulfoxide repair, and ex vivo human follicles show this redox burden drives ectopic differentiation and depletion of melanocyte stem cells (MelSCs). This evolution pairs two complementary, demonstrably lipophilic antioxidant strategies in one leave-on scalp serum: melatonin, a lipophilic radical scavenger that accumulates in skin and follicles and quenches ROS directly, plus tetrahydrocurcumin, the colorless cosmetic-grade metabolite of curcumin, which retains the parent's Nrf2-activating pharmacology and aims to restore the follicle's own peroxide-clearing enzyme expression (catalase, glutathione peroxidase). Scavenging plus endogenous-enzyme restoration attack the same H2O2 lesion at two levels, and both agents are lipophilic enough that follicular delivery from a leave-on vehicle is plausible rather than asserted.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 demonstrates that H2O2/redox stress reproduces the greying lesion (ectopic MelSC differentiation and depletion) in isolated human hair follicles; PMID 36853923 frames MelSC loss as the central driver of greying. Material changes resolving both parent critiques: (1) H013's reviewer found the hydrophilic NAC arm unsubstantiated for bulge penetration and recommended dropping it in favor of defensible components — it is dropped and replaced by tetrahydrocurcumin, which is lipophilic and skin-compatible; (2) H022's reviewer demanded a topical delivery argument and an honest acknowledgment that Nrf2 activation is a claim about cells in dishes, not follicles in scalps — delivery is now topical, and it is stated plainly that the Nrf2 evidence is in-vitro-grade with no follicular concentration data and no greying trials for either agent. Evidence ceiling unchanged: mechanism-level plausibility only. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Both components are cosmetic-grade: topical melatonin lotions (0.0033%–0.1%) are already sold OTC for scalp use with negligible absorption, and tetrahydrocurcumin is an established cosmetic antioxidant whose key practical advantage over curcumin is that it is colorless — eliminating the yellow-staining problem that would make curcumin itself cosmetically unacceptable on grey/white hair. Only local irritation risk; no chronic systemic exposure. Consumer access: combine an OTC topical melatonin scalp lotion with a cosmetic tetrahydrocurcumin serum, applied nightly to greying areas. Framed as progression-slowing for follicles with surviving MelSCs, not repigmentation; assess with standardized photos over 6+ months of new anagen growth.
Novelty rationale
Not a rewording of either parent: H013's unsubstantiated NAC arm is removed (the specific salvage its reviewer demanded) and H022's oral-to-topical delivery fix is executed, merging the two strongest surviving mechanisms — direct radical scavenging plus Nrf2-driven enzyme restoration — into one lipophilic follicle-deliverable serum. The colorless-derivative choice also solves a cosmetic-acceptability problem neither parent addressed. No existing consumer product pairs these mechanisms for greying.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 3/10, safety 9/10, novelty 6/10 — verdict revise. PMID check: both citations exist and are attributed correctly -- 33598985 explicitly shows hydrogen peroxide (and noradrenaline) inducing ectopic bulge pigmentation and differentiation-marker induction in ex vivo human follicles, and 36853923 is a MelSC-and-greying review. So the lesion is real and human-relevant. Weakest link: the intervention evidence is empty. Neither melatonin nor tetrahydrocurcumin has any greying trial, any follicular concentration data, or any demonstration of raising follicular catalase/GPx in skin; the Nrf2-restoration claim is in-vitro-grade, which the hypothesis admits. There is also a quantitative problem nobody addresses: grey follicles accumulate MILLIMOLAR peroxide with collapsed enzymatic clearance, and a topical scavenger applied nightly is orders of magnitude away from neutralizing that flux -- this is the same gap that sank pseudocatalase (PC-KUS), which the dossier flags as marketed-but-unefficacious. The honest framing ('progression-slowing on follicles with surviving MelSCs') saves it from the pseudocatalase overclaim, but it also means the expected effect size is small, slow, and practically undetectable without a controlled within-scalp comparison, which the protocol does not include. On the plus side: dropping the hydrophilic NAC arm, choosing a colorless curcumin derivative, and going leave-on topical are exactly the fixes the parent reviews demanded, and this is the safest candidate in the batch. Keep, but add a split-scalp or untreated-control-zone design or the 6-month readout will be uninterpretable.

27. timolol (compounded low-strength leave-on scalp lotion) (H029) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL499 · Elo 1515 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (leave-on scalp lotion) · origin: evolution (round 2) · theme: The stress-axis arm is inverted…

Mechanism
The core mechanism is retained — the stress axis of greying runs through sympathetic norepinephrine release at the follicle, which forces melanocyte stem cells into ectopic differentiation and irreversible depletion from the bulge niche, and follicular beta-blockade would blunt this signal during chronic stress — but the delivery and population problems the reviewer identified are fixed. The ophthalmic 0.5% gel-forming drop is replaced by a compounded 0.1–0.25% timolol maleate hydroalcoholic leave-on scalp lotion applied with a part-line applicator, a scalp-appropriate vehicle at a lower per-area dose. The treatable population is sharply defined: adults with stress-correlated greying onset or progression and no asthma, bradycardia, or cardiac-conduction disease, screened before use.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 demonstrates directly that noradrenaline induces ectopic pigmentation and depletion of MelSCs in isolated ex vivo human follicles — the exact lesion this would interrupt; PMID 36853923 frames MelSC loss as the central greying driver; the dossier's Nature 2020 animal data establish the sympathetic-stress-to-greying chain. Each reviewer demand is addressed: a scalp-appropriate vehicle is now specified; a defined low-risk subpopulation is stated; and it is acknowledged frankly that the human stress-greying premise is itself unproven — supported mainly by one small retrospective mapping study — so this candidate treats a stress-reactive axis whose contribution to ordinary premature canities may be minor. No interventional data exist. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Access stated plainly: prescription-only, but timolol is a cheap generic any GP can supply and compounding into a scalp lotion is routine; the topical infant-hemangioma precedent supports skin safety at low doses, not follicular efficacy. Mandatory pre-use screening excludes asthma, bradycardia, and conduction disease. The rare systemic beta-blocker alopecia signal is now handled with explicit monitoring language rather than dismissed: discontinue if shedding increases, and track with monthly standardized photos. Local irritation is the expected worst-case local effect. Framed strictly as slowing stress-axis MelSC depletion over months of new anagen growth.
Novelty rationale
Materially changes H017 on exactly the axes its reviewer flagged: delivery moves from an ophthalmic gel to a purpose-formulated low-strength scalp lotion with a defined applicator and dose; the candidate population is narrowed to screened, stress-correlated cases; and alopecia monitoring plus a frank premise caveat are added. The mechanism itself — sympathetic-axis protection of the MelSC niche — remains unique in this run and orthogonal to every antioxidant, prostaglandin, and repletion incumbent.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 4/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict reject. PMID check: citations exist; the attribution of 33598985 is a mild overstatement -- that paper demonstrates noradrenaline-induced ectopic pigmentation and differentiation-marker induction as a surrogate assay for early greying steps, not demonstrated MelSC 'depletion' ex vivo, and it is explicitly a methods paper. The deeper problem is the premise-to-prescription chain. The stress-NE-MelSC axis is mouse-grade (Nature 2020) plus one ex vivo surrogate; the human contribution of sympathetic surges to ordinary premature canities is unproven, rests on a small retrospective mapping study, and the hypothesis concedes this. So the proposal is: chronically beta-block the scalps of healthy young people, for a cosmetic endpoint, on an unproven premise. That fails the run's own constraint -- a cosmetic indication with zero tolerance for systemic side effects. Topical timolol is systemically absorbed (ophthalmic drops cause bradycardia and bronchospasm; chronic scalp application over a large surface area is not obviously micro-dose), the treatable population excludes asthmatics and cardiac patients only because the risk is real, and there is a paradoxical beta-blocker alopecia signal now handled by 'monitoring' -- i.e., the proposed hair drug can cause hair loss. The delivery fix (compounded 0.1-0.25% scalp lotion) is competent and the mechanism is the most original in the run, but originality cannot carry a risk/benefit ratio this inverted for a cosmetic goal. This would need a human proof that the stress axis matters in canities before any formulation work is justified.
