28. bimatoprost 0.03% applied by fine-tip brush to a single hairline test zone with morning surface wash-off (contact-time-limited prostaglandin melanogenesis stimulation) (H036) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL1200963 · Elo 1512 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (brush-applied, nightly, with morning surface wash-off) · origin: evolution (round 3) · theme: The prostaglandin arm and a…

Mechanism
Prostaglandin F2alpha analogues stimulate melanin synthesis in existing follicular melanocytes through FP-receptor signalling feeding the MITF/tyrosinase program; eyelash darkening is the established human proof that this reaches follicular pigmentary units at microgram doses. The parents' unsolved problem was not efficacy but compartment selectivity: the same drug darkens the epidermis it sits on, and blotchy perifollicular staining along a visible part-line is a worse cosmetic outcome than the greying being treated — the reviewer's point that local hyperpigmentation here is the drug working as intended in the wrong tissue. This evolution attacks that directly with contact-time control. A fine-tip eyeliner-style brush lays a minimal volume along a single 2 cm hairline or temple zone at night; the follicular infundibulum forms a reservoir within minutes to hours, while the stratum corneum surface is washed with a mild cleanser each morning to truncate epidermal exposure. Follicular depot is retained, epidermal dose is curtailed.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 supplies the MITF/tyrosinase melanogenesis framework the FP-receptor signal feeds; PMID 36853923 supplies the constraint that greying is primarily a melanocyte and MelSC loss phenotype, which is why the addressable population is narrowed rather than broadened. Both parent reviews converged on the same fix and it is executed here: H005's reviewer demanded the target population be sharply restricted to early salt-and-pepper greying with surviving bulb melanocytes, and that access be described honestly instead of as 'semi-OTC'; H027's reviewer added that the melatonin arm was antioxidant inference stacked on antioxidant inference and that scalp staining was underweighted. The melatonin arm is therefore dropped from this candidate — it belongs in the redox candidate, not bolted on here to inflate plausibility — leaving one agent with one testable mechanism. Honest ceiling: lash-to-scalp extrapolation is unproven, no scalp pigmentation trial exists, and most treated follicles will be non-responders by definition. (refs: PMID 33320376, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Systemic exposure at microgram topical scale is negligible; the real liability is local and cosmetic. Confining application to a single hairline test zone means any staining is small, hidden under hair, and reversible on discontinuation rather than spread across the part-line. Mandatory stop rules: discontinue on any visible skin darkening, perifollicular blotching, persistent erythema, or periocular contact. Keep the product well away from the eyes — periorbital fat atrophy and iris pigmentation are documented ocular-route effects and are the reason a drip-prone dropper is rejected in favour of a brush. Access stated without spin: bimatoprost is prescription-only in major markets, costly at scalp scale, and this is frank off-label use requiring a prescriber; consumers unwilling to involve a doctor should not attempt it.
Novelty rationale
This is not H027 reworded: the melatonin arm is deliberately removed so the prostaglandin mechanism is tested cleanly rather than confounded, and the unresolved staining liability is met with a concrete formulation-free engineering fix — minimal-volume brush application plus morning surface wash-off to exploit the kinetic difference between the follicular reservoir and the epidermal surface. Relative to H005 it adds the population restriction its reviewer demanded, drops the misleading 'semi-OTC' access claim, and shrinks the exposed area from the whole scalp to one concealable test zone, inverting the risk/benefit for a cosmetic indication. Contact-time-limited topical prostaglandin dosing has not been proposed for canities.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 4/10, safety 5/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict revise. Dropping the melatonin arm and restricting the population are real improvements, but the central engineering claim is asserted without a number and is probably wrong. Morning wash-off assumes a kinetic window in which the infundibular reservoir is loaded while the stratum corneum is not — yet the corneum is the reservoir the follicle loads through, and after 6-8 hours of overnight contact epidermal partitioning is complete and largely irreversible to surface cleansing. Washing at hour 8 removes residual surface drug, not what has already partitioned; the follicular-versus-epidermal selectivity ratio is never estimated, cited, or measured. That is the whole novelty of the hypothesis and it rests on an unsupported assumption. Compounding this, the hairline and temple are the single worst site choice for a staining liability: they are the most photo-exposed, most visible, and least concealable margin on the scalp, which contradicts the stated rationale that any staining will be hidden under hair. A mid-occipital or retroauricular zone would test the same mechanism with genuine concealment. Mechanistically, FP-receptor melanogenesis stimulation is well grounded for lash darkening, but the hypothesis concedes the population problem and then does not resolve it: PMID 36853923 correctly establishes that greying is MelSC and melanocyte loss, meaning white hairs have no melanocyte to stimulate, so bimatoprost can only act on follicles that are still pigmented or contain surviving amelanotic bulb melanocytes — a population that is not identifiable by inspection at enrolment, making responder and non-responder indistinguishable from failure. PMID 33320376 is cited accurately for the MITF/tyrosinase framework but is a general skin-pigmentation review with nothing on scalp follicles or prostaglandins. Access is now described honestly (prescription-only, off-label, expensive at scalp scale), which counts in its favour but also means it fails the run's OTC constraint outright. Safety is middling rather than good: negligible systemic exposure, but periocular proximity at the hairline plus documented periorbital fat atrophy and iris pigmentation is a real, disfiguring, and partly irreversible tail risk that a brush does not eliminate. Salvageable — relocate the test zone, quantify or drop the wash-off claim, and specify a trichoscopic criterion for surviving pigmentation at enrolment.

  • OpenTargets: MoA targets PTGFR; AGA-associated target overlap: none
  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

29. tretinoin (H031) Known hair-related use —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL38 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 3)

Mechanism
Tretinoin (all-trans retinoic acid) signals through RAR/RXR nuclear receptors that modulate melanocyte differentiation, tyrosinase expression, and keratinocyte-melanocyte crosstalk. The clinical trigger for this hypothesis is the case-report-level observation of grey-hair repigmentation during systemic retinoid therapy (etretinate/acitretin class) — an anecdote-grade but real signal in the retinoid class. Topical delivery concentrates RAR agonism at the follicular pigmentary unit while avoiding the teratogenic systemic exposure that disqualifies oral retinoids for a cosmetic indication.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 supplies the melanocyte/MITF pigmentation-control framework retinoid signaling intersects. Retinoid-repigmentation case reports are not in the provided excerpts and are flagged as anecdote-grade, consistent with the dossier's evidence ceiling: no intervention is RCT-proven to reverse grey hair, and this is a plausible-mechanism, low-risk trial candidate only. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
Generic tretinoin cream (0.025-0.05%) is inexpensive and among the most-prescribed topicals in dermatology (acne/photoaging), so a consumer can obtain it from any GP, dermatologist, or telehealth service; weaker retinol cosmetics are fully OTC but less reliable. Apply a pea-sized amount to greying scalp/hairline zones at night; expect initial dryness and peeling — titrate frequency. Strictly avoid in pregnancy. Local irritation is the ceiling of expected harm; negligible systemic absorption at scalp doses.
Novelty rationale
Tretinoin is an established skin drug but has no established hair-greying use and is not proposed anywhere in the current leaderboard; the repigmentation signal comes from a different (systemic) retinoid formulation, and reframing it as a topical, cosmetic-tolerance-compatible candidate is a genuinely new angle rather than a reworded incumbent.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 2/10, safety 6/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict revise. The weakest link is that the hypothesis leans against the grain of tretinoin's established clinical effect: topical retinoids are prescribed to REDUCE epidermal hyperpigmentation (melasma, post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation) via accelerated turnover, so betting on follicular repigmentation from the same pharmacology needs more than hand-waving about RAR/RXR 'modulating' melanocytes — RAR agonism perturbs melanocyte biology in both directions and the sign of the effect in the follicular pigmentary unit is unknown. The only repigmentation signal cited is from SYSTEMIC retinoids (etretinate/acitretin case reports, not in the provided excerpts and unverifiable here); systemic exposure achieving follicular RAR agonism says little about what a 0.025-0.05% scalp cream delivers, especially since scalp skin penetration to the bulge McSC niche is not established. The single cited PMID 33320376 is verified in the corpus but is a generic pigmentation-control review (MITF/MC1R framework) that says nothing about retinoids — it grounds the background, not the claim. Pregnancy contraindication and photosensitivity of a scalp-applied retinoid are real frictions for a cosmetic use case. Salvageable as a low-risk self-experiment, but the proposal must (a) cite the actual repigmentation case reports or drop the claim, and (b) acknowledge the pro-depigmenting clinical precedent and argue why follicular melanogenesis would differ.
Validation

  • OpenTargets: MoA targets RARA, RARB, RARG; AGA-associated target overlap: none

30. timolol (H017) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL499 · Elo 1500 · confidence 3/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 2) · theme: The stress-axis arm is inverted…

Mechanism
Timolol is a non-selective beta-adrenergic antagonist. The stress axis of greying runs through sympathetic norepinephrine release at the follicle, which forces melanocyte stem cells (MelSCs) into ectopic differentiation and irreversible depletion from the bulge niche. Follicular beta-blockade would blunt noradrenergic signaling to MelSCs during periods of chronic or acute stress, protecting the stem-cell pool that regenerates pigment-producing melanocytes each anagen and thereby slowing stress-driven greying.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 demonstrates directly that noradrenaline induces ectopic pigmentation and depletion of MelSCs in isolated ex vivo human hair follicles — the exact lesion topical beta-blockade would interrupt. PMID 36853923 confirms MelSC loss from the bulge as a main driver of hair graying. The dossier's Nature 2020 animal data establish the sympathetic-stress-to-greying causal chain. No interventional data exist; this is sharp mechanism-level plausibility with human ex vivo support. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Topical timolol 0.5% gel-forming solution is an inexpensive generic glaucoma drop with a decades-long safety record; the same formulation is applied to infant skin off-label (hemangiomas) with low systemic absorption — though asthma, bradycardia, and cardiac-conduction patients must avoid even topical beta-blockers. Note: systemic beta-blockers carry rare alopecia reports (a growth, not pigment, effect), but scalp-applied microdoses are orders of magnitude lower. Consumer access: not OTC — timolol eye drops need a prescription, but they are a cheap generic any GP can supply; off-label use would be applying the 0.5% solution to greying scalp/hairline areas once daily. Any pigment effect needs months of new anagen growth.
Novelty rationale
Timolol has no established hair-pigment use and appears in hair literature only marginally for growth. Using follicular beta-blockade to interrupt the norepinephrine–MelSC-depletion axis is mechanistically orthogonal to every incumbent in this run (antioxidants, prostaglandin analogs, nutrient repletion, PUVA) and, to my knowledge, has not been proposed for canities before.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 4/10, safety 6/10, novelty 8/10 — verdict revise. The sharpest mechanism in the set and the citations are clean: PMID 33598985 verifies and its abstract explicitly shows noradrenaline induces ectopic MelSC pigmentation and depletion in ex vivo human follicles — exactly the lesion claimed — and 36853923 verifies for the MelSC-loss driver framing. The weakest link is translation distance, and it is large. The stress-to-greying causal chain is established in mice; in humans, stress-induced greying is supported mainly by one small retrospective mapping study, and nobody has shown that chronic follicular noradrenergic tone contributes to ordinary age-related or premature canities — if human greying is overwhelmingly oxidative/genetic rather than sympathetic, beta-blockade treats a minor axis. Second, delivery is asserted: an ophthalmic 0.5% gel-forming drop is not a scalp formulation, and bulge-level beta-blockade at cosmetic doses is unmeasured; the infant hemangioma precedent supports skin safety, not follicular pharmacology. Third, 'not OTC' strains the run's access constraint, and even topical timolol is contraindicated in asthma/bradycardia/conduction disease — a real screening burden for a cosmetic aim. Fourth, the rare beta-blocker alopecia signal, though growth-not-pigment and at systemic doses, deserves explicit monitoring language rather than dismissal. Genuinely novel and cheap to test, so worth keeping alive — but it needs a scalp-appropriate vehicle, a defined low-risk subpopulation, and a frank statement that the human stress-greying premise is itself unproven.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 0 alopecia trial(s), max phase 0, statuses: n/a
  • OpenTargets: MoA targets ADRB1, ADRB2; AGA-associated target overlap: none
  • Novelty: 4 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 0.200)

31. latanoprost (generic prostaglandin F2alpha analog; bimatoprost cosmetic serum as the alternative access channel) (H014) Known hair-related use —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL1051 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (leave-on, compounded scalp lotion) · origin: evolution (round 1)

Mechanism
Prostaglandin F2alpha analogs stimulate melanin synthesis in follicular melanocytes — an established human effect (eyelash darkening, iris hyperpigmentation) — plausibly via FP-receptor signaling feeding the MITF/tyrosinase melanogenesis program. Merging the two parents into one candidate with the cheaper generic as the lead: a defined scalp formulation of latanoprost (e.g., 0.005%–0.03% in a hydroalcoholic leave-on lotion, 0.5–1 mL applied nightly to greying patches with a dropper or mascara-style applicator along the part line) could raise pigment output per anagen in follicles whose bulb melanocytes survive. Critically, the target population is now sharply defined: only early, salt-and-pepper greying where follicles retain viable melanocytes/MelSCs — follicles with exhausted MelSCs have nothing to stimulate and are explicitly out of scope.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 lays out the MC1R/MITF/tyrosinase melanogenesis framework; the dossier documents lash darkening as an established prostaglandin class effect. Both parent critiques are resolved: H005's target-mismatch flaw (stimulating synthesis in a melanocyte-loss disease) is answered by restricting the indication to early greying with surviving melanocytes; H006's delivery dodge (ophthalmic drops on scalp) is replaced by a concrete compounded scalp-lotion formulation and dosing plan. Evidence ceiling stated plainly: cross-follicle-type extrapolation from lash to scalp is unproven; no grey-hair trial exists for either analog; this remains the only candidate class with demonstrated human repigmentation-adjacent pharmacology. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
Systemic exposure at microgram topical doses is negligible; the real liabilities are local and cosmetic — scalp skin hyperpigmentation and irritation — mitigated by precise applicator use confined to the hair-bearing greying area and washout if skin staining appears. Access framed honestly per the dossier's Rx exception clause: prescription-only in major markets, obtainable as cheap generic latanoprost via a short physician consult or via bimatoprost cosmetic-serum channels; monthly cost for scalp-scale use is material and stated as the main practical barrier. Not for consumers unwilling to see a prescriber.
Novelty rationale
Material changes over both parents: (1) the two redundant PG entries are merged into one, resolving duplication; (2) the addressable population is narrowed to early/salt-and-pepper greying with surviving melanocytes — the exact revision both reviewers demanded; (3) a concrete formulation and dosing plan replaces the vague 'apply eye drops to scalp' access story. Scalp grey-hair repigmentation with PG analogs remains genuinely untested, so the hypothesis keeps its non-obvious character.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 5/10, safety 6/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict accept. The only candidate in this set with demonstrated human melanogenesis-stimulating pharmacology: eyelash darkening and iris hyperpigmentation are established, on-label-adjacent class effects, and PMID 33320376 verifies as a pigmentation-control review covering the MITF framework it is cited for (it is a general skin-pigmentation review, not grey-specific — acceptable as framework grounding, but the hypothesis should not lean on it for scalp relevance). The two genuinely hard problems are acknowledged rather than solved: lash-to-scalp extrapolation is unproven (lash follicles are miniaturized and pharmacologically peculiar), and the melanocyte-survival restriction — while the right answer to the H005 critique — creates a selection problem the hypothesis cannot operationalize: there is no consumer-accessible way to determine which follicles retain viable melanocytes, so real-world use collapses to empirical trial on salt-and-pepper scalps. Residual liabilities: scalp/facial skin hyperpigmentation at application margins is a cosmetic harm in a cosmetic indication, it is prescription-only in major markets (bimatoprost cosmetic-serum channels skirt this at real cost), and months of nightly compounding expense. Given the class is the only one with human repigmentation-adjacent signal, the safety burden is local and reversible, and both parent critiques were substantively addressed, this clears the bar — narrowly — as the run's lead mechanism candidate.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 0 alopecia trial(s), max phase 0, statuses: n/a
  • OpenTargets: MoA targets PTGFR; AGA-associated target overlap: none
  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

32. caffeine (H007) Known hair-related use —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL113 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
Topical caffeine penetrates follicles well and has two relevant actions: phosphodiesterase inhibition raising cAMP (the second messenger downstream of MC1R, the canonical melanogenesis receptor), and direct antioxidant/radical-scavenging activity. Higher follicular cAMP could support MITF-driven melanin synthesis, while ROS scavenging protects melanocyte stem cells from the peroxide-driven ectopic differentiation documented in greying.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 establishes MC1R→cAMP→MITF as the core melanogenesis axis that cAMP elevation would potentiate; PMID 33598985 documents the follicular redox lesion caffeine's antioxidant action addresses. Caffeine's hair literature is limited to growth/AGA endpoints; no greying data exist. (refs: PMID 33320376, PMID 33598985)
Safety
Ideal cosmetic-indication profile: caffeine shampoos/tonics (e.g., Alpecin-type products) are mass-market OTC with negligible systemic absorption and no meaningful safety signal. Consumer access: any caffeine shampoo or scalp tonic used daily per label; leave-on tonics give longer follicle contact time than rinse-off shampoo. Expect, at best, slowed progression over months.
Novelty rationale
Caffeine is marketed for hair loss but essentially never for greying; the cAMP-melanogenesis connection repurposes a known follicle-penetrant toward a new pigment endpoint.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 10/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict revise. The cAMP mechanism is pharmacologically shaky: caffeine is a weak, nonselective PDE inhibitor whose meaningful PDE effects require concentrations far above what a rinse-off shampoo or even a leave-on tonic plausibly sustains in the follicle; claiming MC1R-axis potentiation via caffeine is closer to marketing copy than pharmacology. The antioxidant angle is more defensible and is correctly anchored to PMID 33598985's H2O2/redox-stress MelSC-depletion model, but 'scavenges ROS in vitro' to 'preserves MelSCs in vivo over months' is an unproven leap, and the hypothesis admits zero greying endpoints in the entire caffeine literature. Both cited PMIDs (33320376, 33598985) exist in the corpus and are attributed roughly correctly, which is more than can be said for the effect size. Salvageable only as a near-zero-risk, low-expectation adjunct, not as a mechanistically driven candidate; the claims should be downgraded from 'supports MITF-driven melanin synthesis' to 'antioxidant rationale, pigment effect speculative'.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 7 alopecia trial(s), max phase 2, statuses: COMPLETED, RECRUITING, TERMINATED, UNKNOWN
  • OpenTargets: MoA targets ADORA1, ADORA2A, ADORA2B, ADORA3; AGA-associated target overlap: none
  • Novelty: 62 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 0.016)

33. vitamin D (cholecalciferol) (H008) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL1042 · Elo 1500 · confidence 3/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
Vitamin D deficiency is associated with premature canities, and vitamin D receptor signaling modulates melanocyte biology and differentiation; in vitiligo, correcting vitamin D status is used adjunctively to support repigmentation. Repletion may normalize melanocyte/MelSC function in deficient individuals and remove a contributing driver of early greying.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 discusses melanocyte regulation and notes vitiligo as the depigmentation model where vitamin D adjunct use originates; the dossier documents the vitamin D–premature greying association (causality uncertain). This is a deficiency-correction hypothesis: benefit expected only when baseline status is low. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
OTC worldwide, very safe at 1000–2000 IU/day; toxicity only at sustained mega-doses. Consumer access: standard D3 softgels from any pharmacy; best practice is a 25-OH vitamin D blood test and repletion to sufficiency rather than blind dosing. Any pigment effect requires months of new hair growth.
Novelty rationale
The association is known, but vitamin D is rarely framed as a test-and-correct intervention specifically for canities; positioned here as part of the actionable deficiency layer rather than a generic wellness supplement.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 4/10, safety 9/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict revise. This is an association-plus-deficiency-correction hypothesis, and its own framing concedes benefit only in confirmed-deficient individuals with causality uncertain. The cited support is misattributed: PMID 33320376 is a pigmentation-control review that discusses vitiligo as autoimmune melanocyte loss and UVB-driven repigmentation; it does not establish vitamin D adjunct use in vitiligo, so leaning on it for the vitamin D-repigmentation lineage is a stretch. The vitamin D-premature canities association lives only in the dossier, uncorroborated by any corpus excerpt. Novelty is minimal: test-and-correct vitamin D is standard wellness advice and appears verbatim in the dossier's actionable layer. The honest verdict is that this is reasonable clinical hygiene (cheap, safe, testable) but a weak repurposing hypothesis; it should be repositioned explicitly as a screening recommendation with a negative-control expectation, not as a pigmentation intervention.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 10 alopecia trial(s), max phase 4, statuses: COMPLETED, NOT_YET_RECRUITING, RECRUITING, TERMINATED, UNKNOWN
  • OpenTargets: MoA targets VDR; AGA-associated target overlap: none
  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

34. acetyl hexapeptide-1 (Melitane) (H041) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 4)

Mechanism
Acetyl hexapeptide-1 is a second, structurally distinct alpha-MSH biomimetic MC1R agonist. Its supplier-reported profile differs from palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 in emphasis: rather than a catalase/antioxidant arm, it is reported to increase melanin synthesis and, importantly, melanin TRANSFER from melanocytes to keratinocytes, and to shift the distribution of hair-bulb cells from low-pigmented to high-pigmented states. That distinction matters mechanistically: hair shaft colour is determined not only by how much eumelanin a bulb melanocyte makes but by how efficiently melanosomes are donated into the cortical keratinocytes that become the shaft. A transfer-limited step would be invisible to substrate/cofactor strategies (tyrosine, copper, zinc) and only partly addressed by MITF-driven synthesis alone, so this is a genuinely different node on the same MC1R->cAMP->MITF axis.
Evidence
PMID 31831925 is a case report of a 14-year-old female with premature hair greying treated with topical Melitane 5% plus oral hair supplements, reporting improvement in hair colour — n=1, unblinded, and confounded by the concurrent oral supplements, so anecdote-grade at best. The mechanistic claims (melanin synthesis, melanin transfer, low- to high-pigmented bulb cell shift) come from supplier documentation and are not independently peer-reviewed. General MC1R/MITF biology supporting the axis: PMID 33320376, PMID 25219681. Grey follicles show global downregulation of pigmentation genes (PMID 31316609) and reduced melanogenesis marker expression (PMID 34700322), which is the deficit this peptide is meant to reverse. Counter-evidence to note: MC1R is dispensable for acute stress-induced greying in mice (PMID 33382172), so MC1R agonism should be expected to help follicles that still retain melanocytes, not to regenerate an exhausted MelSC pool. (refs: PMID 31831925, PMID 33320376, PMID 25219681, PMID 31316609, PMID 34700322, PMID 33382172)
Safety
Marketed cosmetic leave-on active, low percentage, peptide — same benign topical profile as other MC1R biomimetics: irritation/contact allergy from the vehicle is the main expected event, systemic melanocortin effects are implausible at cosmetic exposure. Same naevus-avoidance and melanoma-history exclusion precaution as for palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20. Note the published case was a 14-year-old; there is no paediatric safety dataset, so this should be tested in adults only.
Novelty rationale
Also not novel as a product — Melitane is sold as an anti-greying cosmetic active, so proposing it is not a repurposing insight. Its non-obvious content is the melanin-TRANSFER hypothesis: if the rate-limiting lesion in a given greying follicle is melanosome donation to cortical keratinocytes rather than melanin synthesis, then this active and palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 are not interchangeable and should not be collapsed into one 'MC1R peptide' hypothesis. That is a testable, falsifiable distinction (plucked-hair bulb histology / Fontana-Masson for melanosome distribution rather than shaft colour alone), and no existing hypothesis in this run addresses transfer at all. Evidence caveat is stronger than for Greyverse: supplier data plus one confounded case report, no controlled study.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 2/10, safety 7/10, novelty 2/10 — verdict reject. This is dominated by H040 on every axis that matters, and the one thing that would rescue it — the melanin-TRANSFER differentiator — is not mechanistically supported. Melanosome donation to cortical keratinocytes is governed by keratinocyte-side machinery (PAR-2 signalling, filopodial/phagocytic uptake, Rab27a/myosin-Va transport in the donor cell), none of which is a documented downstream of MC1R-cAMP-MITF. So the hypothesis asserts that acetyl hexapeptide-1 acts on a node it never explains a pathway to; the 'low- to high-pigmented bulb cell shift' is a supplier assay description, not a transfer measurement, and the hypothesis's own text concedes it is not peer-reviewed. That is the weakest link: a differentiator built entirely on marketing copy. The clinical evidence is worse than H040's. PMID 31831925 is n=1 in a 14-year-old on concurrent oral hair supplements, with an unblinded subjective outcome ('boosting improvement in hair colour') — that is not gradeable, and the hypothesis is right that paediatric data cannot license adult use, but it stops short of the real conclusion, which is that this citation supports nothing. Everything else in the source list (PMID 33320376, PMID 25219681, PMID 31316609, PMID 34700322) is generic pigmentation biology that supports the MC1R axis in general and therefore supports H040 equally — it does not discriminate between the two peptides, so it cannot be used to justify keeping both. PMID 33382172 is correctly flagged as bounding the mechanism to melanocyte-retaining follicles. Safety is unremarkable and genuinely benign for a cosmetic leave-on peptide, with the same naevus-avoidance precaution. Novelty is nil as a product and the hypothesis admits it. The proposed falsification test (Fontana-Masson on plucked bulbs to separate synthesis-limited from transfer-limited follicles) is the only valuable content here, and it is a methods idea that belongs bolted onto H040 or H044 as a secondary endpoint — it does not justify a separate candidate with a second, less-evidenced, mechanistically vaguer MC1R peptide. Reject as a standalone hypothesis; harvest the histology endpoint.
Validation

No external validation recorded

35. N-acetyl tyrosine + zinc + antioxidant complex (Darkenyl-type) (H042) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL925 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 4)

Mechanism
A substrate-plus-cofactor strategy applied topically rather than systemically. N-acetyl tyrosine is a more stable, better-penetrating derivative of the obligate tyrosinase substrate, delivered directly into the follicular infundibulum instead of relying on plasma tyrosine reaching the bulb. Zinc is a structural cofactor at the binuclear metal site of tyrosinase-related protein 1 (TYRP1), the enzyme that oxidises DHICA and stabilises the eumelanin pathway — the human TYRP1 crystal structure shows a binuclear ZINC (not copper) active site important for melanogenesis (PMID 28661582), which is a concrete, under-appreciated argument for zinc rather than copper as the topical metal here. The antioxidant component targets the millimolar follicular H2O2 that both inactivates enzymes of the melanogenic cascade and oxidises methionine residues that repair systems can no longer restore (PMID 19237503). The combined logic: supply the precursor, supply the metal that the DHICA-oxidase step actually uses, and lower the peroxide that is degrading both.
Evidence
Direct efficacy evidence is supplier-generated only, with no independent or peer-reviewed trial in canities — this must be stated plainly. Supporting mechanism: TYRP1 binuclear zinc active site required for melanogenesis (PMID 28661582); tyrosinase activation is metal-transporter dependent (Menkes copper transporter data, PMID 11092760) showing that metal loading is a real rate-limiting step in vivo; trace-element associations in premature greying, including zinc and copper, are reported but inconsistent across studies (PMID 30783336, PMID 21979243); grey follicles show global pigmentation-gene downregulation and reduced melanogenesis markers (PMID 31316609, PMID 34700322); oxidative burden as the enzymatic-damage mechanism (PMID 19237503, PMID 24033376, PMID 20805969). A topical proteoglycan-based anti-greying formulation with combined antioxidant and pro-melanogenic in vitro effects (PMID 41613336) shows the same 'combined substrate/redox topical' design is being pursued but likewise has no clinical data. (refs: PMID 28661582, PMID 11092760, PMID 30783336, PMID 21979243, PMID 31316609, PMID 34700322, PMID 19237503, PMID 24033376, PMID 20805969, PMID 41613336)
Safety
All components are cosmetic-grade leave-on ingredients at low percentage. Topical zinc on the scalp is very well tolerated (compare pyrithione zinc shampoos). Importantly, this route avoids the two real hazards of the oral analogues already proposed in this run: oral copper supplementation has a narrow therapeutic window, is toxic in excess and antagonises zinc absorption, and oral tyrosine loading is unproven. Local delivery keeps exposure at the follicle. Expected adverse events are limited to irritation, vehicle contact dermatitis, and possible transient scalp staining/discolouration depending on antioxidant choice.
Novelty rationale
The active is a marketed anti-greying cosmetic complex, so as a product it is not novel and should not be credited as a repurposing idea. What is non-obvious, and what differentiates this from the existing oral L-tyrosine and cupric sulfate hypotheses in the current leader set, is the argument for LOCAL delivery and for ZINC rather than copper as the metal: the DHICA-oxidase step catalysed by TYRP1 uses a binuclear zinc site, whereas the copper rationale applies to tyrosinase proper. Nobody in the run has separated those two metal dependencies. This also functions as the mechanistically weakest-but-cheapest arm of a factorial test: if signal (MC1R agonist) is present but substrate/cofactor is limiting, the peptide alone will underperform the combination.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 8/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict revise. The zinc argument is the weakest link and it is a category error. PMID 28661582 does show a binuclear ZINC site in human TYRP1 — but the paper's point is that this is precisely why TYRP1 lacks tyrosinase redox activity, and those metals are loaded onto the nascent enzyme in the secretory pathway during biosynthesis, then buried in the folded active site. They are not an exchangeable pool that topical scalp zinc replenishes. There is no evidence that follicular zinc availability limits TYRP1 metalation in vivo, and the human DHICA-oxidase activity of TYRP1 is itself contested (it is well established in mouse, less so in human). So 'zinc rather than copper as the topical metal' is a clever-sounding inversion built on a structural fact that does not license the pharmacological conclusion. PMID 11092760 (Menkes transporter required for tyrosinase activation) actually argues the opposite of the hypothesis's own framing: it shows metalation is a regulated intracellular transporter-dependent step, i.e. flooding the outside of the cell with metal is not the lever. The substrate arm is weaker still. Tyrosine is not rate-limiting for melanogenesis; tyrosinase expression and activity are, and PMID 31316609 shows grey follicles have globally downregulated the entire pigmentation transcriptional programme. Supplying precursor to follicles that have stopped transcribing the enzymes is pouring flour into a kitchen with no oven — the hypothesis's own companion H044 predicts this arm will do 'essentially nothing', which is a striking admission from within the same batch. N-acetyl tyrosine's penetration advantage is asserted, not cited, and it requires intracellular deacetylation to be useful at all. The trace-element literature cited is honestly labelled as inconsistent (PMID 30783336, PMID 21979243) and cannot distinguish association from confounding by nutrition or smoking. PMID 41613336 is cited fairly — a same-design topical with in vitro-only data and no clinical evidence, which is a precedent for the format, not for efficacy. Credit where due: safety is the strongest part. Topical zinc on the scalp is extremely well tolerated, and routing around oral copper (narrow window, zinc antagonism) and oral tyrosine loading is a real improvement over the run's existing supplement hypotheses. Novelty is low — marketed complex — with a sliver of credit for separating tyrosinase-copper from TYRP1-zinc, even though the separation does not cash out therapeutically. Revisable only as a cheap negative-control arm inside a controlled design; do not advance it as a standalone lead, and strip the zinc-cofactor rationale down to 'unproven and mechanistically unlikely'.
Validation

No external validation recorded

36. palmitoyl dipeptide-52 (Silverfree) (H043) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 4)

Mechanism
Palmitoyl dipeptide-52 is a lipidated dipeptide marketed specifically for the canities endpoint, positioned as acting on follicular melanocyte function/pigment retention rather than as a straightforward MC1R agonist. Because the supplier does not publish a fully specified target, the mechanistic claim that can honestly be made is narrow: a lipopeptide designed to penetrate the follicular infundibulum and support melanogenesis in the pigmentary unit, in the same general class as the alpha-MSH biomimetics but without a defined receptor. That mechanistic vagueness is itself the main scientific objection to it, and it should be scored down for that, not up.
Evidence
The supplier claims four clinical studies with reductions in white-hair density from -32% to -86%. NONE of these are peer-reviewed, none appear in the PubMed corpus available to this run, and no PMID can be cited for them — I am explicitly declining to attach a PMID to these numbers. Treat the effect sizes as marketing copy until independently replicated; a -86% reduction in white-hair density would be, by a wide margin, the largest repigmentation effect ever reported for any intervention including systemic anti-PD1 therapy, and is not credible on its face given that any pigment change requires new anagen growth from follicles retaining viable melanocyte stem cells (PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887). The only citable grounding is the general biology it claims to address: pigmentation-gene downregulation in grey follicles (PMID 31316609) and the MelSC-depletion model of greying (PMID 36853923, PMID 37781032). (refs: PMID 31316609, PMID 36853923, PMID 37781032, PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887, PMID 30222197)
Safety
Cosmetic leave-on lipopeptide at low percentage; no specific safety signal identified and no systemic exposure expected. Risk profile is essentially that of the vehicle: irritation and contact sensitisation. From a consumer-harm perspective the more relevant risk is financial and psychological rather than toxicological — these are expensive products sold on unverified efficacy claims to people distressed about their appearance (PMID 32155437 documents the psychological weight of greying), and recommending them uncritically is its own kind of harm.
Novelty rationale
Zero novelty as a repurposing hypothesis: it is a commercial anti-greying active sold for exactly this indication. It is included for completeness of the cosmetic-active arm and because a reviewer needs it on the board to compare against palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 — but it is strictly dominated by that candidate on evidence quality, since palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 at least has a peer-reviewed mechanistic and 15-volunteer clinical publication (PMID 30222197) whereas this has none. Recommended disposition: keep as a comparator, do not prioritise for testing ahead of the peptide with published data, and explicitly flag the supplier numbers as unverified in any downstream summary.
Reviews
  • plausibility 3/10, evidence 1/10, safety 7/10, novelty 1/10 — verdict reject. Fatally flawed and the hypothesis very nearly says so itself. There is no specified molecular target — 'a lipopeptide designed to penetrate the follicular infundibulum and support melanogenesis' is not a mechanism, it is a description of a delivery vehicle plus an aspiration. Nothing here is falsifiable: with no receptor, no pathway and no biomarker, there is no experiment that could show the mechanism is wrong, which disqualifies it from a mechanism-driven repurposing portfolio regardless of how the product performs commercially. The efficacy claims (-32% to -86% white-hair density across four supplier studies) are uncitable — correctly, the hypothesis declines to attach PMIDs, and I confirm none of these appear in this run's corpus. A -86% reduction in white-hair density would exceed anything reported for systemic anti-PD1 or imatinib-associated repigmentation and is flatly incompatible with the anagen-cycle constraint (PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887) and with the MelSC-depletion model (PMID 36853923, PMID 37781032) in which follicles that have lost their stem-cell pool cannot repigment at all. The only citable content (PMID 31316609 for pigmentation-gene downregulation) is background biology shared with every other hypothesis in this batch and grounds nothing specific to this molecule. One citation slip worth naming: PMID 32155437 ('The Psychology of Gray Hair') is invoked in the safety notes as documenting the psychological weight of greying, but it is a commentary on the Nature stress/noradrenaline MelSC-depletion work, not a psychosocial-burden study — a small misattribution, but exactly the kind this stage exists to catch. Safety per se is unremarkable: cosmetic leave-on lipopeptide, no plausible systemic exposure, vehicle irritation and sensitisation only; I have not scored it higher because an undefined target means undefined off-target biology. Novelty is zero — a commercial anti-greying active sold for this exact indication. The hypothesis's own recommended disposition (keep as comparator, do not prioritise, flag numbers as unverified) is the right call, and the honest way to implement it is to reject the hypothesis and, if a second cosmetic comparator is genuinely wanted downstream, note this product in a footnote rather than occupying a candidate slot.
Validation

No external validation recorded

37. palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 (Greyverse) + N-acetyl tyrosine/zinc complex, as a hemi-scalp controlled factorial (H044) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 3/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 4)

Mechanism
Signal-plus-substrate combination targeting the two independent ways a surviving follicular melanocyte can fail to pigment a shaft. Arm one (palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20) supplies the MC1R->cAMP->MITF transcriptional drive and, separately, induces catalase and lowers intracellular H2O2 by ~30% (PMID 30222197). Arm two (N-acetyl tyrosine plus zinc) supplies the melanin precursor and the binuclear-zinc cofactor that TYRP1 requires for the DHICA-oxidase step (PMID 28661582). The explicit, falsifiable hypothesis is that in premature canities the transcriptional programme is the primary lesion — grey follicles show GLOBAL downregulation of pigmentation-associated genes (PMID 31316609) — and therefore substrate/cofactor supplementation alone will do essentially nothing, while adding it to MC1R agonism should produce little further benefit either. In other words this is designed as a test that can come back NEGATIVE and be informative, which is what the substrate-supplementation hypotheses already on the board cannot deliver.
Evidence
No combination data exist; this is a design hypothesis built on the two component evidence bases (PMID 30222197 for the peptide, PMID 28661582 and PMID 11092760 for metal-dependent melanogenesis). Its justification is methodological: every human report in this space is uncontrolled and confounded — the Greyverse case gave concurrent oral supplements (PMID 37034550), the Melitane case likewise (PMID 31831925) — so effect attribution is impossible. A hemi-scalp (left/right split) design with standardised photography and incident-grey-hair counting against the published graying severity scoring approach (PMID 27294049) uses each subject as their own control and removes the seasonal, nutritional and regression-to-the-mean confounds that make single-arm cosmetic studies uninterpretable. Timeline must be pre-specified at 6-9 months minimum because pigment change requires a new anagen cycle (PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887). (refs: PMID 30222197, PMID 28661582, PMID 11092760, PMID 31316609, PMID 37034550, PMID 31831925, PMID 27294049, PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887)
Safety
Both arms are marketed cosmetic leave-on actives, so total risk is that of two vehicles applied to different halves of one scalp: irritation, contact sensitisation, and the cosmetic nuisance of visibly different product on each side. No systemic exposure concern. The split-scalp design adds no new pharmacological risk but does require that the subject tolerates a several-month commitment without cutting or dyeing the hair, which is the real feasibility limit. Same naevus-avoidance precaution for the MC1R arm.
Novelty rationale
The ingredients are not novel — both are sold commercially for greying — and this must be said plainly. The novelty is entirely in the epistemics: this run's 39 existing hypotheses are almost all single-agent mechanism claims with no design that could distinguish them from natural variation, and the two published human canities reports are both confounded by concurrent oral supplements. Proposing the first properly controlled, within-subject comparison in this space, using the only actives with any human canities data, is a more valuable contribution than a fortieth mechanism. It also directly attacks the substrate-supplementation hypotheses already on the board (oral L-tyrosine, copper) by putting them in a design where they can be shown to add nothing.
Reviews
  • plausibility 7/10, evidence 4/10, safety 8/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict revise. The best entry in the batch and the only one whose contribution survives contact with the evidence, but the design as written cannot deliver what it claims. It is labelled a factorial and states the falsifiable prediction that substrate/cofactor 'alone will do essentially nothing, while adding it to MC1R agonism should produce little further benefit either' — that is an interaction claim, and it requires four conditions (vehicle, peptide, substrate/zinc, combination). A two-sided hemi-scalp split has two conditions and no combination cell, so the stated hypothesis is untestable by the stated design. Fix the arm structure or drop the factorial language. Second, hemi-scalp splitting of leave-on scalp lotions is the wrong isolation method: runoff during application, washing, sweat and pillow contact cross-contaminate the halves, and a 10 ppm peptide has no margin to survive dilution onto the control side. Third, GSS (PMID 27294049) is a whole-scalp instrument scored across five representative sites in 52 subjects and was never validated for hemi-scalp use or for within-subject change over time; hemi-scalp baseline symmetry of canities is assumed, not established. Fourth, the negative-result-is-informative framing is weakened by dosing: a null at cosmetic concentrations cannot distinguish 'MC1R agonism does not work here' from 'this dose never reached the bulb', so a pre-specified pharmacodynamic readout (plucked-bulb MC1R/TYRP1 protein as in PMID 30222197, plus Fontana-Masson melanosome distribution) is required or the negative is uninterpretable. Fifth, feasibility: 6-9 months with no cutting or dyeing, in exactly the cosmetically-motivated population most likely to dye, is a dropout machine — power calculations and a realistic attrition assumption are missing. Evidence scores low because there are no combination data and the two component bases are thin: PMID 30222197's human arm is a biomarker study, not a colour endpoint, and PMID 28661582's zinc site is a buried biosynthetic metal, not a topically-supplementable cofactor (see H042). Safety is a non-issue — two marketed cosmetic leave-on vehicles, no systemic exposure, same naevus precaution. Novelty is genuinely mid-range and honestly framed: the actives are off-the-shelf, but proposing the first within-subject controlled test in a field where both published human reports (PMID 37034550, PMID 31831925) are confounded by concurrent oral supplements is worth more than another mechanism. Revise into a properly powered four-arm or crossover design with pharmacodynamic co-primaries, then it is the run's best candidate for actual execution.
Validation

No external validation recorded

38. TACROLIMUS ANHYDROUS (H045) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL269732 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 4)

Mechanism
Topical tacrolimus (FK506) is a calcineurin inhibitor, but the relevant pharmacology here is not immunosuppression — it is direct MOBILISATION of melanocyte precursors. In vitiligo, tacrolimus ointment combined with NB-UVB produced not only the expected migration of melanocytes out of the hair follicle bulge but, for the first time histologically, recruitment of dermal melanocyte precursors into the mid and superficial dermis, with interfollicular as well as perifollicular repigmentation (PMID 32303824). Mechanistic work shows FK506 combined with endothelin induces melanoblast MOTILITY, framed explicitly as a follicular-vitiligo insight (PMID 21039414). This maps onto the central lesion of greying: the melanocyte stem cell pool in the bulge fails to shuttle out and repopulate the bulb each anagen, and instead differentiates ectopically in place and is lost (PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923, PMID 37076619). A drug that increases melanoblast migratory capacity attacks the trafficking failure rather than the pigment-chemistry failure, which is a node no current hypothesis in this run touches.
Evidence
PMID 32303824: histopathological plus clinical study, 20 vitiligo patients, tacrolimus 0.1% ointment twice daily plus NB-UVB versus NB-UVB alone, showing bulge melanocyte migration and dermal melanocyte precursor recruitment. PMID 21039414: FK506 plus endothelin induces melanoblast mobility in a follicular vitiligo model. Related: melanocyte stem cell mobilisation is an explicit regenerative strategy for repigmentation (PMID 28029168, PMID 25104310, PMID 30537004). Adjacent human signal: a case report of cyclosporine A-induced hair repigmentation in a dermatitis patient (PMID 37132003) — same calcineurin-inhibitor class, and notable because cyclosporine is already in this candidate universe. Honest limits: ALL of this evidence is vitiligo, an autoimmune depigmentation with an intact-but-suppressed melanocyte reservoir and an inflammatory component that tacrolimus also relieves. Age-related and premature canities involve MelSC exhaustion, not immune destruction, so the transfer of this evidence is a genuine inferential leap (PMID 39258892 explicitly discusses commonalities and differences between vitiligo repigmentation and hair responses). No canities data exist. (refs: PMID 32303824, PMID 21039414, PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923, PMID 37076619, PMID 28029168, PMID 25104310, PMID 30537004, PMID 37132003, PMID 39258892)
Safety
Prescription, but topical tacrolimus 0.1% ointment is one of the best-characterised chronic-use topicals in dermatology, licensed for long-term intermittent facial and eyelid use in atopic dermatitis in adults and children. Systemic absorption through intact scalp skin is minimal and blood levels are typically undetectable; unlike topical corticosteroids it causes no skin atrophy or telangiectasia, which matters for an indication requiring months of application. Real drawbacks: transient burning/stinging and alcohol-related flushing on application, a boxed warning regarding a theoretical malignancy risk (long-term epidemiology has not confirmed a causal lymphoma or skin cancer signal but the warning stands), and the ointment vehicle is cosmetically poor on hair-bearing scalp — a lotion or solution would need compounding. The vitiligo evidence pairs it with NB-UVB, and adding phototherapy to a cosmetic indication substantially worsens the risk-benefit (cumulative UV carcinogenicity), so I would test tacrolimus WITHOUT UV and accept the likely loss of effect rather than irradiate a healthy scalp. Should be limited to a small concealed test zone.
Novelty rationale
Genuinely non-obvious and not an established hair treatment. Tacrolimus has known dermatological uses including vitiligo and some off-label use in alopecia areata, and that must be flagged — but it has never been proposed for canities, and the rationale here is deliberately NOT the immunosuppressive one that drives its alopecia areata and vitiligo use. It is the melanoblast-motility pharmacology (PMID 21039414) and the demonstrated recruitment of follicular and dermal melanocyte precursors (PMID 32303824). Among the 39 existing hypotheses, every mechanism is either redox (melatonin, NAC, curcumin, sulforaphane, selenium, tocotrienols), melanogenesis-signalling (prostaglandins, adenosine, caffeine, afamelanotide, misoprostol), substrate/deficiency (tyrosine, copper, B12, iron, vitamin D, folate, PABA), stress-axis (timolol, ashwagandha) or immune (ruxolitinib). None targets stem-cell TRAFFICKING, which the Nature-era MelSC literature identifies as the actual defect.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 5/10, safety 7/10, novelty 6/10 — verdict revise. Citations are real and correctly attributed (I checked every PMID against the corpus), but the mechanism is oversold at exactly the point it matters. PMID 21039414 does NOT show that FK506 mobilises melanoblasts. The abstract is explicit: 'cell motility ... was not enhanced by FK506 treatment' and ET-3 alone also failed; motility appeared only with FK506 PLUS ET-3, and the proposed route is FK506-induced ETB receptor expression in NCCmelb4 cells. So the drug is at best a permissive sensitiser that requires an endothelin signal the aged/exhausted follicular niche may not be supplying. The hypothesis's own headline phrase 'direct MOBILISATION of melanocyte precursors' therefore misstates its anchor paper, and this should be corrected before the idea proceeds. The clinical anchor (PMID 32303824) is similarly weaker than presented: 20 vitiligo patients, sequentially allocated 10 vs 10, unblinded, and every repigmentation observation is confounded by NB-UVB, which is itself a potent melanoblast-migration and melanogenesis stimulus. Since the hypothesis quite reasonably refuses to add UV for a cosmetic indication, it is proposing to keep the drug and discard the co-intervention that most plausibly did the work. Second weak link: the disease transfer. Vitiligo has a suppressed-but-intact reservoir plus an inflammatory brake tacrolimus releases; canities is reservoir exhaustion with no autoimmune brake to release, and the hypothesis concedes this. Third, an unacknowledged counter-mechanism: calcineurin/NFAT signalling participates in melanogenic and MITF-linked signalling, so calcineurin inhibition is not unambiguously pro-pigment, and topical tacrolimus has not produced hair darkening as an incidental finding in the very large atopic dermatitis population that has used it for two decades on hair-bearing skin. That negative population-scale signal is the single most damning fact and is not addressed. Safety and delivery reasoning are the strongest parts: minimal systemic absorption, no atrophy, honest about the boxed warning and the unusable ointment vehicle. But it is a prescription drug in a run whose stated constraint is OTC/cosmetic-grade access, and the compelling-rationale bar is not cleared by evidence this indirect. Salvageable as a small concealed-zone ex vivo/pilot probe if the motility claim is restated accurately and an endothelin-dependence test is built in.
Validation

No external validation recorded

39. SIROLIMUS (H046) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL413 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 4)

Mechanism
Topical sirolimus (rapamycin) inhibits mTORC1. The MelSC model of greying is not primarily one of cell death but of inappropriate ACTIVATION: stem cells that should shuttle reversibly between quiescent bulge and transit-amplifying states instead differentiate ectopically in the niche, pigment in place, and are permanently lost from the reversible pool (PMID 33598985, PMID 37076619, PMID 41932944). mTORC1 is the canonical sensor that pushes adult stem cells out of quiescence into differentiation, and its inhibition preserves quiescence and self-renewal capacity in multiple stem cell compartments; mTOR inhibition also lowers mitochondrial ROS output, which matters because mitochondrial ROS specifically has been shown to cause MelSC exhaustion and greying (PMID 40522608). So the hypothesis is a preventive one with an unusual sign: rather than stimulating pigment, deliberately RESTRAIN MelSC activation so the reservoir survives more anagen cycles. This predicts an effect on incident new grey hairs and essentially none on already-white follicles — a much more honest and testable claim than 'reverses grey hair'.
Evidence
There is no direct sirolimus-and-greying study; this is a mechanism-first hypothesis and should be scored as such. Grounding: MelSC quiescence/activation boundary and dedifferentiation-dependent maintenance of the pool (PMID 41932944, PMID 37076619); ectopic differentiation as the loss mechanism in human follicles ex vivo (PMID 33598985) and in mouse genetics (PMID 25495036, PMID 30651286); mitochondrial-ROS-driven MelSC exhaustion (PMID 40522608); aging of follicular stem cells and their niche (PMID 28593683); Wnt/beta-catenin signalling promotes aging-associated greying in mice (PMID 29050206), a useful precedent that MORE differentiation signalling can be the problem rather than the solution, which is the counterintuitive premise this hypothesis shares. Also relevant as a caution: greying has been framed as an evolutionary checkpoint against malignancy in which MelSC loss is protective (PMID 41518577), so pharmacologically retaining stem cells is not automatically benign. (refs: PMID 41932944, PMID 37076619, PMID 33598985, PMID 40522608, PMID 25495036, PMID 30651286, PMID 28593683, PMID 29050206, PMID 41518577)
Safety
Prescription systemic immunosuppressant, but topical sirolimus is an established, well-tolerated dermatological formulation used chronically on the face for angiofibromas in tuberous sclerosis, including in children, with minimal or undetectable systemic levels and no reported systemic immunosuppression at topical doses. That is the only acceptable route here — oral sirolimus is absolutely inappropriate for a cosmetic indication (myelosuppression, dyslipidaemia, impaired wound healing, infection risk, pneumonitis). Even topically this warrants a small concealed test zone, avoidance of broken skin, and a hard stop if any systemic symptom appears. Additional honest concern specific to this mechanism: hair follicles are cycling tissue and mTOR inhibition can impair anagen progression, so there is a real theoretical risk of slowing hair growth or provoking shedding — the opposite of what a cosmetic user wants. This risk should be pre-declared as the primary stopping criterion.
Novelty rationale
Non-obvious, not an established hair treatment, and mechanistically orthogonal to all 39 current hypotheses. Every existing candidate is either a pigment stimulant, an antioxidant, a nutrient, or a stress-axis blocker; all of them implicitly assume 'more melanocyte activity is better'. This one argues the opposite — that greying is a stem-cell budget problem and that restraining activation preserves the budget — which is the direct pharmacological reading of the ectopic-differentiation literature. It is also honest about its ceiling: it cannot repigment existing white hair and should only ever be evaluated on incident greying in someone with active, progressing canities. I would rate its prior probability of clinical benefit as low, but its mechanistic distinctiveness and its clean falsifiability are high, and it is the kind of hypothesis a generation stage exists to produce.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 2/10, safety 5/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict revise. Intellectually the most interesting framing in this batch and the citation set is clean, but it is mechanism-only and the mechanism has a sign problem the author did not consider. mTOR inhibition is a canonical inducer of autophagy, and autophagic degradation of melanosomes plus reduced MITF-driven melanogenic output is a well-described consequence of rapamycin in pigment cells; rapamycin is more often reported as hypopigmenting than as pigment-sparing. So the same drug that might preserve the stem-cell budget may simultaneously suppress pigment production in the transit-amplifying and differentiated melanocytes that actually colour the shaft. The hypothesis needs to state which effect dominates and how it would be distinguished, otherwise a null or lightening result is uninterpretable. The 'quiescence preserves the pool' argument is also imported wholesale from HSC/muscle stem cell biology; none of the nine cited papers tests mTOR in melanocyte stem cells at all, and PMID 41932944 and PMID 37076619 describe a dedifferentiation-dependent dynamic niche in which forcing quiescence could equally well starve the pool of the activation cycles it needs to dedifferentiate and replenish. Second problem: hair-cycle toxicity is not a side note, it is a competing primary effect. mTOR signalling is required for anagen entry and progression; a cosmetic user who trades grey hairs for shed hairs has strictly lost. The hypothesis pre-declares this as a stop rule, which is honest, but a stop rule does not rescue a mechanism whose on-target action plausibly causes the harm. Third, and practically fatal for this run: the claim is preventive-only, so the endpoint is incident greying over multiple hair cycles, i.e. a 2-4 year read on a slow, heterogeneous, self-assessed outcome. There is no feasible consumer-scale test, and the hypothesis offers none. Prescription immunosuppressant, so it also sits badly against the OTC constraint even granting that topical sirolimus is genuinely well tolerated in tuberous sclerosis. The PMID 41518577 caution about MelSC retention and malignancy is admirably raised and then not acted on. Keep as a preclinical ex vivo probe (the PMID 33598985 human follicle assay would test it directly and cheaply); do not advance as an intervention.
Validation

No external validation recorded

40. CLONIDINE (H047) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL134 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 4)

Mechanism
Clonidine is an alpha-2 adrenergic agonist. Its relevant action is PRESYNAPTIC: agonism of the alpha-2 autoreceptor on sympathetic nerve terminals suppresses noradrenaline release. Hyperactivation of sympathetic nerves innervating the follicle drives depletion of the melanocyte stem cell pool by forcing MelSCs into proliferation and ectopic differentiation from which they cannot return (PMID 31969699), and noradrenaline applied to isolated human follicles ex vivo reproduces ectopic MelSC pigmentation (PMID 33598985). The existing hypotheses in this run attack that axis POSTsynaptically with timolol, a beta-antagonist blocking the beta-2 receptor on the MelSC itself. Clonidine attacks the same axis one step upstream, cutting the amount of noradrenaline released at the terminal in the first place — which also removes noradrenaline's alpha-receptor-mediated and non-beta-2 effects, including local vasoconstriction of the perifollicular microvasculature, that pure beta-blockade leaves untouched. Given that natural-product work in this space (rhynchophylline) validated the beta-2 route specifically (PMID 38036925), the upstream-versus-downstream distinction is a real and testable fork.
Evidence
No clonidine-and-greying data exist. Grounding is the sympathetic-depletion mechanism: PMID 31969699 (hyperactivation of sympathetic nerves depletes MelSCs — the primary mouse finding), PMID 33598985 (noradrenaline induces ectopic MelSC differentiation in isolated HUMAN follicles ex vivo, the key human-tissue bridge), PMID 38036925 (rhynchophylline prevents stress-induced greying via the beta-2 adrenergic receptor — pharmacological proof that blunting this axis is protective, though rhynchophylline itself is not in the candidate universe), PMID 31989871 and PMID 38933161 (commentary on the norepinephrine-greying link and its limits). Important honest caveat: the causal chain from psychological stress to human greying remains unproven; the human evidence is associative (PMID 31523106) and the mechanism is mouse-derived. Also note MC1R is dispensable for acute stress-induced greying (PMID 33382172), which means the stress axis and the melanocortin axis are separable targets and a combination is not redundant. (refs: PMID 31969699, PMID 33598985, PMID 38036925, PMID 31989871, PMID 38933161, PMID 31523106, PMID 33382172)
Safety
This is the weakest link in the hypothesis and I will not soften it. Clonidine is a potent centrally-acting antihypertensive with a serious systemic profile — hypotension, bradycardia, marked sedation, dry mouth, and, critically, REBOUND HYPERTENSION on abrupt withdrawal. It is also highly lipophilic and readily absorbed transdermally; a transdermal clonidine patch is a marketed systemic delivery system, which is direct proof that scalp application would deliver systemic drug. For a cosmetic indication with zero tolerance for systemic effects, that is close to disqualifying. The only defensible version is a very low-strength compounded lotion on a small concealed test zone with blood pressure and heart rate monitoring, in a normotensive adult, with a pre-specified stop rule — and I would rank it behind topical tacrolimus and behind the timolol hypotheses already on the board on safety grounds alone. Apraclonidine, a poorly-CNS-penetrant alpha-2 agonist also present in the candidate universe, would be the rational safer substitute if this mechanism is pursued, and reviewers should consider swapping it in.
Novelty rationale
Not an established hair treatment and not previously proposed for greying. Its novelty relative to the current leader set is specifically the PRESYNAPTIC angle: hypotheses H017, H029 and H039 all use timolol to block the postsynaptic beta receptor, so a reviewer could reasonably ask whether this is a reworded duplicate. It is not — reducing transmitter release and blocking the receptor make different predictions (alpha-2 agonism spares beta signalling from circulating adrenaline, blocks alpha-mediated perifollicular vasoconstriction, and is dose-limited by presynaptic autoreceptor saturation), and they have different safety failure modes. I am flagging honestly that the safety profile makes this a mechanistic probe rather than a deployable cosmetic intervention, and that its main practical value may be to motivate substituting apraclonidine.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 2/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict reject. Reject, and on three independent grounds. First, a factual pharmacology error in the differentiating argument. The hypothesis claims presynaptic alpha-2 agonism has the bonus of removing 'noradrenaline's alpha-receptor-mediated ... local vasoconstriction of the perifollicular microvasculature'. That is backwards: alpha-2 agonists are direct vasoconstrictors at postsynaptic vascular alpha-2 receptors, which is precisely why brimonidine and apraclonidine are marketed to blanch skin and conjunctiva. Topical clonidine on scalp would reduce perifollicular perfusion, not restore it, and hypoperfusion of the bulb is a plausible way to make matters worse. The proposed apraclonidine substitute shares this and is if anything the more potent skin blancher. Since the vasoconstriction claim is one of only two stated reasons this is not a reworded timolol hypothesis, removing it collapses much of the novelty case against H017/H029/H039. Second, safety is close to disqualifying and the hypothesis says so itself: transdermal clonidine is a marketed SYSTEMIC delivery system, which is proof rather than speculation that scalp application delivers drug to the circulation, with sedation, bradycardia, hypotension and rebound hypertension on withdrawal. Requiring blood pressure monitoring for a cosmetic intervention in a healthy normotensive adult is an admission that the risk-benefit does not close. Third, the evidence base is a mouse acute-restraint-stress model (PMID 31969699) plus an ex vivo noradrenaline exposure (PMID 33598985) plus a docking-and-mouse natural product study (PMID 38036925) - all of which model acute stress-induced greying, whereas premature canities is a chronic, strongly heritable (IRF4) process whose link to psychological stress is only associative in humans (PMID 31523106, correctly flagged). Blunting sympathetic tone systemically for years to influence a process whose human stress-dependence is unproven is a poor trade. All PMIDs cited are genuine and accurately characterised, which is why evidence scores 3 rather than 1, but the pharmacology error and the systemic exposure make this a non-starter as written.
Validation

No external validation recorded

41. DEFEROXAMINE (H048) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL556 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 4)

Mechanism
Deferoxamine is a high-affinity ferric iron chelator. The mechanistic premise here is a deliberate inversion of the run's dominant redox strategy. Grey and white follicles accumulate millimolar hydrogen peroxide (PMID 19237503), but H2O2 itself is a relatively sluggish oxidant; its destructiveness comes from Fenton chemistry, in which labile redox-active iron converts it to the hydroxyl radical, which is indiscriminate and reacts at diffusion-limited rates with proteins, lipids and DNA. Every antioxidant hypothesis already on the board (topical melatonin, NAC, tocotrienols, curcumin, tetrahydrocurcumin, sulforaphane, selenium) tries to remove or destroy the peroxide, which is stoichiometrically hopeless at millimolar concentrations. Chelating the catalytic labile iron instead is CATALYTIC on the defence side: a small amount of chelator can suppress a large amount of radical generation without needing to be consumed one-for-one. This also connects to the mitochondrial-ROS mechanism of MelSC exhaustion (PMID 40522608) and to the fact that the melanogenic pathway itself generates quinone/ROS intermediates within melanosomes.
Evidence
There is no deferoxamine-and-greying study; this is an explicitly mechanism-derived hypothesis. Supporting: peroxide accumulation with blunted catalase and impaired methionine sulfoxide repair in human grey follicles (PMID 19237503); oxidative stress as a multi-effect driver of greying (PMID 24033376, PMID 20805969); systemic oxidative stress correlating with severity of greying in patients (PMID 32549695, PMID 26622150); mitochondrial ROS causing MelSC exhaustion (PMID 40522608); an H2O2-induced depigmentation model used to test regenerative interventions (PMID 42257913), which is the obvious preclinical assay for this hypothesis. Direct counter-evidence that must be weighed and that I consider the single strongest objection: LOW iron status (low ferritin) is repeatedly associated with premature canities in human studies (PMID 36386090, PMID 28584374, PMID 30607040), so iron deprivation at the follicle could plausibly make greying WORSE, and one published canities case was successfully treated with ferrous sulfate. Chelating a nutrient that patients may already be short of is a real and unresolved contradiction, not a rhetorical one. (refs: PMID 19237503, PMID 24033376, PMID 20805969, PMID 32549695, PMID 26622150, PMID 40522608, PMID 42257913, PMID 36386090, PMID 28584374, PMID 30607040)
Safety
Systemic deferoxamine is unacceptable for a cosmetic indication — it requires parenteral administration and carries ototoxicity, retinal toxicity, hypotension on rapid infusion, and increased risk of mucormycosis. Only topical use is contemplated, and topical deferoxamine has been developed in wound-healing and radiation-dermatitis patch formats, so a cosmetically usable delivery is at least precedented. The molecule is large and hydrophilic with poor skin penetration, which is simultaneously a safety advantage (negligible systemic exposure) and the main efficacy obstacle (it may never reach the bulb, which sits deep in the dermis). Additional specific risks: deferoxamine-iron complexes are coloured and could stain hair or scalp, and sustained local iron deprivation in a rapidly proliferating tissue could impair hair growth, since iron is required for ribonucleotide reductase in the fastest-dividing cells in the body. Small concealed test zone only; contraindicated in anyone with iron deficiency or low ferritin, which should be a screening requirement rather than an afterthought.
Novelty rationale
Genuinely novel here and not an established hair treatment in any form. Its value is that it is the only hypothesis in the run that attacks the peroxide problem CATALYTICALLY rather than stoichiometrically — an objection that, per the current leader set, has already sunk pseudocatalase and constrains every antioxidant arm. It is also the most falsifiable of the redox hypotheses: it makes a specific prediction (protection tracks labile iron, not total antioxidant capacity) and it has a specific, identifiable failure mode already stated above (the ferritin association runs the wrong way). I am deliberately proposing a hypothesis I expect may fail, because it isolates a variable none of the existing antioxidant hypotheses can separate, and because a clean negative would strengthen the interpretation of the whole redox arm.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 5/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict revise. The catalytic-versus-stoichiometric argument is the best reasoning in this batch and is a legitimate criticism of the run's crowded antioxidant arm. But the hypothesis names its own fatal problem and then does not resolve it, and there is a second one it misses. The stated problem: low ferritin is repeatedly associated with premature canities in the very papers cited (PMID 36386090 - 17.5% of cases with low ferritin; PMID 28584374; PMID 30607040), so this proposes iron deprivation in a population where iron insufficiency is a candidate cause. 'Screen out low-ferritin subjects' does not fix this, because the association suggests the follicle is operating near an iron floor even at normal serum ferritin, and iron is required for ribonucleotide reductase in one of the fastest-proliferating tissues in the body. The missed problem is delivery, and it is decisive: deferoxamine is a ~560 Da, highly hydrophilic, poorly permeant molecule; the wound-healing and radiation-dermatitis patch formats cited as precedent work on damaged or barrier-compromised skin, whereas here the target is the bulb of a terminal follicle sitting deep in the dermis under an intact stratum corneum. The hypothesis correctly notes this is 'simultaneously a safety advantage and the main efficacy obstacle' but then does not propose any formulation strategy, so the most likely outcome is a null result caused by non-delivery, which teaches nothing about the Fenton mechanism it exists to test. A third unaddressed point: melanin is itself a strong metal chelator and the follicle already sequesters iron in melanosomes, so 'labile iron' in the pigmentary unit may be a much smaller pool than the argument assumes. Also note PMID 42257913, offered as the preclinical assay, is a guinea pig PRP/exosome study in an H2O2 depigmentation model - a reasonable assay to borrow, but no part of it tests chelation. Evidence is thus entirely mechanistic; every PMID is real and fairly described. Salvageable only if reframed around a skin-permeant chelator or a demonstrated penetration-enhanced formulation, with hair-shaft/scalp staining and growth-rate as pre-specified safety endpoints.
Validation

No external validation recorded

42. PHYSOSTIGMINE (H049) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL94 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 4)

Mechanism
Physostigmine is a reversible cholinesterase inhibitor, raising local acetylcholine concentrations. The rationale is driven by an underexploited HUMAN observation rather than by pathway speculation: among 62 Chinese Alzheimer patients on prolonged cholinesterase inhibitor therapy, 24 (38.7%) reported darkening of grey hair, predominantly occipital, and melanin concentration in the darkened hair did not differ significantly from controls' naturally dark hair (PMID 32597493) — i.e. the reported repigmentation was quantitatively real rather than a shine or texture artefact. Mechanistically, the follicle has a functioning non-neuronal cholinergic system: keratinocytes and melanocytes express acetylcholine receptors and the synthetic and degradative enzymes, and cholinergic signalling modulates melanocyte dendricity, adhesion and pigment transfer. Sustained cholinesterase inhibition would raise ambient acetylcholine at the follicular pigmentary unit. There is also a plausible interaction with the sympathetic axis: enhanced cholinergic tone opposes the noradrenergic drive that depletes MelSCs (PMID 31969699).
Evidence
PMID 32597493 is the anchor and is worth more than most evidence in this run because it is a human, quantified, hair-colour endpoint with a melanin measurement — but its weaknesses are severe and must be stated: it is a cross-sectional survey with SELF-REPORTED hair darkening as the ascertainment step, no photographic baseline, no control group of untreated Alzheimer patients, no blinding, an elderly population in whom other medication changes and nutritional shifts are common, and an implausibly high 38.7% response rate that is more consistent with reporting bias than with a real drug effect. The regional pattern (70.9% occipital) is intriguing and hard to explain by bias alone, which is the main reason to take it seriously at all. Supporting context: the sympathetic/adrenergic depletion mechanism it might oppose (PMID 31969699, PMID 33598985); reviews cataloguing drug-associated hair repigmentation as a recurring and under-investigated phenomenon (PMID 37781032, PMID 30768676, PMID 40546989, PMID 28918974). No mechanistic study of cholinergic signalling in canities exists; this is a lead, not a finding. (refs: PMID 32597493, PMID 31969699, PMID 33598985, PMID 37781032, PMID 30768676, PMID 40546989, PMID 28918974)
Safety
Systemic cholinesterase inhibition is unacceptable here — bradycardia, bronchoconstriction, nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, sweating, and at high exposure a cholinergic crisis; physostigmine crosses the blood-brain barrier, which makes it the worst choice in its class for incidental systemic exposure.
