A topical scalp preparation is nevertheless conceivable, since physostigmine is used topically in ophthalmology, but the predictable local adverse effect is profuse localised sweating and scalp irritation, which for a cosmetic user is likely to be intolerable in itself. Realistic framing: this should be pursued first as a mechanistic question (is follicular cholinergic tone linked to pigmentation?) using a small concealed test zone at low concentration with heart rate monitoring, and if the class effect is real, a peripherally-restricted, non-BBB-penetrant cholinesterase inhibitor such as neostigmine — also present in this candidate universe — would be the safer topical vehicle for the mechanism. I would not recommend consumer self-experimentation with this at any dose.
Novelty rationale
Highly non-obvious, no known hair-related use, and entirely absent from the 39 existing hypotheses, none of which touch cholinergic signalling. The cholinesterase-inhibitor repigmentation observation (PMID 32597493) is exactly the kind of overlooked human signal that a repurposing exercise should surface, and it went unseen in the first three rounds. I am flagging honestly that the strength of this candidate is the observation, NOT the drug: the survey implicates the class (donepezil, rivastigmine, galantamine in typical Alzheimer practice), and physostigmine is proposed as the topically-tractable class representative available in this universe rather than because it was the drug studied. Its realistic disposition is as a preclinical mechanism probe — for instance in the ex vivo human follicle ectopic-differentiation assay (PMID 33598985) — not as a near-term cosmetic intervention.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 2/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict revise. The anchor observation is genuine and I verified it: PMID 32597493 reports 24/62 (38.7%) of Chinese Alzheimer patients on cholinesterase inhibitors for 6+ months reporting hair darkening, 70.9% occipital, with melanin concentration not differing from controls' dark hair. That is one of very few human hair-colour endpoints anywhere in this run and the hypothesis is commendably brutal about its weaknesses (self-report ascertainment, no photography, no untreated control arm, no blinding). Two things it under-weights. First, the melanin measurement does not do the work claimed for it: showing that darkened hair has melanin content similar to normally dark hair is expected under either hypothesis - if a hair grew pigmented it will have normal melanin - and it does not establish that the hair was previously white, which only a photographic or segmental analysis of the same shaft could. So the 'quantitatively real rather than a shine artefact' claim overreads the data. Second, the occipital predominance offered as evidence against reporting bias has an obvious confound in a bedbound elderly cohort: the occiput is the region under mechanical pressure and the region a patient cannot self-inspect, so it is the region a caregiver reports on, and it is also where hair is most subject to matting and moisture. The deeper structural flaw is drug identity. The observation implicates donepezil/rivastigmine/galantamine given orally for years; physostigmine was not studied, and it is the WORST member of the class to pick for incidental systemic exposure because it crosses the blood-brain barrier. The predicted local effect - profuse scalp sweating from muscarinic stimulation of eccrine glands, plus irritation - is likely to make a topical intolerable before any pigment endpoint could be read over the months a hair cycle requires. Follicular non-neuronal cholinergic signalling is real but no cited paper links it to canities; that part is pathway speculation dressed as mechanism. I am marking revise rather than reject only because the underlying class observation is the strongest human canities signal in this batch and touches a pathway nothing else in the run touches. The revision required is not cosmetic: swap to a peripherally restricted, non-BBB-penetrant agent (neostigmine, as the hypothesis itself suggests), and test first in the ex vivo human follicle assay (PMID 33598985) rather than on a person. Do not advance physostigmine itself.
Validation

No external validation recorded

43. copper tripeptide-1 (GHK-Cu) 1-2% leave-on scalp serum (H055) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL55643 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 5)

Mechanism
Tyrosinase is a binuclear COPPER monooxygenase and is the rate-limiting enzyme of follicular melanogenesis; without its two Cu(I) ions it is catalytically dead. GHK-Cu is glycyl-L-histidyl-L-lysine pre-complexed with Cu2+ at a moderate affinity chosen to allow exchange, so it acts as a copper donor rather than a chelator. The proposed lever is NOT remetallation of mature tyrosinase already sitting in melanosomes — that site is buried in the folded enzyme and unreachable from outside — but enlargement of the bioavailable cytosolic copper pool in bulb melanocytes at anagen onset, when new tyrosinase is being synthesised and metallated. Metallation is chaperone-mediated: cytosolic copper is handed via ATOX1 to the ATP7A (Menkes) P-type ATPase, which pumps it into the trans-Golgi lumen where nascent tyrosinase acquires it. PMID 11092760 is the decisive demonstration: recombinant tyrosinase expressed in Menkes fibroblasts is inactive, co-expression of MNK restores activity and melanogenesis, and that rescue is abolished by chelating copper in the medium. That last detail is what makes a topical copper donor mechanistically coherent at all — the pathway is demonstrably sensitive to the copper available to the cell, not only to the transporter. The claim is therefore narrow and stage-specific: supply copper to the cell during early anagen and only if bulb copper supply, not enzyme abundance or McSC number, is the limiting term.
Evidence
Enzymology anchor: PMID 11092760 (ATP7A-dependent tyrosinase activation, copper-chelation-sensitive). PMID 33995009 confirms tyrosinase is the copper-containing member of the melanogenic metalloenzyme family and that TYRP1/TYRP2 stabilise and modulate it. The objection raised against H042 — that the metal site described in PMID 28661582 is buried and not topically supplementable — does NOT transfer cleanly, because that structure is TYRP1 and its site is a binuclear ZINC site, which is precisely why TYRP1 lacks tyrosinase redox activity; it is a statement about a different enzyme and a different metal. But the objection survives in a modified and still serious form: the copper site of tyrosinase proper is equally buried in the folded protein, so any benefit must come at the biosynthetic/metallation step inside the cell, not by soaking an existing enzyme. The harder question the run has not asked is whether bulb tyrosinase is cofactor-limited at all, and the honest answer from the corpus is that it probably is not the dominant lesion: PMID 19237503 shows tyrosinase in grey follicles is disabled by methionine sulfoxidation at Met374 under millimolar H2O2 with absent catalase and MSRA/B — an oxidative-damage lesion that adding copper does not repair, and free copper can worsen via Fenton chemistry. PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 place the primary defect in reduced MITF- and tyrosinase-controlled melanogenesis, defective melanosome transfer and melanocyte loss, not cofactor scarcity. Human copper-status data are weak and inconsistent: PMID 21979243 found serum copper lower in premature canities but only marginally (90.7 vs 105.3 ug/dL, p = 0.048) and in the same cohort serum IRON was HIGHER in cases, which undercuts a simple micronutrient story; PMID 30783336 reported lower iron, copper and calcium but the significant severity correlations were with iron and calcium, not copper. Serum copper is in any case a poor proxy for the metal actually reaching the bulb. Strongest counter-evidence to the whole cofactor strategy: PMID 41948976 found that what actually reversed graying in mouse, porcine and human skin models was transdermal delivery of the intact active tyrosinase enzyme in polymeric nanocapsules — i.e. investigators who could have supplied the cofactor supplied the holoenzyme instead, implying the bottleneck is functional enzyme, not metal. No canities trial of GHK-Cu exists. (refs: PMID 11092760, PMID 28661582, PMID 33995009, PMID 41948976, PMID 19237503, PMID 21979243, PMID 30783336, PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887)
Safety
Excellent cosmetic-indication fit on tolerability: GHK-Cu is sold worldwide in OTC leave-on serums at roughly 0.05-2%, with decades of consumer exposure and no systemic signal; realistic worst harm is contact irritation or, rarely, sensitisation, plus transient blue-green staining of the scalp/pillow at higher concentrations and possible temporary discolouration of white hair shafts by the copper complex itself, which is a genuine confound for a colour endpoint and must be controlled by washing before photography. Do not co-apply with vitamin C or strong reducing/chelating actives, which strip or reduce the copper. No prescriber needed. Systemic copper toxicity is not plausible at cosmetic topical doses on intact skin; that risk belongs to the oral arms (H004, H053), and this hypothesis is deliberately not gated on serum copper because it is not a repletion strategy.
Novelty rationale
Novel relative to this run in route and mechanism, not as a product. Copper appears in the run only as ORAL repletion (H004 cupric sulfate, H015, H053), all gated on measured deficiency; GHK appears zero times in the 54 hypotheses despite copper enzymology being the run's one named biochemical mechanism in melanogenesis. Being honest: GHK-Cu is a mass-market cosmetic active with a very large existing anti-ageing literature, so proposing it is not a novel repurposing idea in the commercial sense — what is new here is the specific claim (early-anagen intracellular copper supply feeding ATP7A-dependent metallation of nascent tyrosinase) and the explicit acknowledgement that it competes with, and is probably beaten by, the oxidative-damage account of tyrosinase failure. It is also not an MC1R agent and must not be conflated with palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 (H040/H050) or acetyl hexapeptide-1 (H041/H051).
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 8/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict reject. The citations are accurate — PMID 11092760 really does show ATP7A-dependent tyrosinase activation abolished by medium copper chelation, and the author is right that PMID 28661582 is TYRP1's binuclear ZINC site and does not transfer. Credit for that correction. But the hypothesis then narrates its own refutation and submits anyway. Its own evidence_summary concedes the load-bearing premise — that bulb tyrosinase is cofactor-limited — is probably false, and the corpus agrees: PMID 19237503 puts the lesion at Met374 sulfoxidation under millimolar H2O2 with absent catalase/MSRA/B (a covalent oxidative lesion copper cannot repair and, via Fenton chemistry, can worsen); PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 both put the primary human defect at declining MITF/tyrosinase OUTPUT, defective melanosome transfer and melanocyte loss; and PMID 41948976 is close to decisive against the cofactor strategy, since investigators who could have supplied copper supplied the holoenzyme in nanocapsules instead and that is what reversed graying in porcine and human skin. A hypothesis whose stated mechanism is an if-clause the corpus answers 'no' to is not a hypothesis, it is a footnote. Second, independent, and in my view fatal problem for a COSMETIC endpoint: the safety_notes admit copper complexes tint white hair shafts blue-green. The readout here is hair colour. An agent that directly stains the outcome variable, applied leave-on and unblindable (GHK-Cu serums are visibly and distinctively coloured), cannot support a credible colour claim; 'wash before photography' does not rescue a self-applied consumer product whose subjective benefit is exactly the artefact. Third, the delivery step is asserted, not shown: the mechanism requires copper reaching the CYTOSOL of bulb melanocytes several millimetres down, and there is zero follicular pharmacokinetic data and — as H056 admits — no measurement of bulb copper at any greying stage anywhere in this corpus. Even the human copper-status literature refuses to cooperate: PMID 21979243 is marginal (p = 0.048) and in the same cohort serum iron was HIGHER in cases, while PMID 30783336's severity correlations ran with iron and calcium, not copper. Novelty is low regardless — copper peptides are a mass-market scalp/hair cosmetic sold for decades; reframing the marketing as ATP7A metallation is a rationale, not a repurposing idea. Safety is genuinely excellent and OTC access is real, which is the only reason this scores above the floor. Recommend dropping the standalone product hypothesis and keeping only the falsification experiment (H057).
Validation

No external validation recorded

44. palmitoyl tripeptide-1 (palmitoylated GHK) leave-on scalp serum (H056) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 5)

Mechanism
Same target enzymology as GHK-Cu but a different delivery premise and a different, and riskier, metal logic. The C16 palmitoyl chain converts the very hydrophilic GHK tripeptide into a lipid-anchored amphiphile that partitions into sebum and the intercellular lipid lamellae and preferentially loads the follicular infundibulum — the transappendageal route is the only realistic way for a peptide to approach the bulb, and it is exactly the route an unmodified tripeptide fails to take. Once delivered, palmitoyl tripeptide-1 arrives as APO-peptide, so its pigment rationale depends on it picking up endogenous Cu2+ locally and shuttling it into melanocytes, i.e. acting as an ionophore/chaperone that redistributes copper already present in the scalp rather than adding any. The upside over GHK-Cu is penetration and the absence of an exogenous free-metal load next to a follicle already carrying millimolar H2O2, where free copper would drive Fenton chemistry. The downside is symmetrical and must be stated: an apo copper-binding peptide applied in excess can just as easily act as a net CHELATOR, sequestering scalp copper away from cells, in which case the predicted direction of effect reverses. Which behaviour dominates is a function of local concentration and competing ligands and is unknown for scalp skin.
Evidence
The metallation pathway it would feed is PMID 11092760 (ATP7A-dependent activation of tyrosinase, blocked by medium copper chelation) and the enzyme's copper dependence is confirmed in PMID 33995009. There is no canities trial, no follicular pharmacokinetic data, and no measurement of copper concentration in human hair bulbs at any greying stage anywhere in this corpus — so the premise that bulb copper supply limits melanogenesis is untested, not merely unproven. Against it: PMID 19237503 attributes tyrosinase failure in grey follicles to Met374 sulfoxidation under an unrepaired H2O2 load, PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 attribute human greying principally to declining MITF/tyrosinase-driven melanogenesis, defective melanosome transfer and eventual melanocyte and McSC depletion, and PMID 41948976 shows that the intervention which actually reversed graying across mouse, porcine and human skin models delivered active tyrosinase holoenzyme transdermally rather than its cofactor — a direct argument that the deficit is functional enzyme. PMID 28661582 does not apply: its buried metal site is TYRP1's binuclear ZINC site, not tyrosinase copper. Human copper-status evidence is thin and internally inconsistent (PMID 21979243, PMID 30783336). (refs: PMID 11092760, PMID 33995009, PMID 41948976, PMID 19237503, PMID 28661582, PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887, PMID 21979243, PMID 30783336)
Safety
Cosmetic-grade and widely available OTC in anti-ageing and scalp serums; negligible systemic exposure, no prescriber. Lipopeptides carry a modest contact-sensitisation risk — patch test the forearm for 3 days. Unlike GHK-Cu this product contributes no exogenous copper, which removes both the Fenton concern and the blue-green staining/colour-endpoint confound; that makes it the cleaner arm for a photographic colour readout. The specific hazard to watch is the opposite of toxicity: if apo-peptide chelation dominates, prolonged use could in principle depress rather than support local melanogenesis, so any trial should include a stop rule on visible lightening of still-pigmented hair.
Novelty rationale
Genuinely unexplored in this run: no hypothesis among the 54 mentions GHK in any form, and every copper hypothesis to date is oral repletion. It is, however, an already-marketed OTC cosmetic peptide with a large anti-ageing/wound-healing literature, so it is not a novel molecule and I am not claiming it as one; the novelty is the topical copper-delivery framing for canities specifically, plus the honest statement of the chelation-reversal risk that vendors do not mention. It has no MC1R activity and is mechanistically distinct from the alpha-MSH biomimetics already in the run (H040, H041, H043, H050, H051).
Reviews
  • plausibility 3/10, evidence 2/10, safety 7/10, novelty 4/10 — verdict reject. This one is disqualified by a defect the author names but does not resolve: the predicted SIGN of the effect is unknown. Applied as apo-peptide, palmitoyl tripeptide-1 may act as an ionophore that shuttles endogenous copper into melanocytes, or as a net chelator that strips it away, and the hypothesis states plainly that which dominates 'is a function of local concentration and competing ligands and is unknown for scalp skin.' A proposal that cannot say whether it darkens or lightens hair is not falsifiable as a therapy — it is a reagent awaiting a bioassay, and the proposed stop rule on 'visible lightening of still-pigmented hair' is an admission that the trial could actively harm the endpoint it is run to improve. It inherits every weakness of H055's premise (PMID 19237503 oxidative Met374 lesion, PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 pointing at MITF/tyrosinase output and melanosome transfer, PMID 41948976 showing holoenzyme rather than cofactor is what actually works in human skin models) and adds a second unevidenced step on top, since it needs endogenous scalp copper to be both present in a chelatable form and in the right place. The penetration argument is the best thing here and is genuinely better reasoned than most cosmetic-peptide entries in this run: palmitoylation to drive transappendageal infundibular loading is a real and correctly-invoked formulation principle, and the absence of exogenous free copper next to a follicle documented to carry millimolar H2O2 is a legitimate advantage over H055, as is the absence of the blue-green shaft-staining confound. Evidence scores near the floor because there is no citation in this bundle that speaks to palmitoyl tripeptide-1 at all — PMID 11092760 and PMID 33995009 establish that tyrosinase needs copper, which nobody disputes, and say nothing about this molecule, this route, or this indication. Novelty is modest: another already-marketed OTC cosmetic peptide, novel only in framing. The salvageable core is exactly one thing — its role as the copper-free peptide control arm inside H057 — and it should be retained there rather than as a standalone candidate.
Validation

No external validation recorded

45. Vehicle-matched three-arm hemi-scalp discrimination: copper tripeptide-1 (GHK-Cu) vs palmitoyl tripeptide-1 vs cupric chloride, each versus vehicle (H057) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL444436 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 5)

Mechanism
This is a mechanism-discriminating design rather than a new pharmacology, and it exists because the copper-delivery idea has three mutually confusable explanations that no single-arm cosmetic trial can separate. Arm A (GHK-Cu) supplies peptide plus a pre-complexed, exchangeable copper load. Arm B (palmitoyl tripeptide-1) supplies the same GHK backbone with superior follicular penetration but NO added copper, so any benefit must come from the peptide's own signalling or from redistribution of endogenous copper. Arm C (cupric chloride at a matched molar copper dose in the same vehicle) supplies copper with no peptide carrier at all. The predictions dissociate cleanly: if copper delivery is the active principle, A and C converge and B is inert or negative; if the peptide is the active principle, A and B converge and C is inert; if only the chaperoned form works, A alone separates, which is the signature of a delivery-limited metallation step feeding ATP7A. A null across all three arms is the most informative outcome available and would close the topical copper question for this programme instead of leaving it to be re-proposed each round.
Evidence
The hypothesis being tested is PMID 11092760's implication that cellular copper availability gates tyrosinase activation, applied to the bulb. It is worth running only because the competing explanation is strong and specific: PMID 19237503 locates the tyrosinase defect in Met374 sulfoxidation under millimolar follicular H2O2 with absent catalase and MSRA/B, a lesion no cofactor supplementation repairs; PMID 41948976 shows holoenzyme delivery, not cofactor delivery, reversing graying in human-skin models; PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 both place the primary human lesion at declining MITF/tyrosinase output and melanosome transfer in the anagen bulb. The human copper-status literature cannot adjudicate: PMID 21979243 (copper marginally lower, iron paradoxically higher in cases) and PMID 30783336 (lower iron/copper/calcium, severity correlating with iron and calcium rather than copper) are association studies on serum, which does not report bulb copper. Design constraints follow from the biology: PMID 38099887 states that only during anagen can the phenotype of a growing hair change and that early anagen is the phase most conducive to melanocytic reintegration, so the endpoint must be a proximal-shaft repigmentation band on individually tagged hairs plus trichoscopic counts of pigmented versus unpigmented emerging hairs in matched 1 cm2 zones at 0, 6 and 12 months, not global photographs. PMID 27294049's graying severity score gives a standardised global scale as a secondary measure. (refs: PMID 11092760, PMID 19237503, PMID 41948976, PMID 32965076, PMID 38099887, PMID 21979243, PMID 30783336, PMID 27294049, PMID 28661582)
Safety
Arms A and B are OTC cosmetic actives with benign profiles. Arm C is the one requiring care: an inorganic copper salt at a molar-matched dose has no cosmetic precedent as a leave-on scalp product, is a mild irritant, will stain, and — the substantive concern — deposits free redox-active copper onto a follicle already documented to carry millimolar H2O2, where Fenton chemistry could plausibly accelerate rather than retard the oxidative lesion. Arm C should therefore be capped at the lowest molar-matched concentration, confined to a small concealed retroauricular zone, and abandoned on any irritation. All arms are topical with no systemic copper exposure. Copper compounds discolour white hair shafts directly, so all photography must follow a standardised wash and every colour reading must be shaft-band-based, not surface-tint-based.
Novelty rationale
New as a design, not as a molecule: nothing in the 54 hypotheses tests topical copper at all, and the existing copper hypotheses (H004, H015, H053) are oral repletion gated on serum deficiency, which cannot answer the delivery question. The specific contribution is the copper-free peptide arm and the peptide-free copper arm, which together make the cosmetic-active hypotheses falsifiable instead of merely plausible — the failure mode that recurs across this run's cosmetic-peptide entries, where supplier claims cannot be attributed to any component. Cupric chloride here is a mechanistic control, not a proposed consumer product.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 4/10, safety 6/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict revise. The instinct is right and rare in this run: build the experiment that can kill the idea rather than another product entry, and treat a null across all arms as the informative outcome. But the factorial logic does not survive inspection. Arm A (GHK-Cu) and Arm B (palmitoyl tripeptide-1) differ in TWO variables at once — copper load AND lipid-anchored follicular penetration — so 'A and B converge means the peptide is active' and 'A alone separates means chaperoned copper is needed' are not distinguishable from 'A and B differ because only B reaches the bulb.' The design needs the two missing cells to be interpretable: apo-GHK (peptide, no copper, poor penetration) and, ideally, a copper-loaded palmitoyl tripeptide. As drawn, the confound is baked in. Worse, the colour endpoint is differentially contaminated across arms: copper compounds tint white shafts, so Arms A and C carry a staining artefact that Arm B does not, biasing the very comparison the design exists to make. Shaft-band scoring on tagged hairs mitigates this and the author deserves credit for specifying proximal-band repigmentation on individually tagged hairs plus trichoscopic pigmented/unpigmented counts in matched 1 cm2 zones rather than global photographs, correctly grounded in PMID 38099887's point that only anagen can express a phenotype change and early anagen is most conducive — but it does not eliminate it, and blinding is impossible for coloured leave-on serums. No sample size, no power calculation, and no accounting for the fact that a 6-12 month hemi-scalp cosmetic pilot is powered for essentially nothing. Safety drops for Arm C: molar-matched cupric chloride leave-on has no cosmetic precedent, is a mild irritant, stains, and deposits redox-active copper onto a follicle carrying millimolar H2O2 with absent catalase (PMID 19237503) — the confinement to a small retroauricular zone with an irritation stop rule is the right mitigation but does not make it a benign arm. Two further problems: the chembl_id CHEMBL444436 is attached to what is a study design rather than a compound, which is at best sloppy; and the prior for the whole question is poor — PMID 41948976 delivered holoenzyme, not cofactor, and that is what reversed graying. Running a well-designed trial of a premise the corpus already argues against buys information, but at real cost. Revisable: add the apo-GHK cell, drop or radically de-risk Arm C, pre-specify power, and state explicitly that a null closes the topical-copper line for this programme.
Validation

No external validation recorded

46. copper tripeptide-1 (GHK-Cu) combined with palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 (Greyverse) as a sequential apo-enzyme-plus-metal pairing (H058) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL55643 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 5)

Mechanism
The two leading cosmetic strategies in this universe attack different terms of the same equation and neither addresses the other's limiting step. Palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 is an alpha-MSH biomimetic acting through MC1R-cAMP-MITF, and MITF's transcriptional output is tyrosinase, TRP-1 and TRP-2 PROTEIN. But newly translated tyrosinase is apo-enzyme: it is catalytically inert until ATP7A loads two copper ions into it in the trans-Golgi, which PMID 11092760 shows is both transporter-dependent and sensitive to the copper available to the cell. Driving MITF harder therefore raises apo-enzyme abundance and, if and only if copper handling is at all limiting, converts an enzyme-abundance problem into a metallation problem. GHK-Cu supplies the cytosolic copper pool feeding that step. The combination is thus mechanistically complementary rather than merely additive, and it makes a falsifiable prediction the single agents do not: GHK-Cu should show little or no effect alone (no extra apo-enzyme to load) but should potentiate palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20. If instead the copper arm adds nothing on top of the MC1R arm, copper supply is not limiting in the human bulb and the whole topical-copper line should be dropped.
Evidence
Metallation dependence: PMID 11092760. Enzyme identity and stabilisation by TYRP1/TYRP2: PMID 33995009. For the MC1R partner the evidence must be stated precisely and not inflated: PMID 30222197 is in vitro and ex vivo pharmacology — catalase activity and the roughly 30% intracellular H2O2 reduction come from human follicle DERMAL PAPILLA cells, not melanocytes, and its 15-volunteer arm measured MC1-R protein on plucked hairs at 3 months as target engagement, with no placebo and no colour endpoint; it is not a clinical efficacy trial. The only human colour-endpoint report, PMID 37034550, is n = 1, uncontrolled, and confounded by concurrent oral biotin and calcium pantothenate. PMID 33320376 supplies the canonical alpha-MSH/MC1R/m-MITF melanogenesis cascade that the peptide is designed to engage, and PMID 25219681 is a reminder that MC1R is highly polymorphic with variable functional consequence, so responders may be genotype-restricted. Counter-evidence that this pairing cannot dismiss: PMID 19237503 shows the tyrosinase in grey follicles is oxidatively damaged at Met374, a lesion neither more MITF nor more copper repairs, and PMID 41948976 shows the working intervention in human-skin models was delivery of active tyrosinase itself. (refs: PMID 11092760, PMID 33995009, PMID 28661582, PMID 30222197, PMID 37034550, PMID 33320376, PMID 25219681, PMID 19237503, PMID 41948976)
Safety
Both components are OTC cosmetic actives with benign topical profiles and no systemic exposure; the pairing adds no new class of hazard. Two formulation cautions are real: copper complexes are incompatible with reducing agents and with several peptide-serum preservatives, so the two should be applied sequentially from separate products rather than blended, and copper can tint white shafts, which must be washed out before any photographic assessment. A combination also forfeits attribution unless a factorial layout is used, which is why the recommended test is a four-zone hemi-scalp factorial (vehicle, GHK-Cu alone, palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 alone, both) rather than a two-arm comparison. Patch test both first.
Novelty rationale
H044 already pairs palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 with the N-acetyl tyrosine/zinc complex, but that pairing supplies SUBSTRATE plus zinc; zinc is not tyrosinase's catalytic metal — PMID 28661582 shows the binuclear zinc site belongs to TYRP1, which is precisely why TYRP1 lacks tyrosinase redox activity. This hypothesis corrects that metal mismatch by supplying copper, the actual tyrosinase cofactor, and it does so through a carrier peptide rather than an inorganic salt. Neither GHK-Cu nor any copper-delivery peptide appears anywhere in the 54 existing hypotheses. Both components are marketed cosmetic actives, so this is a novel combination rationale, not a novel molecule, and palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 is explicitly a product sold for the greying endpoint itself.
Reviews
  • plausibility 3/10, evidence 3/10, safety 7/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict reject. Two unproven actives stacked does not equal one supported hypothesis. The pairing rests on an apo-enzyme bottleneck — drive MITF harder with palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20, then feed ATP7A with GHK-Cu — but that bottleneck is conditional on copper handling being limiting in the human bulb, which is the same premise the corpus declines to support (PMID 19237503 oxidative Met374 sulfoxidation; PMID 32965076 and PMID 38099887 locating the defect at melanogenic output, melanosome transfer and melanocyte depletion; PMID 41948976 reversing graying with delivered tyrosinase holoenzyme rather than cofactor). The MC1R partner is no firmer, and to the author's real credit the hypothesis states this correctly rather than inflating it: PMID 30222197 is in vitro/ex vivo pharmacology whose catalase and ~30% H2O2 reduction come from dermal papilla cells, not melanocytes, with a 15-volunteer MC1-R protein biomarker arm on plucked hairs, no placebo and no colour endpoint — it is NOT a clinical efficacy trial, and this is the only entry in the round to say so plainly. PMID 37034550 is n = 1, uncontrolled and confounded by concurrent oral biotin and calcium pantothenate. PMID 25219681 is fairly invoked as a responder-restriction caveat given MC1R polymorphism. So the combination multiplies two unvalidated components and then proposes to detect their INTERACTION — 'GHK-Cu should do little alone but potentiate PTP20' — which is the single most sample-hungry quantity in the design space, typically requiring several times the n needed for a main effect. A four-zone hemi-scalp factorial in a cosmetic pilot cannot resolve that, and no sample size is offered. The unblindable copper tint on white shafts again contaminates a colour endpoint, and the formulation note (sequential application because copper complexes are incompatible with reducing agents and several peptide preservatives) means the two zones are not even application-matched. The correction to H044's zinc-vs-copper metal mismatch via PMID 28661582 is legitimate and well argued, but a correct chemistry footnote does not make a candidate. Safety and OTC access are fine; novelty is low, since both components are marketed cosmetic actives and palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 is already sold for this exact endpoint. Redundant with H057, which tests the same premise more cleanly.
Validation

No external validation recorded

47. roflumilast 0.3% topical cream/foam (scalp-directed PDE4 inhibition) (H059) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL193240 · Elo 1500 · confidence 3/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 5)

Mechanism
This is the run's first serious entry on the immune/inflammatory axis, and it is chosen because PDE4 inhibition is the one intervention that engages that axis and the melanogenic axis with the same molecular event. PDE4 degrades cAMP. cAMP is the second messenger downstream of MC1R that drives PKA-CREB-MITF and hence tyrosinase transcription — the same node the run's alpha-MSH biomimetics (H040, H041, H050, H051) try to reach from the receptor side. Blocking its degradation raises cAMP tone in follicular melanocytes independently of MC1R genotype, which matters because MC1R is highly polymorphic (PMID 25219681) and because PMID 33382172 shows MC1R is dispensable for acute stress-induced greying in mice, i.e. the receptor is not the only way in. Simultaneously, raised cAMP in myeloid and T cells suppresses TNF-alpha, IFN-gamma and IL-17 output — the cytokine tone that the immunotherapy observations implicate in reversible pigment suppression. One molecule, two arms of the lesion, and a formulation already engineered for scalp and hair-bearing skin.
Evidence
The immune-axis lead the run has never mined: PMID 28700789 describes 14 lung-cancer patients on anti-PD-1/PD-L1 who underwent diffuse hair repigmentation, and PMID 42387269 upgrades this from anecdote with a controlled prospective design in 29 thoracic-tumour patients — grayscale-quantified trichoscopy showed significant darkening in the immunotherapy group (p = 0.0008) but not the chemotherapy group (p = 0.1427), with a between-group effect size of Cohen's d = 0.90. PMID 29034554 records the same phenomenon. Two readings are possible and the corpus does not settle between them: either checkpoint blockade's immune activation directly remodels the follicular niche, or — the confound that must be stated, since 13 of 14 patients in PMID 28700789 had good clinical response — repigmentation tracks tumour regression and therefore the WITHDRAWAL of tumour-associated systemic inflammatory cytokine tone. The second reading is the one that generates a usable candidate, and it is corroborated by drug-level cases in which lowering TNF/IL-1/IL-6 signalling coincided with repigmentation: anti-TNF adalimumab (PMID 26158363) and the immunomodulatory imides lenalidomide and thalidomide (PMID 22440400, which explicitly proposes removal of IL-1/IL-6/TNF-alpha inhibition of follicular melanogenesis, and PMID 27444138), with PMID 37196305 showing lenalidomide directly augments differentiation of hair-follicle-derived melanocyte stem cells into functional melanocytes in culture — mechanism, not just temporal association. PMID 41855777 assembles the general model of an inflammatory microenvironment suppressing hair-follicle melanocyte stem cell function, and PMID 29723194 provides an independent genetic link between MITF and innate-immune gene regulation in which a viral mimic was sufficient to expose genetic susceptibility to hair greying. The cAMP-MITF leg is PMID 33320376. No PDE4 inhibitor has been tested for canities; no roflumilast greying data exist. (refs: PMID 28700789, PMID 42387269, PMID 29034554, PMID 26158363, PMID 22440400, PMID 27444138, PMID 37196305, PMID 41855777, PMID 29723194, PMID 33320376, PMID 25219681, PMID 33382172)
Safety
Unusually good fit for a cosmetic indication among immune-axis agents. Topical roflumilast is an approved once-daily cream/foam for plaque psoriasis and seborrhoeic dermatitis, including scalp application, so the vehicle problem is already solved and steroid-class hazards (atrophy, telangiectasia, tachyphylaxis) do not apply. Systemic exposure from scalp-limited application is low, but roflumilast is a systemically active drug and oral PDE4 inhibitors cause nausea, diarrhoea, weight loss and carry a psychiatric/mood-disturbance signal; anyone with a history of depression should be excluded even from topical use, and application should stay confined to a defined scalp area rather than whole-scalp daily indefinite use. It is prescription-only, which sits against this run's OTC-preference constraint and must count against it. Explicit statement on the parent observation: checkpoint inhibitors themselves (anti-PD-1/PD-L1) are NOT proposable candidates here at any dose or route — immune-related colitis, pneumonitis, hepatitis, hypophysitis and thyroiditis are unacceptable against a cosmetic endpoint, and they are cited only as the mechanism lead that motivates this hypothesis.
Novelty rationale
The immune/inflammatory axis is absent from all six of this run's themes and none of the 54 hypotheses cites PMID 28700789 or PMID 42387269. The nearest existing entry, H025 (ruxolitinib), is framed narrowly as JAK blockade in an autoimmune-overlap subset with vitiligo; this is a different pharmacology (cAMP rather than JAK-STAT), a different patient frame (no autoimmunity required), and it uniquely doubles as a pro-melanogenic MITF stimulus rather than merely removing an inhibitory signal. Roflumilast has no hair indication of any kind and no prior alopecia or canities trial, so this is a genuine repurposing proposal rather than a marketed anti-grey active.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 5/10, safety 4/10, novelty 8/10 — verdict revise. The most interesting entry in the round and the only genuine repurposing proposal — PDE4 inhibition reaching PKA-CREB-MITF from below the receptor is a real mechanistic idea, and it is correctly motivated by PMID 25219681 (MC1R highly polymorphic) and PMID 33382172 (MC1R dispensable for acute stress-induced greying in mice), which together argue that MC1R-agonist entries elsewhere in this run may be attacking a bypassed node. Explicit credit also for the hard line that anti-PD-1/PD-L1 agents are NOT proposable candidates at any dose or route — that is the correct call and the hypothesis does not drift. But the central logic is self-contradicting on direction. The hypothesis proposes an ANTI-inflammatory and justifies it by reading the immunotherapy repigmentation data as withdrawal of tumour-associated cytokine tone following response — yet its own strongest citation refutes that reading. PMID 42387269 is a controlled comparison in which the CHEMOTHERAPY arm, which also produces tumour regression, showed no significant darkening (p = 0.1427) while the immunotherapy arm did (p = 0.0008, d = 0.90); the whole point of that design was to attribute the effect to immune ACTIVATION rather than to tumour response. PMID 28700789's 13-of-14-responders confound is real and correctly flagged, but 42387269 is precisely the study that controls it, and it points the opposite way to the hypothesis's chosen interpretation. The axis is not uniformly directional either: PMID 42423614 shows dupilumab — an immunosuppressive Th2 blockade — producing new-onset vitiligo with GREY hair, i.e. cytokine suppression associated with pigment LOSS. So 'lower inflammatory tone equals more pigment' is not established, and the adalimumab (PMID 26158363), lenalidomide (PMID 22440400) and thalidomide (PMID 27444138) cases are single reports with post hoc mechanism; PMID 37196305 is the strongest of that set but it is cultured murine whisker MelSCs and lenalidomide is a cereblon modulator, so it does not bridge to PDE4 at all. PMID 41855777 is a vitiligo review, not a canities study, and PMID 29723194's poly(I:C) result actually shows innate immune ACTIVATION exposing greying susceptibility — again the opposite sign. Safety is the second problem: this is prescription-only, which directly violates this run's OTC/easily-obtainable constraint for a cosmetic indication with zero tolerance for systemic effects. Approved topical scalp vehicles do solve formulation and there is no steroid atrophy risk, both real advantages, but roflumilast is systemically active with a long-lived N-oxide metabolite, and the class carries nausea, diarrhoea, weight loss and a psychiatric/mood signal; excluding anyone with depression history from a HAIR COLOUR product is itself an argument that the risk-benefit is wrong. Salvageable, not acceptable as written: pick a direction and defend it against 42387269 and 42423614, drop the tumour-regression framing or justify it, and either argue the cAMP-MITF leg standalone (which needs no immune story at all and is the stronger case) or find an OTC-accessible cAMP-elevating agent instead.
Validation

No external validation recorded

48. pentoxifylline 5% compounded topical scalp lotion (oral 400 mg tid as the fallback route) (H060) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL628 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 5)

Mechanism
Pentoxifylline is a non-selective phosphodiesterase inhibitor whose best-characterised clinical action is suppression of TNF-alpha production, with additional reduction of IL-1 and IL-6 and improvement of microcirculatory flow through increased erythrocyte deformability. It engages exactly the cytokine set that PMID 22440400 nominated as the inhibitory brake on follicular melanogenesis when explaining lenalidomide-associated repigmentation, and it does so through a mechanism with fifty years of clinical use rather than a teratogenic immunomodulatory imide. The PDE leg gives the same cAMP-PKA-CREB-MITF support argued for roflumilast, though less potently and less selectively, and the haemorheological leg is independently relevant because the anagen bulb is a high-metabolic-demand structure whose pigmentary unit is the first function to fail under stress. The claim is that lowering chronic perifollicular TNF/IL-1/IL-6 tone lifts a reversible suppression of melanogenesis in follicles that still retain melanocyte stem cells — which PMID 38099887 identifies as the window in which greying is still reversible, since irreversibility arrives only once bulge McSCs are also depleted.
Evidence
Same immune-axis lead as the PDE4 entry and the same honest reading of it: PMID 42387269's controlled comparison (immunotherapy vs chemotherapy, grayscale trichoscopy, p = 0.0065 between groups, d = 0.90) establishes that repigmentation tracks immunotherapy specifically, while PMID 28700789's 14-patient series is confounded by 13 of 14 patients having good tumour response, making relief of tumour-driven systemic inflammation a live alternative explanation and the one this candidate targets. Cytokine-lowering drugs are the repigmentation cases with the most direct mechanistic reading: PMID 22440400 (lenalidomide, hypothesis explicitly framed around IL-1/IL-6/TNF-alpha), PMID 27444138 (thalidomide), PMID 26158363 (adalimumab, TNF blockade), with PMID 37196305 giving the cell-level counterpart that lenalidomide augments hair-follicle melanocyte stem cell differentiation into functional melanocytes. PMID 28918974 supplies the base-rate correction that keeps this honest: across 8052 patients in 36 trials of targeted anticancer agents, all-grade HAIR pigmentary changes ran at 21.5%, so drug-associated hair colour change is common, direction is variable, and single case reports are weak evidence. PMID 41855777 gives the general inflammatory-microenvironment-suppresses-McSC model. PMID 38099887 constrains the achievable endpoint to follicles retaining bulge McSCs and to the anagen window. There is no canities study of pentoxifylline and no topical follicular pharmacokinetic data. (refs: PMID 42387269, PMID 28700789, PMID 22440400, PMID 27444138, PMID 26158363, PMID 37196305, PMID 28918974, PMID 41855777, PMID 38099887)
Safety
Oral pentoxifylline is an old, generic, broadly benign drug (main issues: dose-dependent nausea, dyspepsia, dizziness; caution with anticoagulants and in bleeding risk given the platelet effect; avoid in recent cerebral/retinal haemorrhage) but a cosmetic indication has near-zero tolerance for systemic exposure, so the topical route is the primary proposal and the oral route is a fallback only if a topical arm shows nothing and the individual accepts systemic risk knowingly. Topical pentoxifylline is compoundable at 5% in standard lotion bases and has been used experimentally on skin; percutaneous absorption is real but modest at scalp-limited doses. It is prescription/compounded, not OTC, which counts against it under this run's access constraint. Restating the boundary: the anti-PD-1/PD-L1 agents that generated this mechanism lead are not candidates and should not be proposed — their immune-related adverse-event profile is categorically incompatible with a cosmetic endpoint.
Novelty rationale
Pentoxifylline has no hair indication and does not appear among the 54 hypotheses; the cytokine axis it targets appears in none of them either. It is deliberately positioned as the deployable surrogate for the IMiD signal (lenalidomide/thalidomide) which is the strongest drug-level repigmentation evidence in the corpus but is undeployable for a cosmetic indication because of teratogenicity and REMS-level controls — those two drugs are named here as mechanism anchors and are explicitly NOT being proposed. Distinct from roflumilast in the same output: non-selective PDE rather than PDE4, a TNF-suppression rather than cAMP-first rationale, plus a microcirculatory component, and it is a cheap generic. Lower confidence than roflumilast because it lacks a scalp-validated topical vehicle.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 4/10, safety 6/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict revise. Citations check out (22440400 really does nominate IL-1/IL-6/TNF-alpha; 42387269 really does report p=0.0065, d=0.90; 28918974 really does report 21.5% all-grade hair pigmentary change), and the self-flagging of the 28700789 tumour-response confound is exactly the honesty this stage should reward. The problem is that none of the evidence is about pentoxifylline. The chain is: one lenalidomide case report whose authors SPECULATED about cytokines (22440400 offers no cytokine measurement at all, and in the same breath offers a competing hypophyseal-adrenal explanation and a competing melanocyte-migration explanation), plus a thalidomide case (27444138) and a single adalimumab case (26158363) in a drug class far more often associated with alopecia and paradoxical inflammatory skin events than with pigment gain. Against a 21.5% background rate of pigmentary change on targeted agents, three case reports is close to noise, and the hypothesis cites that base rate without letting it discount its own anchors. Mechanistically there are three legs and two of them break under the proposed route. The haemorheological leg (erythrocyte deformability, microcirculatory flow) is a systemic plasma-level effect and is simply unavailable to a topical scalp lotion; it should be deleted, not offered as independent support. The PDE leg is weak: pentoxifylline is a low-potency non-selective PDE inhibitor at micromolar concentrations, so invoking cAMP-PKA-CREB-MITF here is borrowing roflumilast's argument at a fraction of the potency and none of the selectivity. That leaves TNF suppression alone, and TNF suppression in skin is achieved at tissue concentrations no one has demonstrated for a compounded 5% lotion, in the absence of any follicular penetration or bulb-concentration data, which the hypothesis concedes. There is also an unresolved directional contradiction the write-up papers over: checkpoint blockade ACTIVATES immunity and is the strongest repigmentation signal in the corpus, while this candidate proposes the opposite intervention. The 'relief of tumour-driven systemic inflammation' reconciliation is post hoc and, as written, unfalsifiable by any topical scalp experiment. Credit where due: the anti-PD-1/PD-L1 boundary is restated explicitly and no checkpoint inhibitor is proposed. Revise: drop the oral 400 mg tid fallback entirely (systemic exposure for a cosmetic endpoint is indefensible and the run's constraint forbids it), drop the rheology leg, and gate any trial on a prior scalp-biopsy or tape-strip demonstration that topical pentoxifylline reaches the bulb and lowers local TNF. Also note the compounded-prescription status is a direct violation of the run's OTC/easy-access preference and needs justifying rather than merely conceding.
Validation

No external validation recorded

49. cromolyn sodium 4% topical scalp solution (perifollicular mast-cell stabilisation) (H061) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL74 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 5)

Mechanism
Perifollicular mast cells sit directly against the hair follicle's connective-tissue sheath and are the resident source of histamine, tryptase, TNF-alpha and a set of proteases that remodel the follicular microenvironment. They and melanocytes are, uniquely, governed by the same master regulatory system: KIT and KIT ligand control survival, migration, proliferation and activation in both lineages, so the perifollicular mast cell is not a bystander in the pigmentary niche but a co-tenant competing for and responding to the same ligand. Cromolyn is a mast-cell stabiliser that blocks degranulation without immunosuppression. The hypothesis is that damping chronic perifollicular degranulation lowers the local inflammatory mediator load on the bulb, protecting melanogenesis and the KIT-ligand-dependent McSC niche during the anagen window when the pigmentary unit is rebuilt. This is a niche-protection claim, not a repigmentation claim.
Evidence
PMID 38711220 is the anchor: it reviews KIT ligand/KIT as the shared master regulator of melanocytes and mast cells, notes that blockade of the pathway depletes cells while overactivation produces mastocytosis or melanoma, and explicitly nominates hair greying alongside vitiligo, melasma, urticaria and mastocytosis as targets for therapeutic modulation of this system. PMID 41053225 makes the niche leg concrete and quantitative in mice: melanocyte stem cell fate under stress is governed by niche-derived KIT ligand, and conditions that raise arachidonic acid metabolism and niche KIT ligand suppress seno-differentiation and preserve McSC self-renewal — meaning the mediator environment around the follicle, not the melanocyte alone, decides greying. Independent support that mast-cell mediators are on-pathway for greying specifically comes from PMID 41613336, a proteoglycan-based topical developed for hair greying which used reduced mast-cell histamine release as one of its three primary readouts alongside restored MITF expression under Wnt/beta-catenin inhibition and antioxidant capacity — i.e. a formulator working on this exact indication already treated mast-cell degranulation as a relevant endpoint. PMID 41855777 supplies the broader inflammatory-microenvironment-suppresses-follicular-McSC model. Important limitation stated plainly: mast cells are a KIT ligand TARGET, not its main source, so stabilising them does not raise niche KIT ligand and the mechanism runs only through mediator load; and PMID 41053225's finding that McSC preservation and melanoma risk are two faces of the same niche signalling (echoed by PMID 41518577's framing of greying as an evolutionary anti-malignancy checkpoint) is a caution against pushing this niche in the pro-survival direction too hard. No greying trial of cromolyn exists. (refs: PMID 38711220, PMID 41053225, PMID 41613336, PMID 41855777, PMID 41518577, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Among the best safety profiles in the entire candidate universe: cromolyn is not absorbed to any meaningful degree from mucosal or topical application, has no systemic immunosuppressive action, no steroid-class local hazards, and is available OTC as a nasal/ophthalmic solution in most countries — a consumer could realistically apply a 4% solution to the scalp, though this is off-label improvisation of a product not formulated for scalp use, and the aqueous vehicle penetrates poorly, which is the main reason to expect a null. Worst realistic harm is transient stinging or contact dermatitis. Its poor skin penetration is a genuine and possibly fatal objection to the hypothesis and is the first thing a trial would discover; a penetration-enhanced or microneedle-assisted vehicle would be needed for a fair test.
Novelty rationale
Mast cells, KIT ligand and the perifollicular immune niche appear in none of the 54 existing hypotheses; the run's only immune-adjacent entries are H025 (ruxolitinib, JAK/IFN-gamma in autoimmune overlap) and H045 (tacrolimus, explicitly reframed away from immunosuppression toward melanoblast mobilisation). Cromolyn has never been proposed for any hair indication. The proposal is also honest about its ceiling: it is a prevention/niche-protection hypothesis with a serious penetration problem, not a repigmentation agent, which is why the confidence is 2 despite the excellent safety profile.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 4/10, safety 9/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict revise. The safety and novelty case is the best in this set and is fairly stated: cromolyn is essentially non-absorbed, non-immunosuppressive, OTC-adjacent, and the worst realistic outcome is stinging. The mechanism, however, contains an inversion the write-up does not catch, and it is the weakest link. PMID 41053225 is cited as the niche leg, but read carefully it says the opposite of what a mast-cell stabiliser would do: McSC self-renewal was PRESERVED by activating arachidonic acid metabolism and niche-derived KIT ligand, while the greying arm was seno-differentiation. Perifollicular mast cells are a principal local source of arachidonic acid metabolites (PGD2, leukotrienes). Blocking their degranulation therefore plausibly LOWERS the very arachidonic-acid signal that 41053225 associates with McSC retention. The hypothesis correctly flags that mast cells are a KIT ligand target rather than source, and correctly flags the melanoma-risk tension, but it does not confront the possibility that its intervention is directionally pro-greying on its own cited evidence. PMID 38711220 does verify: it is a review that explicitly lists hair greying among targets for KIT ligand modulation. But a review naming greying as a candidate indication is not evidence that mast cells contribute to greying, and it proposes modulating KIT ligand, not stabilising mast cells. PMID 41613336 is the thinnest support of all: it is a manufacturer-linked in vitro study of a proteoglycan cosmetic in which mast-cell histamine release was one assay in a panel run in a mast cell line, with no hair follicle, no perifollicular mast cell, and no link from that readout to the MITF result obtained in a separate melanocyte culture. Treating a formulator's assay panel as independent confirmation that the mediator is on-pathway is over-reading. Second fatal-in-practice objection, already half-conceded: cromolyn is a highly polar disodium dicarboxylate with negligible stratum corneum penetration; an aqueous 4% ophthalmic/nasal solution improvised onto scalp will not reach the perifollicular dermis, so a null result would be uninterpretable and the trial would teach nothing about the hypothesis. Revise: state the arachidonic-acid directional risk explicitly and pre-specify which way a null or a worsening would be read; specify a penetration-enhanced vehicle or microneedle delivery with a demonstrated dermal concentration; and pick a falsifiable proximal readout (perifollicular tryptase or histamine in biopsy) rather than pigment, since a niche-protection claim cannot be tested by looking at hair colour over a cosmetic timescale.
Validation

No external validation recorded

50. colchicine 0.5-1% topical scalp gel, small concealed test zone (NLRP3-inflammasome/gasdermin-directed) (H062) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL107 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 5)

Mechanism
Colchicine binds tubulin and destabilises microtubules, and the consequence that matters here is that NLRP3 inflammasome assembly and the subsequent caspase-1-mediated cleavage of gasdermin are microtubule-dependent; low-dose colchicine is the standard clinical tool for suppressing that pathway. The relevance is specific rather than generic anti-inflammatory hand-waving: gasdermin A3, the executioner pore protein of pyroptosis, has been reported to DRIVE hair graying by causing ectopic melanocyte stem cell differentiation and migration — that is, the exact cellular lesion (ectopic differentiation, loss of the ability to return to quiescence) that the run's central McSC model already identifies as the point of no return. If inflammasome-to-gasdermin signalling is upstream of ectopic McSC differentiation in human follicles, then microtubule-dependent inflammasome assembly is a druggable node above it, and colchicine is the only agent in this universe that reaches it.
Evidence
PMID 42536303 is the anchor and the reason this hypothesis exists: Gasdermin A3 drives hair graying via ectopic melanocyte stem cell differentiation and migration. PMID 33598985 establishes the corresponding human-tissue lesion — ex vivo human hair follicles exposed to H2O2, ionising radiation or noradrenaline show ectopic pigmentation of bulge MelSCs followed by their depletion, a state from which return to quiescence appears lost — and PMID 36853923 frames bulge McSC loss as a main cause of greying. PMID 29723194 supplies an independent innate-immunity link, showing MITF regulates systemic innate immune gene expression and that a viral mimic, poly(I:C), was sufficient to expose genetic susceptibility to hair greying in mice — direct evidence that innate immune activation alone can precipitate greying in a susceptible background, which is what makes an innate-immune (rather than adaptive/T-cell) target coherent. PMID 41855777 gives the inflammatory-niche-suppresses-McSC model. The honest weaknesses are large and I will name them: GSDMA3 is a murine gene whose human counterpart's role in the follicle is not established, the pathway is inferred rather than demonstrated in human bulge tissue, colchicine's inflammasome effect is documented systemically rather than in follicles, and no dose-response for a topical scalp preparation exists anywhere. (refs: PMID 42536303, PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923, PMID 29723194, PMID 41855777)
Safety
This is the least cosmetically comfortable proposal in this set and I am not softening that. Oral colchicine has a narrow therapeutic index with severe GI toxicity, myelosuppression at overdose, and dangerous interactions with CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitors including clarithromycin, ciclosporin and statins — oral use for a cosmetic endpoint is not defensible and is not being proposed. Topical colchicine gels do exist clinically (actinic keratosis, verrucae) at similar concentrations and produce local irritation and erosion by design; percutaneous absorption from a small area is low but not zero, and colchicine is an antimitotic, so applying it over a hair-bearing region risks impairing the rapidly dividing matrix keratinocytes and causing anagen effluvium — hair loss is a far worse cosmetic outcome than grey hair. Any test must therefore be restricted to a small concealed retroauricular zone, time-limited, with immediate withdrawal on shedding or erosion, and it is not suitable for consumer self-experimentation. Absolutely contraindicated in pregnancy and in anyone on interacting drugs.
Novelty rationale
Innate immunity, the inflammasome and pyroptosis appear nowhere in the 54 hypotheses, and colchicine has no hair indication of any kind. It is the only candidate in the universe that plausibly reaches the gasdermin pathway that PMID 42536303 implicates directly in the run's own central mechanism, which is why it earns a slot despite the safety profile; it is proposed as a mechanistic probe of the inflammasome hypothesis rather than as a product. Confidence is held at 2, and would be 1 on deployability alone.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 2/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict reject. Reject, and the safety argument alone is sufficient. Colchicine's entire pharmacology is microtubule destabilisation; the anagen hair matrix is one of the fastest-proliferating tissues in the body and colchicine-class antimitotics are a textbook cause of anagen effluvium. This hypothesis proposes applying an antimitotic at 0.5-1% to hair-bearing scalp in order to improve a cosmetic pigment endpoint, where the most likely pharmacologically-predicted outcome is not grey hair but no hair. The write-up names this risk honestly, then proceeds anyway with a retroauricular test zone; naming a disqualifying risk does not neutralise it, and 'stop on shedding' is not a mitigation when shedding is delayed by weeks and the affected follicles have already been hit. Add the class irritancy (topical colchicine gels are used for actinic keratosis precisely because they erode), the narrow therapeutic index, the CYP3A4/P-gp interaction landscape, and an absolute contraindication in pregnancy, and this fails the run's stated zero-tolerance standard for a cosmetic indication several times over. The evidence base is also thinner than the confident mechanistic framing implies. PMID 42536303 is the sole anchor and is a title-only record in this corpus with no abstract available to verify what was actually shown; the claim rests on the title. GSDMA3 is a murine paralogue with no one-to-one human ortholog (human GSDMA is the nearest relative and its follicular role is unestablished), so the entire causal chain sits on mouse-specific gene biology in a run whose own dossier anchor (PMID 38099887) explicitly warns against directly transposing mouse graying concepts to humans. PMID 33598985 verifies but shows only that H2O2, ionising radiation and noradrenaline induce ectopic bulge MelSC pigmentation in ex vivo human follicles; it says nothing about inflammasomes, caspase-1 or gasdermin, so it establishes the lesion, not the proposed upstream node. PMID 29723194 verifies and is genuinely interesting (poly(I:C) exposing greying susceptibility in Mitf heterozygotes) but it is mouse genetics with a strong sensitising background, not evidence that NLRP3 drives human canities. Nothing anywhere connects colchicine to a follicle. The result is a three-inference stack (murine gasdermin -> human inflammasome -> microtubule-dependent assembly in the bulge) supporting a drug whose predicted on-target side effect is worse than the disease. If the inflammasome axis is worth probing, probe it with a genetic or antibody tool in ex vivo human follicles using the 33598985 assay, and keep colchicine out of any consumer-facing proposal.
Validation

No external validation recorded

51. betamethasone valerate 0.1% scalp application, pulsed and gated on trichoscopic evidence of perifollicular inflammation (H063) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL1497 · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 5)

Mechanism
A distinct immune-axis proposition: that a minority of premature-greying scalps carry a subclinical perifollicular lymphocytic component which reversibly suppresses the pigmentary unit, and that in this gated subset a short pulsed topical corticosteroid can lift it. The clearest natural experiment is alopecia areata, where the follicle's immune privilege collapses and regrowing hair characteristically returns WHITE, then converts to pigmented as the infiltrate is treated — showing that an immune attack can silence melanogenesis in follicles whose melanocyte stem cells are still present and recoverable. Corticosteroids suppress the IFN-gamma/IL-15-driven CD8 cytotoxic programme shared by alopecia areata and vitiligo, and they are the incumbent first-line topical for restoring follicular immune privilege. The claim is deliberately narrow: this is not a treatment for constitutional age-related greying, in which no infiltrate exists and a steroid can only cause harm; it is a treatment for an inflammatory phenocopy of it, and the gating criterion is the whole hypothesis.
Evidence
PMID 38500297 reports two cases of persistent white hair regrowth after alopecia areata subsequently turning pigmented following treatment — the cleanest demonstration in the corpus that immune-mediated pigment silencing is reversible in follicles retaining McSCs. PMID 39258892 sets out the shared IFN-gamma/IL-15/CD8 pathogenesis of alopecia areata and vitiligo, why both respond to corticosteroids and kinase inhibitors, and how bulge hair-follicle stem cells and hair-germ melanocyte stem cells jointly determine whether regrowing terminal hair is pigmented. PMID 41855777 describes IL-15-supported tissue-resident memory T cells and inflammatory microenvironments suppressing hair-follicle melanocyte stem cell function and thereby destabilising repigmentation. PMID 38099887 defines the biological window: greying becomes irreversible only once bulge McSCs are also depleted, and only during anagen can a growing hair's phenotype change — so this is worth attempting only early. The immunotherapy observations (PMID 42387269 controlled trichoscopy, PMID 28700789 case series) motivate the axis but point in a confusing direction for a steroid, since checkpoint blockade ACTIVATES immunity while repigmenting hair; that tension is the main argument against this hypothesis and I am not resolving it — it is why the proposal is gated rather than general. PMID 42423614 is a further caution in the same direction: dupilumab, an immunomodulator, has been associated with new-onset vitiligo accompanied by grey hair, so immunomodulation is not uniformly pro-pigment. (refs: PMID 38500297, PMID 39258892, PMID 41855777, PMID 38099887, PMID 42387269, PMID 28700789, PMID 42423614)
Safety
The weakest safety case in this set for routine cosmetic use, and it should not be a consumer product. Class hazards on scalp skin are well known: atrophy, telangiectasia, folliculitis, tachyphylaxis, and hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal suppression with large-area chronic use. It is prescription-only, contrary to this run's OTC preference. The only defensible framing is a physician-supervised, time-limited pulse (for example twice daily for 2 weeks, then stop and reassess) restricted to a defined zone, in individuals with objective trichoscopic or histological evidence of perifollicular inflammation, with a hard stop if no change by 6 months. Applying potent topical steroids to a normal scalp for constitutional greying would be net-harmful and must be stated as such. Restating the constraint on the parent observation: anti-PD-1/PD-L1 checkpoint inhibitors are not candidates for this indication under any circumstances.
Novelty rationale
New in this run as a gated inflammatory-phenocopy hypothesis rather than a general treatment; nothing among the 54 raises follicular immune privilege, the alopecia-areata white-regrowth observation, or trichoscopic gating on perifollicular inflammation. It is honestly the least novel pharmacology here — topical corticosteroids are the oldest tool in dermatology — and its value is diagnostic stratification (identifying an immune-driven subset that all the run's oxidative and melanogenic hypotheses would misclassify) rather than the drug itself. Confidence 2, and the low mark reflects that the gated subset may be small or may not exist in premature canities at all.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 4/10, safety 3/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict reject. The underlying observation is real and the intellectual honesty is high (the checkpoint-inhibitor directional tension is stated and left unresolved rather than argued away, and the dupilumab counterexample in PMID 42423614 is correctly reported as a case of new-onset vitiligo WITH grey hair, i.e. immunomodulation is not uniformly pro-pigment). But the drug does not follow from the evidence. PMID 38500297 verifies and is the load-bearing citation, and it does not support topical betamethasone: both patients received SYSTEMIC therapy (oral methylprednisolone plus compound glycyrrhizin/glycyrrhizin, over 2 and 3 years respectively), n=2, uncontrolled, with no histology, no trichoscopy, and no control for the well-known spontaneous repigmentation of initially-white AA regrowth over successive cycles. Reading a 2-case systemic-steroid-plus-glycyrrhizin series as a rationale for a pulsed potent topical corticosteroid is a genuine drug-to-evidence mismatch, not a minor stretch. PMID 39258892 verifies as a review of shared IFN-gamma/IL-15/CD8 pathogenesis in AA and vitiligo, but it is about AA and vitiligo, not canities; PMID 41855777 is likewise a vitiligo review whose hair-follicle McSC content is one clause about IL-15/TRM. So the whole immune-phenocopy construct is an extrapolation from two autoimmune diseases to premature greying, and the hypothesis's own gating criterion concedes the subset may not exist. There is no citation anywhere establishing that subclinical perifollicular lymphocytic infiltrate is a describable entity in premature canities, which means the gate cannot currently be operationalised: a trichoscopic sign with no validated definition, no prevalence estimate and no histological correlate is not a stratification tool. Second, this is indication drift. If a patient has a perifollicular lymphocytic infiltrate silencing pigment, that patient has alopecia areata or an AA-spectrum disorder and should be diagnosed and treated as such by a dermatologist under an existing standard of care, not enrolled in a canities repurposing programme. Nothing is being repurposed here. Third, novelty is genuinely low: potent topical corticosteroids on scalp are the oldest intervention in dermatology and the incumbent first-line for exactly the condition the natural experiment comes from. Fourth, safety is the worst fit in the set for the stated constraint: prescription-only, class hazards of atrophy, telangiectasia, steroid folliculitis, tachyphylaxis and HPA suppression, applied to a visible cosmetic site, for a subset that may be empty; the write-up itself concedes application to a normal scalp would be net-harmful. Reject the drug. The one thing worth salvaging and moving elsewhere is the diagnostic point: an inflammatory-phenocopy screen (new/rapid onset, patchy distribution, perifollicular signs, AA history) should be a standard exclusion criterion applied across the run's oxidative and melanogenic hypotheses so those candidates are not tested in a misclassified population. Keep the gate, drop the betamethasone. Noted with approval that no checkpoint inhibitor is proposed and the boundary is restated.
Validation

No external validation recorded

52. microneedling (home dermaroller / motorised pen, 0.5-1.0 mm, monthly, single concealed scalp test zone) as a STANDALONE injury-induced melanocyte-stem-cell mobilisation intervention (H068) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 3/5 · route: procedure - home-performed mechanical micro-injury (dermaroller or motorised pen, 0.5-1.0 mm depth, monthly, one concealed scalp test zone with an untreated contralateral control zone) · origin: generation (round 6)

Mechanism
Not a molecule and not a penetration trick: the claim is that controlled dermal micro-injury is itself the active. Repigmentation in vitiligo is anatomically follicular - melanocyte stem cells (MelSCs) resident in the bulge/outer root sheath are the reservoir, and repigmentation proceeds as perifollicular islands as bulge MelSCs differentiate and migrate upward (PMID 33320376). Wounding is a known physiological trigger for that reservoir: microneedling creates thousands of self-sealing columnar micro-injuries that release a wound-healing cascade (growth-factor release, transient ROS, immune-cell recruitment, increased perifollicular perfusion), which activates follicle stem cells and pigment-related gene programmes (PMID 40627439). A mechanistically parallel result is that a purely physical stimulus - pulsed ultrasound - drives melanoblast migration via autocrine M-CSF/FAK signalling with no drug involved (PMID 23725022), which is direct evidence that mechanical/physical input alone can mobilise the pigment lineage. In premature canities the deficit is MelSC exhaustion and mis-scheduled differentiation rather than absent follicles (PMIDs 36853923, 33598985), so the target here is the marginal grey-but-not-white follicle whose bulge reservoir is depleted but not extinct. Prediction is testable and specific: new anagen shafts emerging from treated follicles carry a pigmented proximal band, i.e. hair colour changes at the root, not along the existing shaft.
Evidence
The strongest single datum is PMID 38470079: a double-blind RCT in which microneedling plus 5% N-acetylcysteine was compared with microneedling plus distilled water, and BOTH arms improved significantly (p < 0.01) with no significant difference between them. The intended conclusion was about NAC; the interesting conclusion is that the sham-drug control arm - microneedling with water - produced repigmentation. That is the closest thing in this corpus to microneedling-as-monotherapy evidence, and it is a control arm rather than a designed monotherapy trial, which is exactly how much weight it should carry. PMID 42186640 (scoping review, 14 studies) concludes microneedling helps mainly ADJUNCTIVELY, with benefit when combined with corticosteroids, PRP or NB-UVB, and explicitly attributes effect to both enhanced drug absorption and stimulation of local regenerative pathways - it does not establish a standalone effect. The honest negative is PMID 36419200: a within-patient split-body study of NB-UVB plus microneedling versus NB-UVB alone found no significant between-arm difference (p = 0.17), i.e. adding microneedling to an already-working device bought nothing measurable. There is ZERO canities data: every study cited is vitiligo or alopecia, and grey hair is not vitiligo - it has no autoimmune melanocyte destruction to interrupt. The confounder that must be pre-declared is spontaneous repigmentation of grey hair, which is documented and uncontrolled (PMID 23974581); this is why practitioner anecdote about needle procedures darkening hair is uninterpretable without a contemporaneous untreated zone. Design that answers this: two matched 2 cm mid-parietal zones at least 5 cm apart, one rolled monthly and one untouched, 50 hairs cut from each at 6 and 9 months and scored blind for a pigmented proximal segment beneath an unpigmented distal shaft. Each hair is its own before/after control. (refs: PMID 38470079, PMID 42186640, PMID 36419200, PMID 40627439, PMID 23725022, PMID 33320376, PMID 36853923, PMID 33598985, PMID 23974581, PMID 25056010)
Safety
Modality and setting: home-usable, single-user device; 0.5-1.0 mm needle length (long enough to reach papillary/upper reticular dermis and the bulge region, short enough to avoid bleeding-heavy injury); monthly, not weekly, since the wound-response window is the active and repeated over-treatment risks fibrosis. Alcohol-disinfect the head before and after each use, never share, discard rollers on schedule - the realistic harms are bacterial folliculitis, pustules and, with excessive depth or aggressive technique, scarring or track-mark hyperpigmentation. Two specific cautions: (1) do not needle over inflamed, infected or actively shedding scalp; (2) Koebnerisation - a case of vitiligo induced at a site of laser epilation trauma is on record (PMID 25056010), so anyone with personal or family history of vitiligo should not do this, because the same injury that might mobilise MelSCs can precipitate depigmentation in a susceptible person. No systemic exposure, no prescriber, total cost of a device is trivial - this is the best access fit in the entire candidate universe for this run's OTC constraint.
Novelty rationale
Across 67 hypotheses and five rounds this run has proposed nothing that is not swallowed or applied - not one procedure or device. That is a structural blind spot, not a considered conclusion, and this hypothesis exists to close it. Microneedling has an established hair-related use (alopecia areata and AGA adjunct, vitiligo adjunct), so it is NOT novel as a procedure; what is novel is treating it as the active agent for a PIGMENT endpoint rather than as a delivery enhancer or a growth adjunct, and applying it to canities, where nobody has tested it. The mechanistic bet is also different in kind from every prior hypothesis here: the existing 67 all try to supply something the follicle lacks (a substrate, a cofactor, a scavenger, a receptor agonist). This one supplies no molecule at all and instead attempts to reschedule a stem-cell compartment by injury. If it works, none of the pharmacology arguments in this run were necessary; if it fails, that is informative about whether the residual MelSC pool in a greying scalp is mobilisable at all.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 4/10, safety 4/10, novelty 6/10 — verdict revise. Genuinely the freshest idea in the run - it is the only non-molecular candidate and the observation that 67 prior hypotheses all try to supply something the follicle lacks is a fair structural criticism. Citation handling is accurate: PMID 38470079 really did use microneedling-plus-distilled-water as the control and both arms improved with no between-arm difference; PMID 42186640 really does conclude adjunctive-only; PMID 36419200 really does report p = 0.17 for adding microneedling to NB-UVB; PMID 23974581 really does note occasional spontaneous repigmentation. Nothing is invented. But the evidence is read too charitably at the single point everything rests on. PMID 38470079 has no untreated control - both arms were needled, so 'microneedling with water produced repigmentation' is not what the trial shows; it shows two needled arms improved over baseline in an uncontrolled-for-time comparison, in an unblinded-to-procedure setting, scored partly by patient feedback, in 20 patients with active-treatment expectation. Regression to the mean, seasonal light exposure and spontaneous repigmentation of vitiligo are all unexcluded.
