Set beside PMID 36419200, where microneedling added to a working modality bought nothing measurable (p = 0.17), the honest summary is that there is no evidence microneedling repigments anything on its own. Second: the species/disease jump is bigger than admitted. Vitiligo repigmentation works because a surviving bulge MelSC reservoir is released from ongoing autoimmune destruction; the hypothesis concedes grey hair has no autoimmunity to interrupt, but does not follow that through - in canities the reservoir is the thing that is exhausted, so 'mobilise the reservoir' is asking an empty compartment to fire. Third, PMID 23725022 (pulsed ultrasound, melanoblast migration via M-CSF/FAK) is a cell-culture migration assay in melanoblasts and is doing more rhetorical work than it can bear as 'direct evidence that mechanical input alone can mobilise the pigment lineage'. Fourth, this is an unsupervised home procedure in a cosmetic indication whose documented adverse outcome is the very symptom being treated (see contra_evidence) - that combination, not the folliculitis risk, is what drives the safety score down.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run outside the bundle, and this one returns documented opposite-direction evidence. (a) 'microneedling adverse effects post-inflammatory hypopigmentation leukotrichia trauma-induced vitiligo Koebner scalp' - Koebnerisation was reported in 20-50% of vitiligo patients treated with microneedling in the reviewed series (another series found none), i.e. the procedure itself precipitated depigmentation at treated sites in a substantial minority; post-inflammatory hypopigmentation after microneedling is described as common and may last months; iatrogenic scarring occurs with dragging, blunt needles or excessive depth. Active Koebner phenomenon is a standard exclusion criterion in microneedling trials precisely because of this. The hypothesis cites one laser-epilation Koebner case (PMID 25056010); the actual literature is far heavier than that single anecdote and the safety notes understate it by an order of magnitude. (b) 'hair regrows white after trauma poliosis leukotrichia scar injury' - acquired poliosis circumscripta following physical trauma to the scalp, including burns and surgical procedures, is documented: trauma disrupts follicular melanocytes and the hair regrows unpigmented. That is a documented mechanism by which the proposed intervention produces precisely the outcome it is meant to prevent, in the exact target tissue, from the exact insult being applied. (c) Commercial/actual use check: microneedling is sold and used for collagen induction, scar and acne-scar remodelling, AGA/alopecia adjunct and transdermal delivery enhancement - not as a pigment treatment; where it is used for pigment, it is an adjunct to a working drug or device, never a monotherapy. (d) Contra-search on the mechanism itself: PMID 36419200 within the corpus is a negative controlled result for microneedling adding anything to an active comparator. Overall: injury-induced depigmentation of scalp hair is documented, dose-dependent on depth and technique, and this hypothesis proposes unsupervised monthly injury at home.
Validation

No external validation recorded

53. microneedling as a DELIVERY procedure paired with the run's incumbent leave-on active (palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 / Greyverse 2%), tested as a factorial against needling alone and peptide alone (H069) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 3/5 · route: procedure plus topical - monthly home microneedling (0.5-1.0 mm) immediately before application of a 2% leave-on peptide solution, with daily leave-on application continued between sessions; four-cell within-scalp factorial · origin: generation (round 6)

Mechanism
Every topical hypothesis surviving in this run - the MC1R peptides, GHK-Cu, L-methionine, melatonin - shares one unresolved failure mode that no reviewer has been able to close: nobody has shown the molecule reaches the anagen hair bulb at a concentration that does anything. The bulb sits 3-4 mm deep; a leave-on scalp lotion depends on follicular-infundibular partitioning, and the run has no follicular pharmacokinetic data for any of its candidates. Microneedling addresses that specific problem physically rather than by reformulating: transient aqueous micro-channels through the stratum corneum, made immediately before application, are the mechanism by which microneedling potentiates topicals in vitiligo. This is a delivery hypothesis, and it is stated as one - the pigment pharmacology is entirely the peptide's (MC1R engagement, cAMP, MITF, melanogenic enzyme induction), while the needling only removes the barrier. The factorial design is the point: a needling-alone arm separates the injury effect (see the standalone microneedling hypothesis) from the delivery effect, which no published vitiligo combination study has cleanly done.
Evidence
The delivery-potentiation principle is well replicated in vitiligo. PMID 41640472 randomised 60 patients to microneedling plus 5-FU, TCA or botulinum toxin-A, with VASI reductions of 50%, 29% and 5% respectively - the effect tracked the applied agent, which is exactly what a delivery mechanism predicts. PMID 33497514 used a within-patient design (50 patients, bilateral lesions, all on NB-UVB, microneedling both sides, latanoprost one side and saline the other) and found the latanoprost side significantly better - a placebo-controlled demonstration that needling plus an active beats needling plus vehicle. PMID 42186640 reaches the same conclusion at review level and attributes the benefit partly to enhanced drug absorption. Against this: PMID 36419200 found no added benefit from needling on top of NB-UVB, so potentiation is not universal. The peptide side of the pairing must be graded exactly as this run already established: PMID 30222197 is in vitro and ex vivo pharmacology with a 15-volunteer MC1-R-on-plucked-hairs biomarker arm, NOT a clinical efficacy trial - no placebo, no colour endpoint; the only human colour data is PMID 37034550, n=1, uncontrolled, with concurrent oral biotin/pantothenate. So this hypothesis pairs a delivery method with reasonable procedural evidence to an active with essentially no efficacy evidence. It is worth running only because the four-cell factorial (needling+peptide / needling+vehicle / peptide alone / untreated) is the cheapest way to find out whether the peptide arms that dominate this run's leaderboard have been failing on pharmacology or merely on penetration. (refs: PMID 41640472, PMID 33497514, PMID 42186640, PMID 36419200, PMID 30222197, PMID 37034550, PMID 25056010)
Safety
Same procedural profile as standalone needling (0.5-1.0 mm, monthly, disinfected single-user device, no needling over inflamed scalp, exclude anyone with vitiligo history because of Koebner risk - PMID 25056010), with one added and non-trivial hazard: applying ANY topical into freshly opened micro-channels bypasses the barrier that normally limits systemic absorption and irritation. Only cosmetic-grade, preservative-simple, water-or-glycol vehicles should be used this way; do not apply alcohol-heavy tonics, essential oils, or anything not intended for compromised skin into fresh channels. Practical rule: apply immediately post-needling for one session's worth only, then revert to ordinary leave-on application for the intervening weeks. Systemic exposure of a peptide at cosmetic concentrations remains negligible even with enhanced penetration; the realistic harms are irritant contact dermatitis and sensitisation, amplified by the breached barrier. Patch-test on forearm skin, including a needled forearm patch, for three days first.
Novelty rationale
Microneedling is an established adjunct in vitiligo and alopecia, so the procedure is not novel and is flagged as such. What is novel is the target of the experiment: this is not proposed as a better treatment but as a discriminating test of the run's own dominant hypothesis class. H040/H044/H050/H064 have all argued for palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 and have all been challenged on whether the molecule reaches the bulb; none proposed a way to bypass the barrier and settle it. Adding the needling-alone cell also means the experiment cannot be confounded by the injury effect - a criticism that would sink a simple two-arm combination study. Because it depends on a marketed anti-grey cosmetic active, the peptide half of this pairing is by definition not a novel repurposing idea; the novelty is entirely in the delivery route and the factorial logic.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 4/10, safety 6/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict revise. The delivery framing is the honest part and the arithmetic is the weak link. The hypothesis states the bulb sits 3-4 mm deep and then proposes 0.5-1.0 mm needles. Those needles do not create a conduit to the bulb; they breach the stratum corneum and the upper infundibulum only. So the experiment does not test 'does the peptide reach the bulb' - it tests 'does the peptide reach the upper follicle', which is a different and much weaker question. The bulge (where MelSCs actually live) is arguably within reach, and the hypothesis would be far more defensible if it said so explicitly and predicted a bulge-mediated effect; as written it promises to settle a bulb-delivery question it cannot settle. Second, the pigment pharmacology is entirely borrowed from an active with essentially no efficacy evidence: PMID 30222197 is the manufacturer-linked in vitro/ex vivo package whose only human arm is a 15-volunteer MC1-R-on-plucked-hairs biomarker readout with no colour endpoint and no placebo (verified against the corpus abstract - it is exactly as described), and PMID 37034550 is n=1, uncontrolled, with concurrent oral biotin/pantothenate, so even its positive '>90% conversion' is uninterpretable. Multiplying a delivery method of uncertain relevance by an active of unproven pharmacology gives a four-cell factorial that will almost certainly return four nulls, and a null will be attributed to whichever cell the author prefers. Third, the cited delivery evidence is weaker than presented: PMID 41640472 has no needling-alone arm, so its agent-tracking ordering (5-FU 50% > TCA 29% > BTX-A 5%) is equally consistent with the agents' own pharmacology rather than with enhanced penetration; PMID 33497514 does demonstrate needling+active > needling+vehicle, but every patient was also on NB-UVB, so it shows potentiation on top of phototherapy, not potentiation of a leave-on cosmetic; and PMID 36419200 is flatly null for adding needling to NB-UVB. Finally, all of this is vitiligo - repigmentation of depigmented interfollicular EPIDERMIS by melanocytes migrating from follicular reservoirs - which is not the same target tissue as a grey hair shaft. Salvageable, but only if the depth claim is corrected to the bulge, the peptide arm is graded as the unproven variable it is, and the study is powered for a per-hair proximal-band endpoint rather than a VASI-style area score.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run (web, beyond the bundle): 'microneedling induced hypopigmentation depigmentation adverse effect'; 'microneedling for melasma pigment reduction'; 'microneedling scalp hair loss adverse'; 'microneedling Koebner vitiligo induced depigmentation scalp'; 'palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 Greyverse independent evidence'; 'alpha-MSH agonist peptide skin whitening depigmenting cosmetic'. Opposite-direction findings for the PROCEDURE are real and were omitted by the hypothesis: (1) microneedling is commercially sold and clinically studied as a treatment to REMOVE pigment - a systematic review (PubMed 32897944), a JAAD meta-analysis of microneedling as adjuvant to topicals for melasma, and randomised studies of microneedling-assisted tranexamic acid/cysteamine/metformin report MASI reductions of roughly 29-50%; the same physical procedure this hypothesis proposes as a repigmentation adjunct is the standard adjunct in the depigmentation clinic. The honest nuance is that in melasma the needling is again mostly a delivery vehicle for depigmenting actives, so the procedure is direction-agnostic rather than intrinsically depigmenting - but that is precisely the problem: a direction-agnostic amplifier applied to an active with no proven direction. (2) Post-microneedling hypopigmentation is a documented adverse event at roughly 1.7% of patients in the complications literature, and repeated scalp needling has been linked to perifollicular fibrosis and micro-scarring. (3) Koebner-induced depigmentation is a documented enough hazard that active Koebner phenomenon is a standard exclusion criterion in vitiligo microneedling trials, consistent with the hypothesis's own PMID 25056010. Searched specifically for the ACTIVE going the wrong way: palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20 / Greyverse has no depigmenting or skin-whitening commercial use whatsoever - it is sold exclusively as an anti-grey/melanin-stimulating hair active, it is an MC1R agonist, and no whitening-actives literature claims it. So the molecule is clean in direction; the procedure is not. Also checked whether microneedling alone depigments in vitiligo: no - PMC8751692 and PMC12124262 show microneedling-alone arms repigment, and the mechanism proposed is peripheral melanocyte migration into the lesion, i.e. same-direction.
Validation

No external validation recorded

54. narrowband UVB phototherapy (311 nm) delivered by a handheld home device to a clipped, comb-parted scalp test zone (H070) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: procedure - device; handheld/comb 311 nm NB-UVB unit, home-administered, suberythemal starting fluence with erythema-guided escalation, 2-3 sessions per week, applied only to a clipped or comb-parted concealed scalp zone with a pre-specified cumulative-dose ceiling · origin: generation (round 6)

Mechanism
This is the strongest pigment-restoring mechanism in clinical medicine and the run has never considered it. NB-UVB does not merely tan: it acts on the same follicular stem-cell reservoir that greying depletes. UVB-damaged keratinocytes drive p53-mediated POMC expression, releasing alpha-MSH that engages melanocyte MC1R and raises MITF and melanogenesis - the identical receptor axis the run's cosmetic peptides try to hit biomimetically, but driven endogenously and at physiological potency (PMID 33320376). More importantly for canities, UVB stimulates bulge melanocyte stem cells to differentiate and migrate upward, which is why vitiligo repigmentation appears as perifollicular islands (PMID 33320376), and post-NB-UVB biopsies in vitiligo patients show melanocyte and gene-expression changes consistent with MelSC mobilisation to the epidermis (PMID 42377206). Applied to a greying scalp the hypothesis is that repeated suberythemal 311 nm exposure of the follicular ostia recruits residual bulge MelSCs into the pigmentary unit of the next anagen. The mechanism is real; the delivery and the risk are the problems, and both are addressed below rather than assumed away.
Evidence
The efficacy evidence base is the best in this entire candidate universe, but all of it is vitiligo. PMID 40985573 (176 patients, non-segmental vitiligo): 45% achieved >=50% repigmentation, 95% of responders held it >=6 months and 59% held it >72 months; response tracked longer treatment (>18 months) and higher cumulative dose (>356 J/cm2). PMID 25102894 is the systematic review; PMID 34221228 is a 3,000-patient retrospective. Home delivery is not a hand-wave: PMID 41804555 (18 studies, 1,341 patients; 4 studies meta-analysed) found home-based handheld UVB non-inferior to in-office UVB for >50% (OR 1.04) and >75% (OR 1.26) repigmentation with no excess erythema or burning and 14% lower discontinuation; PMID 39912150 pools 13 studies (557 patients) with >50% repigmentation in 40.8%; PMID 33245043 is a proper three-arm RCT (HI-Light Vitiligo) of home handheld NB-UVB. So a consumer can obtain and correctly use this device. What does not exist is any canities data whatsoever, and the transfer is NOT safe to assume: vitiligo is autoimmune destruction of a melanocyte population that NB-UVB both immunosuppresses and repopulates, whereas canities is age/oxidative exhaustion of MelSCs with no autoimmune component to suppress. NB-UVB may therefore lose half its mechanism when moved to grey hair. (refs: PMID 40985573, PMID 42377206, PMID 41804555, PMID 39912150, PMID 33245043, PMID 25102894, PMID 34221228, PMID 33320376, PMID 42305972, PMID 41053225)
Safety
TWO objections must be stated plainly rather than glossed. First, DELIVERY: hair itself is an efficient UV shield - 311 nm reaching a haired scalp is absorbed and scattered by the shaft canopy long before it reaches the ostium, which is precisely why the vitiligo evidence base is dominated by face, neck, hands and other exposed sites (PMID 40985573 reports the best response rates on face/neck). This is a real obstacle and it is only partly solvable: the only honest protocol is a deliberately clipped or shaved test zone, or a comb-type device that parts the hair and delivers along the exposed part-line, treating one narrow parting at a time. If the user is unwilling to clip a zone, this candidate should be abandoned rather than fudged - a whole-scalp treatment through intact hair will deliver an unknown and probably subtherapeutic dose. Second, CARCINOGENICITY for a purely COSMETIC indication: cumulative UV dose is the dose-response variable for BOTH efficacy and harm, and the responders in PMID 40985573 are the ones who took >356 J/cm2 over >18 months. PMID 42305972 sets out the paradox directly - NB-UVB and excimer are the most effective repigmenting agents known, and the same radiation causes oxidative stress, DNA damage, apoptosis, genomic instability, photoaging and melanoma risk. In vitiligo that trade is defensible because the disease is disfiguring and the depigmented skin is itself unprotected. For grey hair it is a much worse bargain: the patient is asking for a cosmetic outcome and being offered accumulated DNA damage to scalp skin that is often already sun-exposed and, in balding men, permanently so. There is also a mechanism-level reason to suspect UV could BACKFIRE here: PMID 41053225 shows that DNA double-strand breaks drive McSCs into senescence-coupled differentiation, selectively depleting the stem-cell pool and CAUSING hair greying (while protecting against melanoma). A genotoxic stimulus aimed at mobilising MelSCs may instead exhaust them - the very phenotype being treated. Practical mitigations if run at all: strict suberythemal starting dose with erythema-guided escalation, a hard cumulative-dose cap set far below the vitiligo responder range, eye protection, a single small concealed zone rather than whole scalp, and no use in anyone with skin-cancer history, photosensitivity, or photosensitising medication.
Novelty rationale
In vitiligo - the one pigmentary disorder where clinical repigmentation is routinely achieved - the standard of care is a device, not a drug. Five rounds and 67 hypotheses in this run considered only things that can be swallowed or applied, and never once weighed the single strongest piece of device-based pigment evidence in existence. That omission is the reason this hypothesis is here, and stating it is more useful than the hypothesis itself. NB-UVB is emphatically NOT a novel intervention - it is decades-old standard care with an enormous literature - so there is no novelty claim about the modality. The novel move is the indication transfer to canities plus a concrete answer to the two questions that transfer raises (how UV reaches a follicle under hair, and whether the cumulative-dose risk is acceptable for a cosmetic endpoint). My honest answer to the second is that it probably is not, at the doses associated with response, which is why confidence is 2 and not higher despite the best efficacy evidence in the universe.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 3/10, novelty 2/10 — verdict reject. This hypothesis is unusually candid about its own weaknesses and still understates the fatal one. The vitiligo evidence base it leans on measures repigmentation of depigmented SKIN, and the accepted mechanism of that repigmentation is that melanocyte precursors emerge from the hair follicle outward into the epidermis - perifollicular islands, as the hypothesis itself notes. Canities asks for the exact opposite trajectory: pigment restored to the hair SHAFT by a functioning follicular pigmentary unit. The vitiligo literature has already run the closest available approximation of that question and the answer is negative. In vitiligo patches with leukotrichia, repigmentation is documented to occur at macule peripheries and NOT around the affected white hair follicles, and the recent literature states plainly that existing phototherapies are ineffective in patients with complete leukotrichia (see contra_evidence). So the single best-evidenced repigmenting modality in medicine has been applied for decades to white hairs sitting inside actively treated fields, under the most favourable conditions imaginable, and has not turned them dark. That is a natural experiment that largely pre-answers this hypothesis, and it is not cited anywhere in the bundle. On top of that: the UV-shielding-by-hair objection is real and the proposed fix (clip or shave a zone, or part the hair) either destroys the cosmetic endpoint or delivers an unknown dose; the immunosuppressive half of the NB-UVB mechanism has no substrate in canities, as the hypothesis concedes; and the dose-response the author correctly identifies (responders in PMID 40985573 needed >356 J/cm2 over >18 months, verified in the corpus abstract) is the same axis as the carcinogenic and photoaging ledger. Offering accumulated genotoxic scalp dose for a cosmetic endpoint with zero canities data and a negative natural experiment is not a defensible trade. The citations themselves check out - PMID 41804555 really does report OR 1.04 / OR 1.26 non-inferiority for home handheld UVB in 1,341 patients, and PMID 41053225 really does report DSB-driven McSC seno-differentiation causing greying - which makes it more striking that the author read 41053225, described the backfire risk, and proposed the intervention anyway at confidence 2.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run (web, beyond the bundle): 'ultraviolet radiation causes hair greying melanocyte stem cell depletion UVB induced canities'; 'phototherapy NB-UVB leukotrichia white hair poor prognosis repigmentation'; 'UVB phototherapy hypopigmentation adverse effect long term'. Three independent lines of documented opposite-direction evidence, none of them in the bundle. (1) UVB is a documented CAUSE of follicular melanocyte stem cell depletion. Chou et al. (Nat Med 2013; PMC3859297) show that after wounding or UVB irradiation McSCs exit the follicular stem-cell niche before their first division and migrate upward to the epidermis in an Mc1r-dependent manner - explicitly described as occurring at the risk of stem-cell depletion. The hypothesis cites this same upward migration as its therapeutic mechanism. It is the same event: mobilising bulge McSCs out of the follicle is how you depopulate the follicle. For vitiligo that is the desired direction (you want them in the epidermis); for canities it is the disease. (2) In-corpus PMID 41053225 independently establishes that DNA double-strand breaks drive McSC senescence-coupled differentiation, selective depletion and hair greying - i.e. a genotoxic stimulus produces the target phenotype, not its reversal. Reviews of premature greying (PMC11444426) list UV-driven ROS among the drivers of melanocyte damage and greying. (3) The natural experiment: in vitiligo, repigmentation under NB-UVB occurs at macule peripheries and not around leukotrichic follicles, and Lei et al. 2024 (PubMed 38728444, reverse perilesional home phototherapy RCT) open by stating that existing phototherapies are ineffective for vitiligo with complete leukotrichia - decades of the strongest repigmenting modality in medicine applied to white hairs inside treated fields, without turning them dark. Also searched for whether phototherapy causes pigment loss directly: 'altered pigmentation' and photoaging with lentigines are listed adverse effects of UVB phototherapy (The dark side of the light: Phototherapy adverse effects, Clin Dermatol), though the dominant skin effect is tanning, not depigmentation. Net: the opposite-direction evidence here is not about the skin going pale, it is about the hair follicle stem-cell pool being emptied by exactly the stimulus proposed.
Validation

No external validation recorded

55. 308 nm excimer lamp / targeted phototherapy applied to a single small shaved scalp test zone as a dose-limited proof-of-concept for whether canities follicles respond to UV at all (H071) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: procedure - device; clinic-administered targeted 308 nm excimer lamp or LED, 2-3 sessions/week, single shaved scalp zone of a few cm2, erythema-guided dosing with a pre-set session cap · origin: generation (round 6)

Mechanism
Same photobiology as NB-UVB - keratinocyte DNA damage, POMC/alpha-MSH release, MC1R-MITF-driven melanogenesis, and mobilisation of bulge MelSCs into the pigmentary unit (PMID 33320376) - but delivered as a targeted beam to a few square centimetres rather than a field. That geometric difference is the entire rationale. Targeted 308 nm devices deliver higher per-session fluence to a defined patch while leaving surrounding skin completely unirradiated, which converts an unacceptable whole-scalp cumulative-dose problem into a bounded one confined to a zone the size of a postage stamp. The question this hypothesis is designed to answer is not 'should people treat grey hair with UV' - it is the prior question 'do greying scalp follicles retain a UV-responsive MelSC reservoir at all', which is currently unknown and is cheaply answerable in one small zone.
Evidence
PMID 40525788 compared targeted NB-UVB, 308-nm excimer laser and 308-nm excimer lamp head to head in vitiligo: very good to excellent response in 42.4%, 17.6% and 29.4% respectively with no statistically significant efficacy difference between modalities, but significantly more adverse effects in the excimer lamp group. PMID 33047405 compares localised 308-nm excimer light with targeted 311-nm NB-UVB directly. PMID 41074622 compares a 308-nm LED source with a 308-nm monochromatic excimer lamp, which matters because LED sources are the route by which this modality could become affordable and eventually home-usable. Taken together the evidence says targeted 308 nm is roughly equivalent to targeted NB-UVB in vitiligo, with a somewhat worse local tolerability profile for the lamp - so it earns its place here on geometry (dose confinement), not on superior efficacy. There is no canities data of any kind, and the same vitiligo-to-canities transfer objection applies: no autoimmunity to suppress in grey hair. Read-out is per-hair, not per-area: 50 hairs cut from the treated zone and 50 from an untreated shaved control zone at 3 and 6 months, scored blind for a pigmented proximal band. A negative result in this zone is genuinely informative - it would argue that the greying follicle's MelSC pool is not UV-recruitable, which would deprioritise the entire phototherapy branch cheaply. (refs: PMID 40525788, PMID 33047405, PMID 41074622, PMID 33320376, PMID 42305972, PMID 41053225)
Safety
Clinic-based, not home-based, and that is a real access failure against this run's OTC constraint - targeted 308 nm units are practitioner equipment and each session is billed. Modality: 308 nm monochromatic excimer lamp or LED, 2-3 sessions per week, starting at a fraction of the zone's minimal erythema dose with erythema-guided escalation. The zone MUST be shaved - the same UV-blocking-by-hair problem applies to 308 nm exactly as it does to 311 nm, and a targeted beam fired into a hair canopy delivers an unknown dose. Local adverse effects are common and were significantly more frequent with the excimer lamp than with targeted NB-UVB (PMID 40525788): erythema, burning, blistering, and post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation of the surrounding skin, which is a cosmetically awkward outcome on a visible scalp. The cumulative-dose and carcinogenicity argument from the NB-UVB hypothesis is not waived by confining the field - it is reduced to a small area, which is the honest most that can be claimed (PMID 42305972), and the possibility that genotoxic stress accelerates MelSC seno-differentiation and greying rather than reversing it applies here too (PMID 41053225). Because this is proposed as a bounded proof-of-concept rather than a treatment, it should be pre-registered with a fixed session count and stopped at that count regardless of result.
Novelty rationale
Established standard-of-care technology for localised vitiligo with no novelty as a device. It is proposed here for a reason distinct from the NB-UVB hypothesis: not as a candidate treatment but as the cheapest bounded experiment that could falsify the whole UV branch. This run has repeatedly produced hypotheses that cannot fail informatively; this one can. Its weakness against this run's constraints is explicit - clinic-based, practitioner-gated, per-session cost, and unsuitable for whole-scalp use because a diffuse cosmetic complaint is a bad match for a modality designed to treat discrete patches.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 4/10, novelty 2/10 — verdict revise. The framing - a bounded, pre-registered, falsifiable proof-of-concept rather than a treatment - is the most disciplined reasoning in this batch, and it is the only reason this is not a straight reject alongside H070. But the central claim, that whether greying follicles retain a UV-responsive MelSC reservoir 'is currently unknown and is cheaply answerable', is wrong on the first half. It is not unknown. Leukotrichic hairs sitting inside vitiligo fields have received targeted 308 nm and NB-UVB for decades, and the literature states that phototherapy is ineffective in complete leukotrichia and that repigmentation spares white-haired follicles (see contra_evidence). A proposed experiment whose result is already indicated by a large accidental dataset is not the cheapest informative experiment; it is a re-run. The hypothesis must engage that literature explicitly and argue why canities follicles differ from leukotrichic vitiligo follicles - a real argument is available (leukotrichia implies follicular melanocyte reservoir destruction by autoimmunity, whereas canities may leave a quiescent-but-present pool), but it is not made, and without it the design rationale collapses. Secondary problems: the run's stated OTC/easy-access constraint is violated outright - practitioner-gated, billed per session; the readout as specified (50 treated vs 50 control hairs at 3 and 6 months) is underpowered for the low event rate a genuinely quiescent MelSC pool would produce, and shaving a scalp zone plus a shaved control zone is a cosmetically visible cost for a cosmetic complaint; and PMID 40525788 - which I verified in the corpus - actually shows the excimer LAMP (the modality proposed) at 29.4% very-good-to-excellent versus 42.4% for targeted NB-UVB with significantly MORE adverse effects, so the hypothesis is choosing the numerically worst and least tolerable of the three compared devices on a geometry argument that targeted NB-UVB would satisfy equally well. Finally, the carcinogenicity ledger is confined, not abolished, and the author says so honestly; but confining a genotoxic dose to a postage-stamp zone on a cosmetic indication is a smaller bad bargain, not a good one.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run (web, beyond the bundle): '308 nm excimer laser hypopigmentation adverse effect'; 'laser induced leukotrichia hair depigmentation after laser hair removal'; 'phototherapy NB-UVB leukotrichia white hair poor prognosis repigmentation'; plus the UV/McSC-depletion searches reported for H070, which apply identically here since the photobiology is the same. Findings. (1) Same-photobiology depletion risk as H070: Chou et al. Nat Med 2013 (PMC3859297) - UVB drives follicular McSCs out of the niche toward the epidermis at the risk of stem-cell depletion; in-corpus PMID 41053225 - DSBs drive McSC seno-differentiation and hair greying. 308 nm is more energetic per photon than 311 nm, so nothing about targeting reduces the per-photon genotoxicity, only the irradiated area. (2) The leukotrichia natural experiment (PubMed 38728444; PMC7477062): repigmentation occurs at macule peripheries and not around white-haired follicles, and existing phototherapies are stated to be ineffective in complete leukotrichia - direct evidence that this modality does not turn white hairs dark. (3) Light/laser devices as a class have documented hair-DEPIGMENTING effects: leukotrichia is a documented complication of intense pulsed light and laser hair removal (PubMed 12135507), attributed to melanocytes having a shorter thermal relaxation time than germinative cells so that pigment cells are destroyed at fluences the follicle survives - i.e. a light device that leaves you with a living but white hair. This is a thermal mechanism and not directly transferable to a non-ablative 308 nm lamp, so I record it as class-level rather than modality-specific evidence. Searched specifically whether 308 nm excimer itself causes hypopigmentation: no - it is used therapeutically FOR hypopigmented scars, striae alba and post-lichen-striatus hypopigmentation (PubMed 15313811), so on skin its documented direction is repigmenting. The opposite-direction evidence here is about the follicular stem-cell pool and about white hairs specifically, not about skin colour.
Validation

No external validation recorded

56. photobiomodulation / low-level laser therapy (red 650-660 nm home laser cap or LED helmet) as the testable, non-genotoxic version of the mitochondrial-ATP argument (H072) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: procedure - device; home red-light photobiomodulation (650-660 nm laser diode or LED, optional 810-850 nm NIR), 10-30 min sessions daily or alternate days for 9-12 months, one marked parietal zone irradiated with the contralateral zone shielded as control · origin: generation (round 6)

Mechanism
Several hypotheses in this run have gestured at follicular energy failure and mitochondrial decline without proposing anything that actually tests it. Photobiomodulation is the conventional, mechanistically specified version of that argument. Red light in the 630-670 nm band is absorbed by cytochrome c oxidase, complex IV of the respiratory chain, raising electron transport, ATP output and nitric-oxide release, and modulating ROS as a signalling species rather than as damage (PMIDs 40968340, 33111207). Downstream, PBM converges on the pathway that maintains the follicle's stem-cell compartments: PBM-generated ROS activate PI3K/AKT/GSK-3beta, which stabilises beta-CATENIN in hair follicle stem cells against proteasomal degradation, while PBM also induces paracrine WNT secretion from skin-derived precursors - and deleting Ctnnb1 in follicle stem cells abolishes the effect entirely, which is a clean causal demonstration rather than a correlation (PMID 34019818). That matters for pigment specifically because Wnt/beta-catenin is the signalling axis governing MelSC maintenance and their commitment to differentiate at anagen onset - the exact decision that fails in greying (PMID 36853923). PBM has also been reviewed directly as a repigmentation mechanism in vitiligo, where the model system is precisely follicular MelSC-derived melanoblast regeneration (PMID 30698884). Crucially, all of this happens with NO ionising or ultraviolet exposure, so the carcinogenicity trade-off that makes NB-UVB a bad bargain for a cosmetic indication does not apply.
Evidence
Direct pigment evidence is thin but not absent: PMID 30698884 is a dedicated review of the molecular events by which PBM induces repigmentation in vitiligo, noting favourable historical responses to low-energy helium-neon laser, and framing vitiligo repigmentation as a model for PBM's regenerative effect on the follicular melanocyte lineage. PMID 41420458 is a case report in which a patient refractory to 308 nm excimer monotherapy repigmented over 3 months when 635 nm red light was ADDED - suggestive of a red-light contribution to pigment, but n=1 and combined. PMID 40546989, a dedicated grey-hair review, lists LLLT among current prevention/treatment approaches, which establishes that the idea is on the radar for canities but not that it works. The growth (not pigment) evidence is much stronger and mechanistically informative: PMID 34019818 for beta-CATENIN causality, PMID 42187428 showing 650 nm irradiation increased follicle numbers and raised skin ATP measured by fluorescence lifetime imaging - a direct measurement of the energetic claim rather than an inference - and PMIDs 33111207, 40968340 for wavelength-dependent mechanism. Honest ceiling, stated flatly: devices are OTC and widely sold for hair growth, and evidence for a PIGMENT effect in humans is absent. Every pigment-relevant result above is either a review, a mouse, a case report or an inference from a shared signalling pathway. Design: alternating half-scalp exposure is impossible with a helmet, so use a handheld or panel emitter on one clipped-marked parietal zone with the contralateral zone shielded, daily or alternate-day sessions at the manufacturer's fluence for 9-12 months, with the per-hair proximal-band readout at 6, 9 and 12 months. (refs: PMID 30698884, PMID 34019818, PMID 42187428, PMID 40968340, PMID 33111207, PMID 40546989, PMID 41420458, PMID 36853923, PMID 41053225, PMID 26983796)
Safety
Home-usable, OTC, and the best risk profile of any device in this candidate list - this is its main argument. Modality: 650-660 nm red (optionally with a near-infrared component around 810-850 nm for deeper penetration), delivered by laser-diode cap, LED helmet or panel, typical consumer fluences of a few J/cm2 per session, 10-30 minutes, daily to alternate-day, for a minimum of 6-12 months because any pigment change requires new anagen growth. No UV component, therefore no cumulative-dose carcinogenicity ledger, no photoaging argument and no DNA-damage route to the MelSC seno-differentiation risk that counts against NB-UVB (PMID 41053225). Realistic harms: eye exposure (never look into laser-diode emitters; use the supplied shielding), transient scalp warmth or erythema, and rare headache. Two non-safety caveats a consumer must be told: consumer devices vary enormously in actual delivered irradiance and many are underpowered relative to trial devices, and low-fluence light exposure has been discussed as a possible driver of paradoxical hair growth, though the review of that literature finds it poorly substantiated for home devices and more likely attributable to inflammation or hormonal factors (PMID 26983796). Cost is the real barrier - a credible cap is a few hundred dollars - but it is a one-off purchase with no prescriber, no injections and no clinic visits.
Novelty rationale
LLLT/PBM devices have an ESTABLISHED hair-related use - they are cleared and widely marketed for androgenetic alopecia hair growth, with systematic reviews behind that indication - so nothing about the device is novel and it must not be presented as a discovery. Two things here are new to this run. First, no procedure or device of any kind appears among its 67 hypotheses; phototherapy has surfaced only as a passing adjunct inside drug hypotheses. Second, and more specifically, this converts the run's recurring but untested 'mitochondrial/ATP restoration' intuition into a named target (cytochrome c oxidase), a named downstream pathway (ROS-PI3K/AKT/GSK-3beta-beta-CATENIN plus paracrine WNT), a measurable intermediate (tissue ATP), and an OTC device that a consumer already has access to - all without invoking UV. Confidence is held at 2 because the entire pigment case rests on a review, a case report and pathway-sharing with hair growth, and because a device sold for one indication accumulating anecdotes for another is exactly the pattern that has misled this field before.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 8/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict revise. This is the round's repeat of the tetrahydrocurcumin failure and nobody in the bundle noticed. The hypothesis proposes 650-660 nm red light at consumer fluences to restore pigment. Red light at 630-660 nm, at radiant exposures of roughly 5-20 J/cm2, is documented to DOWNREGULATE tyrosinase, reduce melanin content and reduce melanocyte viability, and photobiomodulation in exactly this band is under active clinical development as a treatment for MELASMA - i.e. it is being studied and marketed to take pigment away (see contra_evidence). Consumer laser caps and LED helmets operate in and around that fluence window. So the single wavelength band the hypothesis selects is the one with the best-documented DEpigmenting dose-response, and the bundle cites none of it. Everything else follows from that. The pigment case as presented is: one review of PBM repigmentation in vitiligo (PMID 30698884), one n=1 case report where 635 nm was ADDED to 308 nm excimer so the excimer cannot be excluded (PMID 41420458), one grey-hair review that merely lists LLLT as an approach (PMID 40546989), and otherwise pathway-sharing with hair GROWTH - beta-catenin (PMID 34019818) and a murine FLIM ATP study (PMID 42187428, which I verified is C57BL/6 mice, follicle counts and skin ATP, no pigment endpoint at all). Sharing Wnt/beta-catenin with MelSC maintenance is an inference, not evidence; beta-catenin stabilisation in follicle stem cells is a growth result and the paper's own causal test was hair regeneration, not colour. The other weak link is the device itself: the hypothesis concedes consumer units vary enormously in delivered irradiance, which means a null result cannot distinguish 'wrong mechanism' from 'wrong dose' - and given the biphasic melanogenesis dose-response now on the record, an underpowered device and an overpowered device may push pigment in opposite directions. Safety and access remain genuinely the best in this batch, which is why this is revisable rather than rejectable: no UV, no genotoxic ledger, OTC, one-off cost. But it must be rewritten around a melanin dose-response, not a monotonic 'more red light, more pigment' assumption.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run (web, beyond the bundle): 'red light 630 nm LED inhibits melanogenesis tyrosinase'; 'photobiomodulation for melasma pigment reduction whitening'; 'what colour LED for pigmentation'. This returned direct, documented opposite-direction evidence at the proposed wavelengths. (1) Galache et al., 'Photobiomodulation for melasma treatment: integrative review and state of the art' (Photodermatol Photoimmunol Photomed 2024, doi 10.1111/phpp.12935): red 630 nm, amber 585/590 nm and infrared 830/850 nm at radiant exposures between 1 and 20 J/cm2 exert modulatory effects on tyrosinase activity, gene expression and protein synthesis of melanocytic pathway components and significantly REDUCE melanin content; the review concludes PBM is a promising treatment for melasma-associated hyperpigmentation. (2) Specifically: 630 nm, 633 nm and 660 nm LEDs decrease melanin levels, downregulate tyrosinase and reduce melanocyte viability at 5-20 J/cm2 (and at 96 J/cm2); 630-nm LED photobiomodulation attenuates melanogenesis in B16F10 melanoma cells and in primary human melanocytes. (3) 590 nm LED ameliorated pigmentation in melasma clinically (PMC9776419). Commercially, red-light LED masks and panels are marketed for pigmentation/dark-spot fading, and the consumer-facing dermatology literature discusses red light for melasma - so 'what it is actually sold for' includes pigment reduction, not only hair growth. Counter-checked in the other direction: I found no clinical study showing red-light PBM darkens hair or increases follicular melanin in humans; the corpus's own pigment citations are a review, an n=1 combination case and a mouse growth study. Also searched whether PBM is documented to cause hair loss or greying: PMID 26983796 (in corpus) reviews paradoxical hair growth from low-level exposure and finds it poorly substantiated - no greying signal found. Net: the direction of PBM's effect on melanin is fluence- and cell-context-dependent, and the best-documented effect in the 630-660 nm band at consumer-relevant fluences is melanin SUPPRESSION.
Validation

No external validation recorded

57. photobiomodulation device operated in a UV-free VISIBLE-BLUE mode (453 nm) targeting follicular OPN3/OPN2 opsin photoreceptors, as a wavelength-selection experiment rather than a new device (H073) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: procedure - device; UV-free 453 nm visible blue LED at approximately 3 J/cm2 per session, with mandatory eye shielding, proposed FIRST as an ex vivo human follicle wavelength comparison (453 nm vs 660 nm vs dark) before any in-vivo scalp zone test · origin: generation (round 6)

Mechanism
The default assumption behind consumer light caps is that red is the active wavelength because red penetrates deepest and hits cytochrome c oxidase. That assumption may be wrong for the follicle, because the human hair follicle expresses genuine opsin photoreceptors - OPN2 (rhodopsin) and OPN3 (panopsin/encephalopsin) are detectable in distinct compartments of skin and anagen follicle - and opsins are canonical G-protein-coupled receptors that signal through phospholipase C and intracellular calcium release. That is directly relevant to pigment: MITF, the master melanocyte transcription factor, is regulated not only by the cAMP arm downstream of MC1R but also by calcium-dependent signalling, and calcium handling appears as a mechanistic node in pigment models in this corpus - NB-UVB/ADSC-induced repigmentation was linked to Nrf2/HO-1-mediated Ca2+ homeostasis (PMID 34751412), and exosome-driven protection of melanocyte stem cells operated through Nrf2 together with a calcium-dependent signalling pathway (PMID 42029421). So a visible, non-ionising wavelength that engages a follicular GPCR with a calcium-linked second messenger is an ordinary, published route to MITF - no exotic physics required, and specifically not any quantum or induction framing, which describes nothing these devices actually do. The hypothesis is simply that the pigment-relevant wavelength for the follicle may not be the one the growth-focused device industry standardised on.
Evidence
PMID 28418107 is the anchor and it is unusually clean: OPN2 and OPN3 expression was characterised in human skin and anagen follicles by RT-qPCR and immunofluorescence; 3.2 J/cm2 of 453 nm blue light significantly prolonged anagen in ex vivo human follicles with sustained proliferation, while the SAME 3.2 J/cm2 delivered at 689 nm (red) had no significant effect; and silencing OPN3 in outer root sheath cells changed the transcriptomic response. That is a wavelength-discriminating, loss-of-function-supported result in HUMAN follicles, which is stronger evidence than most of what the red-light literature offers. PMID 40968340 provides the general wavelength-penetration-mechanism framework showing that different bands act through different targets. The calcium-to-pigment link is supported only indirectly and at the level of animal and cell models (PMIDs 34751412, 42029421); there is no experiment anywhere showing blue light changes hair colour, and the OPN3 result is an anagen-duration and proliferation endpoint, not a pigment endpoint. Penetration is a genuine physical objection: 453 nm scatters far more than 660 nm and reaches shallower, so bulb-level dosing is questionable even if the receptor biology is right - though the bulge, which is where MelSCs live, sits considerably shallower than the bulb. The proposal is therefore explicitly a bench-first arm: before any human zone trial, test 453 nm versus 660 nm versus dark control on microdissected human anagen follicles for melanin content and MITF/TYR expression. If that fails, drop it. (refs: PMID 28418107, PMID 40968340, PMID 34751412, PMID 42029421)
Safety
UV-free and non-ionising, so the cumulative-dose carcinogenicity argument that counts against 311 nm and 308 nm does not apply. Blue light at these fluences is used cosmetically and dermatologically; the recognised concerns are retinal exposure (blue is the band where photochemical retinal hazard is real - eye shielding is mandatory and non-negotiable, more so than for red), and a possible contribution of visible blue-violet light to hyperpigmentation in darker phototypes, which on facial skin is an unwanted effect but on a scalp being treated for pigment loss is an ambiguous one worth measuring rather than fearing. Practical delivery would be a 453 nm LED panel or comb at a few J/cm2 per session; devices in this band exist as consumer acne and skincare units, so access is plausible but the specific parameters from PMID 28418107 are not what off-the-shelf units advertise, which is a real implementation gap.
Novelty rationale
This is the same candidate entry as the red-light photobiomodulation hypothesis but with the opposite wavelength choice and a completely different receptor rationale, and the two make opposing, discriminable predictions - which is the point of proposing both rather than one. Nothing in this run's 67 hypotheses has considered that a light-based intervention has a wavelength parameter that must be chosen on follicular receptor biology rather than inherited from hair-growth marketing. The novelty is in the target selection (follicular opsins and a calcium-linked route to MITF, stated in ordinary cell-biology terms) rather than in the hardware, which already exists. Confidence is 2 and would be 1 without PMID 28418107; it is capped because the human evidence measures anagen duration, not colour, and because the shallow penetration of blue light is a physical objection that enthusiasm does not overcome.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 6/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict revise. The wavelength-selection framing is the freshest thinking in this batch and the anchor citation is genuinely the best-quality single result in it - I verified PMID 28418107 in the corpus and it is exactly as described: OPN2/OPN3 characterised by RT-qPCR and immunofluorescence in human skin and anagen follicles, 3.2 J/cm2 at 453 nm significantly prolonged ex vivo anagen with sustained proliferation, the same fluence at 689 nm did not, and OPN3 silencing altered the transcriptomic response. That is wavelength-discriminating, loss-of-function-supported human tissue data. But the hypothesis then makes a receptor-pharmacology error that inverts its own prediction. It reasons: OPN3 is a GPCR with a calcium-linked second messenger, calcium regulates MITF, therefore engaging OPN3 should raise MITF. The published pharmacology of OPN3 in human epidermal melanocytes is the opposite - OPN3 couples to Gai, physically complexes with MC1R, and acts as a NEGATIVE regulator of MC1R-mediated melanogenic signalling by suppressing the alpha-MSH-induced cAMP response (see contra_evidence). Engaging the receptor this hypothesis selects is documented to shut down the exact MC1R-cAMP-MITF axis that H069 and the run's whole peptide branch are trying to switch on - which also means that if 453 nm were ever combined with palmitoyl tetrapeptide-20, the two arms would be pharmacological antagonists, a collision nobody in this run has flagged. Beyond that: the human evidence measures anagen duration, not colour, and the author says so; the calcium-to-pigment link rests on a mouse NB-UVB/ADSC model (PMID 34751412) and a mouse exosome study (PMID 42029421), neither of which is about blue light; 453 nm penetration is poor and the bulge-is-shallower argument is asserted without a depth-dosimetry number; and off-the-shelf blue consumer units are acne/skincare devices whose output does not match the 3.2 J/cm2 parameter, which the author concedes is a real implementation gap. The bench-first design is the correct instinct and is what keeps this alive. It should be restructured as a dose-response with a bidirectional hypothesis, an explicit MC1R-antagonism control arm, and mandatory eye shielding.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run (web, beyond the bundle): 'blue light 450 nm OPN3 melanocyte hyperpigmentation'; 'blue light inhibits melanogenesis hair follicle depigmentation'; 'Opsin 3 negative regulator MC1R signaling melanogenesis human melanocytes Ozdeslik PNAS'. Documented opposite-direction evidence on three fronts. (1) The receptor: Ozdeslik et al., PNAS 2019 (doi 10.1073/pnas.1902825116), 'Human nonvisual opsin 3 regulates pigmentation of epidermal melanocytes through functional interaction with melanocortin 1 receptor' - OPN3 couples to Gai to NEGATIVELY regulate the alpha-MSH-induced cAMP response of MC1R, OPN3 and MC1R form a physical complex, and OPN3 acts as a negative regulator of MC1R-mediated melanogenic signalling; the same work reports OPN3 does not mediate visible-light sensitivity in these melanocytes despite binding retinal. That is the direct inverse of this hypothesis's proposed mechanism. (2) The wavelength: blue light inhibits melanin synthesis in B16 melanoma 4A5 cells and suppresses UVB-induced skin pigmentation in guinea pigs (PubMed 15030593); blue light suppresses melanin formation following repeated UVB exposure. (3) The tissue: 457 nm blue light significantly reduced viability and proliferation of human hair follicle stem cells and dermal papilla cells in vitro in an intensity-dependent manner (PubMed 39377836) - i.e. blue light damages the very stem-cell compartment the hypothesis wants to preserve, which is a hazard the safety section does not mention. Evidence in the CLAIMED direction also exists and I record it for fairness: Regazzetti et al. JID 2018 (PubMed 28842328) show melanocytes sense blue light and regulate pigmentation through Opsin-3 with sustained hyperpigmentation in phototype III+, and PubMed 39241981 (2024) reports blue-light pigmentation via OPN3-TRPV1 calcium influx, CAMKII/CREB and MITF phosphorylation. So the literature documents OPN3 signalling in BOTH directions, with the sign apparently dependent on fluence (450 nm failed to activate OPN3 at 200 mJ/cm2), phototype and whether MC1R is concurrently stimulated. A mechanism documented to cut both ways, with a peer-reviewed negative-regulator result sitting on the exact axis the run's other hypotheses are trying to activate, cannot be presented as a one-way route to MITF.
Validation

No external validation recorded

58. fractional non-ablative laser (1550 nm erbium glass) as a combined controlled-micro-injury and topical-penetration procedure, paired with a prostaglandin analogue on a small scalp zone (H074) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: procedure plus topical - clinic-administered non-ablative fractional 1550 nm laser at low density monthly, with a prostaglandin F2-alpha analogue applied into the channels immediately post-pass and continued as a daily leave-on between sessions, on one clipped concealed zone · origin: generation (round 6)

Mechanism
Two mechanisms at once, and both are already exploited in vitiligo. First, fractional laser creates columns of controlled thermal micro-injury that trigger regeneration and, like microneedling, mobilise follicular stem-cell compartments through the wound-healing response (PMID 40627439). Second, and more reliably, those columns are transient conduits: laser-assisted drug delivery reproducibly increases transepidermal penetration of applied topicals, which is precisely the barrier that blocks every leave-on candidate in this run from reaching the bulb. Pairing it with a prostaglandin F2-alpha analogue is deliberate - prostaglandin analogues have an established melanogenic effect (eyelash and periocular skin darkening is the classic on-label-adjacent observation), and this run already carries latanoprost and bimatoprost hypotheses (H005, H006, H014, H036) that are limited by exactly the same delivery uncertainty. Compared with microneedling, fractional laser gives more reproducible, operator-independent column depth and density, at the cost of requiring a clinic.
Evidence
The combination principle is well supported in vitiligo and, notably, in a hair follicle context. PMID 34750933 tested triple therapy - NB-UVB plus fractional CO2 laser plus topical bimatoprost 0.01% - for non-segmental vitiligo, which is the closest published analogue to this proposal. PMID 39809953 compared fractional CO2 plus NB-UVB against NB-UVB alone. PMID 34546107 assessed high-dose fractional Er:YAG in stable vitiligo. PMID 40252129 is the most transferable: fractional CO2 laser combined with bimatoprost enhanced hair regrowth in alopecia areata - a hair follicle endpoint with a prostaglandin analogue and a fractional laser together. PMID 31820470 covers fractional Er:YAG-assisted delivery of a topical. Two honest deductions: none of this literature measures HAIR COLOUR in a non-vitiligo subject, and most of it uses ABLATIVE CO2 or Er:YAG rather than the non-ablative 1550 nm erbium glass proposed here - non-ablative was chosen for a better safety and downtime profile on a cosmetic indication, but that choice means the cited evidence is for a more aggressive device than the one proposed, and penetration enhancement is correspondingly weaker. That gap is the main reason confidence is low. (refs: PMID 34750933, PMID 39809953, PMID 34546107, PMID 40252129, PMID 31820470, PMID 40627439, PMID 25056010)
Safety
Clinic-based and practitioner-operated - a clear failure against this run's OTC/easy-access constraint, and it should not be dressed up otherwise. Settings would be low-density, low-fluence 1550 nm non-ablative passes at monthly intervals over a small concealed zone, with the topical applied immediately post-pass into the open channels. Realistic harms: pain requiring topical anaesthesia, erythema and oedema for days, post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation or hypopigmentation (a cosmetically perverse risk when the goal is pigment), and infection. Scalp-specific: laser energy is absorbed by melanin in pigmented shafts, so treating a still-pigmented scalp risks unwanted heating of hair and follicular thermal injury - the zone should be clipped. Koebner risk applies as with any injury-based modality (PMID 25056010) and anyone with vitiligo history should be excluded. Delivering a prostaglandin analogue into fresh laser channels materially increases systemic and periocular exposure relative to normal topical use, so bimatoprost/latanoprost must be applied in a strictly bounded amount to a bounded zone, never near the eye, and periorbital hyperpigmentation and iris pigmentation warnings apply. Cost per session is substantial and the number of sessions to any colour endpoint is unknown.
Novelty rationale
Fractional laser has an established hair-related and vitiligo-related use as a combination modality, and prostaglandin analogues are already represented four times in this run's 67 hypotheses - so neither component is novel and both are flagged. The new element is the pairing rationale: the prostaglandin hypotheses in this run have all been constrained by whether the drug reaches its target and by contact-time limits on a haired scalp, and none proposed a physical route around that. Ranked against the other procedures here it is deliberately placed low - it is the most invasive, most expensive and least accessible of the injury-based options while offering, at best, the same mechanism microneedling offers for the price of a dermaroller. It is included because it is the version of that mechanism with reproducible dosimetry, which matters if the cheap version returns an ambiguous result.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 3/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict revise. The weakest link is a device/evidence mismatch the hypothesis itself half-admits and then proceeds anyway. Every cited paper is real and accurately described - I checked all seven against the corpus - but 34750933, 39809953, 34546107, 31820470 and 40252129 are ALL ablative (CO2 or Er:YAG) fractional devices. The proposal is non-ablative 1550 nm erbium glass, which does not ablate the stratum corneum: it coagulates microscopic thermal zones under an intact epidermis that re-epithelialises within 24 h. There are no open channels to drop bimatoprost into. The entire laser-assisted-drug-delivery rationale - the 'more reliable' of the two claimed mechanisms - is a property of the ablative devices in the citations and largely evaporates in the device actually proposed. Choosing the safer device silently deletes the mechanism, and the hypothesis presents this as a quantitative weakening ('penetration enhancement is correspondingly weaker') when it is closer to categorical. Second: not one citation has a hair-COLOUR endpoint. 40252129 is hair REGROWTH in alopecia areata; 34750933/39809953/34546107/31820470 are vitiligo macule repigmentation, i.e. epidermal melanocyte repopulation from the follicular reservoir, which is a different cell-biological problem from restoring a depleted bulge MelSC pool in a normally-haired scalp. Third, the two-variable design is uninterpretable: if a zone repigments you cannot say whether it was the laser injury, the prostaglandin, or the clipping-and-regrowth cycle. Fourth, the safety_notes concede that melanin in pigmented shafts absorbs the laser energy - meaning the device preferentially deposits heat in exactly the pigmented follicles you are trying not to harm, while sparing the white ones you are trying to treat. That is an inverse dose-response against the therapeutic target. Finally the access failure is total: clinic-only, expensive, unknown session count, against a run constraint of OTC/easy-access for a cosmetic complaint. Ranked honestly by the author, but the honesty does not repair the mechanism.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run outside the bundle: (1) 'laser hair removal induced leukotrichia white hair after laser epilation'; (2) 'non-ablative fractional 1550 nm laser hypopigmentation adverse effect delayed'; (3) 'fractional laser induced hair depigmentation leukotrichia hypopigmentation scalp fractional photothermolysis'; (4) 'latanoprost bimatoprost prostaglandin analogue depigmentation poliosis eyelash whitening hypopigmentation adverse'. Both arms have documented opposite-direction effects. LASER ARM: laser/IPL epilation is a documented CAUSE of leukotrichia - Radmanesh & Mostaghimi, Dermatol Surg 2002 (PMID 12135507) reported leukotrichia in 29/821 IPL-treated patients, 13 with no grey hair at baseline and 16 reporting ACCELERATED greying beginning after the first or second session. The proposed mechanism is exactly the one that should worry us here: the thermal relaxation time of the melanocyte is shorter than that of the germinative cells, so a sub-epilatory fluence can kill or disable follicular melanocytes while leaving the follicle alive and growing - i.e. it produces a living white hair, which is precisely the target lesion. Deeper-penetrating wavelengths (532/1064 nm) are reported to give PERMANENT leukotrichia. Laser-induced hypopigmentation and delayed-onset hypopigmentation are well-described resurfacing complications with their own treatment literature (NB-UVB targeted phototherapy for laser-induced hypopigmentation; ablative fractional resurfacing for CO2-induced hypopigmentation), and hypopigmentation is listed among the standard 1540/1550 nm non-ablative fractional adverse-event set alongside PIH. The hypothesis mentions hypopigmentation only in passing as a generic PIH-adjacent risk and never engages with laser-induced leukotrichia as a mechanism-level threat to its own endpoint. DRUG ARM: topical prostaglandin F2-alpha analogues cause POLIOSIS - Chen et al. report a series of 7 patients (4 latanoprost, 2 bimatoprost, 1 travoprost) who developed depigmented eyelashes, onset as early as 6 weeks, interspersed with normally pigmented lashes; there is a dedicated report 'Topical prostaglandin F(2alpha) analog induced poliosis' and a case of latanoprost-induced malar hypertrichosis with eyelash poliosis (Actas Dermosifiliogr). So the single most-cited on-label-adjacent pigment observation for this drug class cuts both ways in the same follicular structure - and the hypothesis presents only the darkening half. Commercial framing: 1550 nm non-ablative fractional is sold for photoaging, acne scars and, notably, for TREATING pigmentation (lentigines, melasma, PIH) - i.e. its cosmetic market is pigment REMOVAL, not pigment induction. Nothing found supporting repigmentation of grey scalp hair by either arm.
Validation

No external validation recorded

59. platelet-rich plasma (PRP) and PRP-derived exosomes by intradermal scalp injection - the only modality in this candidate universe with a direct human GREY-HAIR repigmentation series, and the reason that series should not be believed yet (H075) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: procedure - clinic-administered intradermal scalp injection (autologous PRP, or an exosome preparation), typically 4-6 sessions at 2-4 week intervals with maintenance sessions thereafter · origin: generation (round 6)

Mechanism
Autologous PRP concentrates platelet alpha-granule growth factors (PDGF, VEGF, EGF, IGF-1, TGF-beta) and delivers them intradermally at the follicular level; exosome preparations deliver a defined vesicular cargo instead. The pigment-relevant mechanisms are antioxidant and stem-cell-protective rather than melanogenic per se, which maps onto the best-documented driver of greying - the millimolar follicular H2O2 burden with failed catalase and methionine-sulfoxide repair (PMID 19237503) - and onto MelSC loss as the central lesion (PMID 36853923). PMID 42029421 gives the cleanest mechanistic account available: umbilical-cord MSC-derived exosomes preserved melanocyte stem cell integrity under acute neurogenic oxidative stress in C57BL/6J mice, dose-dependently increasing Melan-a proliferation (53% and 94% at the two highest doses), upregulating Nrf2 and its downstream antioxidant genes HO-1, SOD-1, GR-1 and CAT, activating a calcium-dependent signalling pathway, and reducing stress-induced hair depigmentation in vivo. That is a direct pigment endpoint driven by restoring the follicle's own antioxidant enzymes - which is precisely what this run's topical antioxidant hypotheses have been trying and failing to justify quantitatively.
Evidence
PMID 41201122 is the only study in this corpus reporting repigmentation of GREY HAIR in humans from a device/procedure: 10 patients treated with rose stem cell-derived exosomes, mean 4.6 sessions, repigmentation observed after a mean of 2.4 sessions, mean outcome score 2.8, 60% achieving >=50% improvement, no adverse events, with better response in shorter graying duration, coexisting AGA and moderate baseline severity. It must be read with maximum scepticism and I will state why rather than quote the numbers approvingly: it is cross-sectional and observational with no control group, n=10, the exosome product is a plant-derived cosmetic preparation rather than a characterised therapeutic, the paper itself says 'various procedures' were used - meaning the delivery modality (which almost certainly included needling or microneedling-type administration) is confounded with the product - and the reported effect lasted a mean of only 4.7 months. Given that spontaneous repigmentation of grey hair is documented (PMID 23974581), an uncontrolled 10-patient series with a mixed-modality intervention is at anecdote grade, and the injection procedure itself is a needle-injury intervention indistinguishable from the microneedling hypothesis above. PMID 42257913 is the controlled animal comparison: PRP versus PRP-derived exosomes in an H2O2-induced depigmentation model in 42 guinea pigs, with both reversing histological, immunohistochemical and biochemical depigmentation - and, importantly, the design INCLUDED a recovery group specifically to quantify spontaneous repigmentation after H2O2 withdrawal, which is the control every human anecdote in this area lacks. Human vitiligo combination data exist (PMIDs 34719101, 36062405, 37984828) but always as an add-on to phototherapy, never alone. So the picture is: reasonable mechanism, one controlled animal study, one uncontrolled human grey-hair series, and no controlled human canities data at all. (refs: PMID 41201122, PMID 42257913, PMID 42029421, PMID 34719101, PMID 36062405, PMID 37984828, PMID 19237503, PMID 36853923, PMID 23974581)
Safety
Clinic-based, needle-based, and repeated - a poor fit for this run's OTC access constraint, and that alone should cap its ranking regardless of the mechanism. Autologous PRP has a benign safety profile because the material is the patient's own: expected effects are injection pain, transient oedema, bruising and headache; risks are infection and, rarely, injury from poor technique. Exosome products are a different matter and warrant more caution than the PRP arm: they are not standardised, regulatory status varies by jurisdiction, plant-derived and MSC-derived preparations are not equivalent to each other, product composition is frequently unverified, and the human series above used a cosmetic-grade plant exosome. Anyone considering this should understand they are buying an uncharacterised biological injected into the dermis. Cost is high and recurring, and the reported benefit in the only human grey-hair series faded at under 5 months - meaning indefinite repeat sessions, which multiplies both cost and cumulative procedural risk. No systemic drug exposure and no UV dose, so the specific hazards that count against phototherapy do not apply.
Novelty rationale
PRP has an established hair-related use in AGA and alopecia areata, and exosome therapy is actively marketed for hair, so neither is a novel intervention - both are flagged as such. What is genuinely new to this run is that no procedure of any kind appears among the 67 existing hypotheses, and that this one has something none of them have: a published human series with a grey-hair colour endpoint. The correct use of that fact is not to promote the candidate but to note that the run's best human evidence for ANY intervention now sits with a procedure rather than a molecule, and that the series in question is weak enough (n=10, uncontrolled, mixed modality, transient) that it mainly demonstrates how low the evidential bar in this field is. Confidence 2, held down by the access failure, the cost, the transience of the reported effect and the fact that the injection procedure is confounded with the needle injury it delivers - a confound the microneedling hypothesis tests far more cheaply.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 4/10, safety 5/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict revise. This is the most carefully written hypothesis of the four and it still fails, for a reason it does not see. Citation check: all nine PMIDs are in the corpus and are described accurately - 42029421 really is HUCB-MSC exosomes preserving MelSC integrity via Nrf2-ARE under neurogenic oxidative stress in C57BL/6J mice; 42257913 really is PRP vs PRP-exosomes in an H2O2 depigmentation model in 42 guinea pigs WITH a spontaneous-recovery control group; 41201122 really is the n=10 uncontrolled rose-stem-cell-exosome grey-hair series. The self-criticism of 41201122 is accurate and unusually candid. But the hypothesis conflates three non-equivalent agents under one heading and the conflation is load-bearing. Autologous PRP, human umbilical-cord MSC exosomes, PRP-derived exosomes, and plant (rose) stem-cell cosmetic exosomes share a delivery route and nothing else - different cargo, different species of origin, different regulatory status, different mechanisms. The mechanism section is carried entirely by 42029421 (HUCB-MSC exosomes, mouse) while the human evidence is carried entirely by 41201122 (rose plant exosomes, cosmetic grade) and the safety framing by autologous PRP. None of these supports the others. Worse, PRP's dominant pigment-relevant growth factor is TGF-beta1, which is an established INHIBITOR of melanogenesis (see contra_evidence) - so the PRP arm's own pharmacology points away from repigmentation. The vitiligo human data (34719101, 36062405, 37984828) are all PRP as an ADD-ON to 308 nm excimer or NB-UVB and never establish a PRP-alone pigment effect; 37984828 actually found PRP/NB-UVB numerically WORSE than latanoprost/NB-UVB (13.3% vs 26.7% excellent response), which the evidence_summary omits. Confounding is fatal in 41201122: 'various procedures' means needle injury is inseparable from product, so the series cannot distinguish exosomes from microneedling, and a 4.7-month mean duration is consistent with a single anagen-cycle artefact or with the spontaneous repigmentation documented in 23974581. Access, cost and repeat-session burden are correctly flagged. The correct recommendation is to split PRP from exosomes into separate hypotheses and to drop the pigment claim for the PRP arm entirely.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run outside the bundle: (1) 'platelet-rich plasma melasma treatment TGF-beta1 inhibits melanogenesis tyrosinase MITF skin lightening'; (2) 'exosome therapy skin brightening whitening melasma cosmetic marketed depigmenting'; (3) direct fetch of the ASJ Open Forum 2024 paper on rose stem-cell exosomes and microneedling. Both arms are DOCUMENTED in the opposite direction, and for the exosome arm it is the identical product. (a) PRP IS A MELASMA TREATMENT. Intradermal PRP is used clinically to LIGHTEN pigment (J Cutan Aesthet Surg 2024 intradermal PRP for melasma in dark skin; 'Platelet-rich plasma is a promising therapy for melasma', PMID 34013618; PRP + oral tranexamic acid for facial melasma; microneedle fractional RF + PRP split-face RCT for melasma, JCD 2026; PRP is even trialled for GINGIVAL DEPIGMENTATION, NCT07359339). The stated mechanism is exactly the one the hypothesis relies on: TGF-beta1, the dominant PRP alpha-granule factor, suppresses the MITF promoter, thereby downregulating tyrosinase, TRP-1 and TRP-2 and reducing melanin synthesis. MITF is the master melanocyte transcription factor. So the agent proposed to restore hair pigment is used commercially to remove skin pigment, via inhibition of the exact transcription factor that pigment restoration requires. (b) ROSE STEM-CELL EXOSOMES ARE SOLD AS A BRIGHTENING AGENT. The corpus's one human grey-hair series (41201122) uses rose stem cell-derived exosomes (RSCE). I fetched the prior publication on the SAME product - 'New Therapeutic Approach With Rose Stem-Cell-Derived Exosomes and Non-Thermal Microneedling for the Treatment of Facial Pigmentation' (ASJ Open Forum 2024, ojae060), ASCEplus Dermal Signal Kit, 12 volunteers, indication solar lentigines/melasma/PIH/periorbital hyperpigmentation. Result: superficial pigmentation and spots DECREASED 12.95%, deep pigmentation DECREASED 15.9% at week 12, sustained with no relapse. Same product, same delivery route (microneedling), opposite pigment endpoint, published earlier. Stem-cell exosomes are patented and marketed as cosmetic WHITENING compositions (e.g. US 10,071,050 'Cosmetic composition containing exosomes extracted from stem cell for skin whitening'), and exosome-augmented microneedling is a current melasma modality with a documented 'whitening effect ... without rebounds'. This is the tetrahydrocurcumin failure mode repeating: a commercially-sold depigmenting cosmetic being proposed as a repigmentation agent. It does not by itself prove the grey-hair claim false - a follicular MelSC-protective effect and an epidermal melanin-reducing effect are not strictly contradictory - but it means the ONLY human grey-hair evidence in this run comes from a product whose own manufacturer-adjacent literature reports it reducing pigment, and 41201122's uncontrolled n=10 design cannot survive that. (c) 42029421's positive result is a DIFFERENT agent (human umbilical-cord MSC exosomes) and cannot be transferred to RSCE. Nothing found showing PRP or exosomes causing hair greying per se.
Validation

No external validation recorded

60. electrolysis / galvanic needle epilation as a putative bystander stimulus to NEIGHBOURING follicles - proposed in order to be assessed and, on the evidence available, rejected (H076) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 1/5 · route: procedure - clinic-administered galvanic/blend needle epilation (destructive); rejected as proposed, with a non-destructive low-current scalp stimulation device named only as the form any future electrical hypothesis would have to take · origin: generation (round 6)

Mechanism
Any pigment claim for electrolysis has to survive an immediate structural objection: the procedure works by passing direct current through a fine needle inserted into the follicle to generate sodium hydroxide at the tip, chemically destroying the germinative cells. The treated follicle does not repigment because the treated follicle is gone. Therefore the only coherent version of a pigment claim is a BYSTANDER effect - that the surrounding dermal injury, inflammation and current flow act on neighbouring, untreated follicles, mobilising their bulge melanocyte stem cells in the same way any other controlled micro-injury might (PMID 40627439). Framed that way it becomes a weaker, less controllable and considerably more destructive member of the same family as microneedling, with no dosimetry and no way to separate the current from the injury. If one wanted to test the electrical component specifically - as distinct from the injury - the honest cell-biology route is that melanocyte MITF is regulated by calcium-dependent as well as cAMP-dependent signalling, and calcium handling appears in pigment models in this corpus (Nrf2/HO-1-linked Ca2+ homeostasis in NB-UVB-induced repigmentation, PMID 34751412; calcium-dependent signalling in exosome-mediated MelSC protection, PMID 42029421); a non-destructive low-current stimulus is the only design in which such a mechanism could be examined without destroying the tissue being measured. That would be a different device from an epilation unit, and this corpus contains no study of electrical stimulation of follicles at all - a fact I am reporting rather than papering over.
Evidence
There is NO evidence. The words electrolysis, galvanic and electrical stimulation return zero hits across all 542 corpus records; not one paper examines follicular electrical stimulation for pigment. The entire basis of the claim is practitioner anecdote, and that anecdote has a specific, disqualifying confounder: spontaneous repigmentation of grey hair is documented in the canities literature (PMID 23974581), and full colour variation from normal to white occurs both along individual hairs and between neighbouring hairs on the same scalp - so an observer looking at a treated area over months will see pigmented hairs appear whether or not the treatment did anything.
