Electrolysis clients are also, by definition, undergoing repeated observation of a small skin area with high attention to individual hairs, which is close to an ideal setup for generating false-positive impressions. There is a further reason for caution in the opposite direction: PMID 25056010 documents vitiligo induced by Koebnerisation at a site of laser epilation, i.e. epilatory trauma causing DEpigmentation in a susceptible individual. The one relevant piece of adjacent evidence is that a purely physical stimulus can mobilise melanoblasts (pulsed ultrasound via M-CSF/FAK, PMID 23725022), which keeps the general idea from being absurd without doing anything to support electrolysis specifically. Assessment: the mechanism as usually claimed is incoherent because the procedure is destructive; the bystander reframing is coherent but is strictly dominated by microneedling, which delivers the same injury signal reversibly, cheaply, at home, with dose control, and without destroying follicles. (refs: PMID 23974581, PMID 25056010, PMID 40627439, PMID 23725022, PMID 34751412, PMID 42029421)
Safety
This is the only candidate in this set where the honest recommendation is against use. Modality: clinic-based, practitioner-operated, permanent by design - galvanic or blend electrolysis destroys the treated follicle irreversibly, which on a scalp being treated for a COSMETIC pigment complaint means trading permanent hair loss for a speculative and unevidenced colour effect. That trade is unacceptable on its face. Additional harms: pain, post-treatment erythema and pustulation, pitted or hypertrophic scarring with poor technique, infection, post-inflammatory pigment change, and Koebner-type depigmentation in vitiligo-susceptible individuals (PMID 25056010). Sessions are numerous and costly. If any electrical hypothesis is worth pursuing at all it is the non-destructive low-current variant sketched above, which would need to be built and characterised before it could be tested, and which no published study currently supports.
Novelty rationale
Neither electrolysis nor its pigment claim appears anywhere in this run's 67 hypotheses, so it is new to the run - but new is not the same as good, and the value of including it is the negative verdict rather than the candidate. It is written up explicitly so that this branch is closed on stated grounds (destructive mechanism, zero corpus evidence, anecdote fully explained by documented spontaneous repigmentation, dominated by microneedling on every axis) instead of being left as an unexamined gap that resurfaces later as folklore. Confidence 1, which is the lowest available and is the intended message.
Reviews
  • plausibility 2/10, evidence 2/10, safety 2/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict reject. The hypothesis reaches the right verdict on itself, and I concur with it rather than softening it - but a self-rejecting hypothesis is still a hypothesis that consumed a generation slot, and the scores must reflect the candidate, not the prose. The structural objection is decisive and correctly stated: galvanic electrolysis generates sodium hydroxide at the needle tip to chemically destroy the germinative matrix. A destroyed follicle produces no hair of any colour, so 'repigmentation by electrolysis' is not weakly supported, it is category-incoherent. The bystander reframing is the only survivable version and it is strictly dominated on every axis by microneedling: same wound-healing/immunomodulation signal (40627439), reversible, dosable, cheap, at home, non-destructive. There is no reason to prefer the version that permanently destroys tissue. The corpus check is correct - I confirmed 'electrolysis', 'galvanic' and 'electrical stimulation' return nothing pigment-relevant across the 542 records - and the confounder analysis is the best part of the write-up: electrolysis clients undergo months of high-attention serial observation of a small skin area, which combined with documented spontaneous grey-hair repigmentation and along-shaft colour variation (23974581) is close to a purpose-built false-positive generator. Citations verified: 25056010 is genuinely Koebner-induced vitiligo after Nd:YAG laser EPILATION (depigmentation caused by epilatory trauma, in a patient with prior Sutton nevus - note the pre-existing susceptibility, which the hypothesis does flag); 23725022 is genuinely pulsed ultrasound driving melanoblast migration via M-CSF/FAK, and is honestly labelled as keeping the general idea non-absurd without supporting electrolysis. The MITF calcium-signalling thread (34751412, 42029421) is real but both are about Ca2+ homeostasis in cells, not about applied electrical current, and using them to motivate a hypothetical low-current device is a leap the author acknowledges. Reject, and the branch should stay closed.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run outside the bundle: (1) 'electrolysis permanent hair removal grey white hair only option destroys follicle scarring hypopigmentation'; (2) 'laser hair removal induced leukotrichia white hair after laser epilation'; plus corpus grep for electrolysis/galvanic/electrical. What electrolysis is ACTUALLY SOLD AND USED FOR is the whole answer, and it is the exact opposite of the claim: it is marketed as the definitive method for PERMANENTLY DESTROYING GREY AND WHITE HAIR. Because the probe finds the follicle mechanically rather than optically, it is promoted industry-wide as the only permanent modality that is pigment-independent and therefore the referral route for exactly the white/grey hairs that laser cannot target - clinic marketing pages are near-unanimous on 'white hair treats identically to black hair', and it is the FDA-recognised permanent hair removal method. So the commercial reality is: a person with grey hairs is sent to electrolysis to have those grey hairs eliminated, permanently, follicle and all. Any anecdote of a treated zone 'looking less grey' afterwards has an obvious non-pigmentary explanation - the grey hairs were destroyed, not repigmented. That is opposite-direction in the strongest possible sense, and it is a confounder the hypothesis does not name (it names spontaneous repigmentation and observation bias, but not selective removal of the grey hairs themselves). Additional documented opposite-direction findings: electrolysis causes hypopigmentation as a recognised local adverse effect, especially in darker skin, alongside hyperpigmentation, pitted/hypertrophic scarring, blistering and infection; and Koebner-phenomenon vitiligo is documented after laser epilation (PMID 25056010), with IPL/laser epilation documented to cause and accelerate leukotrichia (PMID 12135507, 29/821 patients, 16 reporting accelerated new white hairs). No study anywhere - corpus or open literature - reports electrolysis, galvanic current or follicular electrical stimulation restoring hair pigment.
Validation

No external validation recorded

61. scalp cooling / controlled thermal stress as a niche-protective measure aimed at the sympathetic-stress arm of greying (H077) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 1/5 · route: procedure - device; home cooling cap or gel wrap, short intermittent sessions during defined high-stress periods; no established dose, duration, temperature or schedule exists for this indication · origin: generation (round 6)

Mechanism
The one place a cooling device could plausibly intersect greying biology is the stress axis. Acute sympathetic activation depletes melanocyte stem cells, and in ex vivo human follicles noradrenaline together with H2O2 drives ectopic differentiation of bulge MelSCs and of outer root sheath amelanotic melanocytes - the cellular signature of the earliest greying steps (PMID 33598985); exosome work in mice frames the same lesion as neurogenic oxidative stress causing hair depigmentation (PMID 42029421). Scalp cooling has an established, unrelated clinical precedent as a follicle-PROTECTIVE measure - vasoconstriction and reduced local metabolic rate limit chemotherapy delivery and cytotoxic injury to the follicle during infusion. The speculative extension is that intermittent local cooling could reduce perifollicular metabolic and oxidative load, or blunt local catecholamine-driven signalling at the niche, during periods of high systemic stress. I do not find this convincing, and say so below.
Evidence
The evidence is effectively nil and I will not manufacture more than exists. Searching all 542 corpus records for cooling returns only laser hair-removal papers using contact cooling for epidermal protection; scalp cooling, cryotherapy and hypothermia for pigment return nothing relevant. There is no animal model, no human series, and no mechanistic study of cooling and hair pigment anywhere in this corpus. The stress-to-greying biology it leans on is real and well supported (PMIDs 33598985, 42029421, 36853923), but the link from that biology to a cooling device is entirely my own inference with no experimental step in between. Two specific reasons for doubt beyond the missing evidence: first, chemotherapy scalp cooling protects follicles during a discrete, known cytotoxic exposure of a few hours, whereas greying is a decades-long chronic process with no comparable window to intervene in; second, the sympathetic mechanism in the greying literature is systemic catecholamine signalling reaching the niche, which local skin cooling would not obviously interrupt and might even amplify, since cold is itself a sympathetic stimulus that triggers local noradrenaline release and vasoconstriction. That last point is close to a mechanistic self-refutation and is the honest reason this hypothesis is ranked last. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 42029421, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Modality and setting: home-usable cooling caps and gel wraps are cheap and freely available, so access is not the problem - plausibility is. Applied as intermittent short sessions rather than the continuous multi-hour protocols used during chemotherapy infusion, the harms are minor and local: discomfort, headache, dizziness, and with excessive duration or direct ice contact, cold injury to skin. Contraindicated in cold urticaria, cryoglobulinaemia, Raynaud phenomenon and scalp skin disease. No systemic exposure, no UV, no needles, no prescriber. The real cost of pursuing this is not harm but opportunity: it would consume a test zone and a year of observation on a mechanism with no supporting experiment, and this run has better-founded procedures competing for the same zone.
Novelty rationale
New to this run in the trivial sense that no procedure appears among the 67 existing hypotheses, and new to the field in the sense that nobody has proposed cooling for canities - but the absence of prior proposals here reflects the absence of a reason rather than an overlooked opportunity. It is included for completeness of the device section and to record an explicit negative assessment: of the eight procedures in the candidate universe, this is the only one with neither pigment evidence nor a mechanism that survives inspection, since local cooling is itself a sympathetic stimulus and the stress pathway it targets is systemic. Confidence 1. It should be ranked below every other procedure proposed in this round and below most of the run's existing molecular hypotheses.
Reviews
  • plausibility 2/10, evidence 1/10, safety 6/10, novelty 4/10 — verdict reject. The author writes that the second objection 'is close to a mechanistic self-refutation'. It is not close - it is one, and it should have prevented the hypothesis being submitted rather than being disclosed at the bottom of it. Cold is a sympathetic stimulus. The entire pigment rationale rests on 33598985 and 42029421, in which noradrenergic/neurogenic signalling at the niche drives ectopic MelSC differentiation and depletion; applying cold to the scalp triggers local cutaneous sympathetic activation, noradrenaline release and vasoconstriction. The proposed intervention delivers the insult its own mechanism identifies as the lesion. Citations verified and accurately described: 33598985 is genuinely an ex vivo human hair follicle method for quantifying stress-associated ectopic MelSC/ORS melanocyte differentiation; 42029421 is genuinely exosomes preserving MelSCs under acute neurogenic oxidative stress; 36853923 is a genuine MelSC-and-greying review. All three establish that a stress axis exists. None of them says anything about temperature, cooling, vasoconstriction, or any device. The inferential gap between 'sympathetic signalling depletes MelSCs' and 'therefore put a cold cap on' has no experimental step in it whatsoever, and the author says so. The borrowed precedent is also mis-shaped: chemotherapy scalp cooling works by vasoconstricting to EXCLUDE a circulating cytotoxic during a known 1-4 hour infusion window. Greying has no infusion, no window, and no circulating agent to exclude - and if the driver is systemic catecholamine reaching the niche, vasoconstriction does not exclude it in any comparable way. The third-order problem is that reduced perifollicular perfusion and metabolic rate would, if anything, impair melanogenesis, which is a high-flux biosynthetic process requiring tyrosine delivery and copper-dependent tyrosinase activity. Access and cost are genuinely favourable and the harms are minor - which is why safety scores 6 and nothing lower - but a safe intervention with no mechanism and no evidence is not a cheap bet, it is a wasted test zone and a wasted year of anagen. Reject.
    contra-evidence (documented) ⚠️: Searches run outside the bundle: (1) 'cryotherapy hypopigmentation melanocytes cryosensitive leukotrichia cold injury depigmentation lentigines'; (2) 'scalp cooling chemotherapy hair colour change regrowth loss of pigment'; plus corpus grep for cooling/cryotherapy/hypothermia (confirms only laser contact-cooling papers, no pigment relevance). The opposite-direction evidence here is textbook-grade and the hypothesis does not mention it at all. MELANOCYTES ARE THE MOST COLD-SENSITIVE CELL IN SKIN. Standard dermatology teaching (StatPearls 'Cryotherapy in Dermatology'; Medscape cryotherapy overview; 'Pigment changes in human skin after cryotherapy', PMID 3769517) is that melanocytes die at approximately -5 to -10 degrees C, far above the temperature needed to kill keratinocytes, and that HYPOPIGMENTATION IS THE MOST COMMON ADVERSE EFFECT OF CRYOTHERAPY. That selectivity is not an accident of dosing - it is why cryotherapy is deliberately used to REMOVE pigmented lesions such as solar lentigines, and why it is a recognised hazard in darker skin. Reported pigment loss after skin cooling or freezing is attributed to reduced melanin production, reduced melanosome production, melanocyte destruction, and impaired melanosome transfer to keratinocytes - and notably, hypopigmentation persists after brief freezes EVEN WHERE FUNCTIONAL MELANOCYTES REMAIN, i.e. sub-lethal cooling alone suppresses pigment transfer. Sub-lethal cooling is exactly what a cooling cap does. Cryotherapy on hair-bearing sites including scalp is also documented to cause alopecia via cold damage to bulge cells - the same bulge that houses the MelSCs this hypothesis wants to protect. On the specific precedent: scalp cooling literature (Hair Safe study; the scalp-cooling persistent-CIA RCT, PMID 38843479; MSK/Mayo patient materials) reports post-chemotherapy regrowth with ALTERED TEXTURE AND COLOUR as a routine outcome, and nowhere reports colour preservation or darkening as a benefit of cooling. Searched for any study of cooling, cold, cryo or hypothermia restoring or preserving hair pigment: none exists in the corpus or in the open literature. Summary: the modality's best-documented pigmentary effect in human skin is hypopigmentation, the intervention's stimulus (cold) is a sympathetic activator and the target pathway is sympathetic depletion of MelSCs, and there is zero supporting evidence in either direction for hair colour.
Validation

No external validation recorded

62. curcumin (H022) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1500 · confidence 2/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 2)

Mechanism
Curcumin is a well-documented activator of Nrf2, the master transcription factor for endogenous antioxidant enzymes — including catalase, glutathione peroxidase, and heme oxygenase-1. The defining biochemical lesion of grey follicles is collapsed catalase with millimolar H2O2 accumulation and impaired methionine-sulfoxide repair. Rather than scavenging ROS molecule-for-molecule (the incumbent antioxidants' approach), Nrf2 activation aims to restore the follicle's own peroxide-clearing enzyme expression, attacking the root enzymatic deficit.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 shows hydrogen peroxide drives ectopic MelSC differentiation and depletion in ex vivo human follicles, and the dossier (Wood et al., FASEB J 2009) documents the catalase/MSR collapse in grey follicles — the enzyme deficit Nrf2 induction would counteract. Curcumin's Nrf2 activation is established outside the hair field; no greying or hair-pigment trials exist. (refs: PMID 33598985)
Safety
OTC with a strong safety record at 500–1000 mg/day; oral bioavailability is poor without piperine or a liposomal/phytosome formulation, so product choice matters. Mild GI upset at high doses and a weak antiplatelet effect (caution with anticoagulants). Consumer access: curcumin-plus-piperine capsules from any supplement retailer, taken daily with a fatty meal; months-long horizon, positioned as prevention of further greying rather than reversal.
Novelty rationale
Enzyme-restoration strategy (Nrf2 → catalase/GPx upregulation) is mechanistically upstream of the direct-scavenging incumbents H001/H002/H013 — a distinct and complementary way to attack the same peroxide lesion. Not an established hair remedy and absent from the current leaders.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 8/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict revise. The mechanistic logic is the cleanest of this batch — Nrf2-driven catalase/GPx restoration attacks the documented H2O2/catalase lesion rather than scavenging stoichiometrically — but it founders on exposure. Curcumin's oral bioavailability is notoriously poor (the hypothesis admits this), and nothing shows that even piperine-boosted or phytosome formulations achieve follicular concentrations sufficient for Nrf2 activation in the pigmentary unit; the Nrf2 literature for curcumin is dominated by in-vitro work at micromolar concentrations unreachable in human tissue, and curcumin's reputation for promiscuous in-vitro activity (PAINS/assay-interference concerns) should discount that evidence further. The cited PMID 33598985 is correctly attributed and does document peroxide-driven MelSC depletion, and the Wood et al. catalase-collapse framing matches the dossier — but neither touches curcumin. There are zero greying or hair-pigment data. To be viable this needs a delivery argument (topical or a formulation with demonstrated skin/follicle levels) and an honest acknowledgment that 'Nrf2 activator' is a claim about cells in dishes, not follicles in scalps.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 2 alopecia trial(s), max phase 0, statuses: COMPLETED, RECRUITING
  • OpenTargets: error — no chembl_id available
  • Novelty: 26 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 0.037)

63. ketoconazole 2% shampoo + leave-on caffeine scalp tonic (scalp oxidative/inflammatory burden-reduction adjunct) (H016) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL157101 · Elo 1499 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (shampoo + leave-on tonic) · origin: evolution (round 1)

Mechanism
Chronic scalp micro-inflammation (Malassezia-driven) and background oxidative stress add to the follicular ROS burden that drives ectopic MelSC differentiation and depletion. This merged adjunct attacks that background burden from two sides: ketoconazole shampoo (2–3x weekly, several minutes of scalp contact) suppresses the Malassezia/inflammatory component, and a leave-on caffeine tonic provides continuous low-grade antioxidant/radical-scavenging exposure — caffeine's PDE/cAMP-melanogenesis claim is dropped per the H007 review as pharmacologically implausible at follicular concentrations. The honest claim is long-horizon slowing of MelSC attrition, not repigmentation.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 establishes that redox and inflammatory stressors deplete MelSCs in ex vivo human follicles — the pathway both components intersect. Both parent critiques are addressed: H007's shaky cAMP/MITF mechanism is removed and the antioxidant rationale is kept but explicitly downgraded to 'speculative pigment effect'; H011's two unproven leaps are acknowledged by reframing as prevention-slowing with an unquantifiable effect size, paired with a measurable endpoint (standardized grey-hair-count photography every 3 months over 12+ months, ideally in scalps with active seborrheic inflammation where the anti-inflammatory component is most plausible). Zero direct greying data exist for either agent; this is the near-zero-risk adjunct tier, not a mechanistically driven candidate. (refs: PMID 33598985)
Safety
Best safety profile in the set: ketoconazole shampoo (1–2% depending on jurisdiction) and caffeine topicals are mass-market OTC with negligible systemic absorption; only local dryness/irritation. Consumer access: anti-dandruff shampoo used 2–3x weekly with several minutes of contact, plus a daily leave-on caffeine tonic (leave-on, not rinse-off, to maximize follicle contact — addressing the contact-time objection in both reviews). Expectation setting: at best slowed progression over a year or more; discontinuation if irritation.
Novelty rationale
Material changes: two separate low-evidence adjuncts are merged into one coherent scalp-burden-reduction regimen; the discredited cAMP-melanogenesis mechanism is deleted rather than reworded; the claim is reframed from implied pigment effect to honest long-horizon prevention with a concrete, measurable endpoint — precisely the salvage conditions both reviewers set. The MelSC-preservation framing for these mass-market ingredients remains non-standard in the hair market.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 9/10, novelty 4/10 — verdict revise. Weakest link is the mechanism-to-citation gap dressed up as resolution. PMID 33598985 is verified in the corpus and does show redox stress (H2O2) and noradrenaline depleting MelSCs ex vivo, but it says nothing about INFLAMMATORY stressors or Malassezia — the entire ketoconazole half of this regimen rests on an inflammatory-greying link that no cited source establishes. The caffeine half is frankly weaker: with the cAMP/MITF claim correctly deleted, what remains is 'low-grade antioxidant exposure' at unquantified follicular concentration — a hand-wave, not a mechanism. Zero direct greying data is admitted, and the proposed measurable endpoint (grey-hair-count photography over 12 months) cannot attribute any slowing to the regimen without a control arm, so the 'honesty' upgrades do not fix the evidentiary vacuum. Ketoconazole + caffeine are the two most shop-worn ingredients in the consumer hair market; the MelSC-preservation framing is a marketing re-skin, not a new idea. Salvageable only as an explicitly optional, near-zero-cost adjunct tier with the inflammatory claim either cited or struck — it should never be ranked against mechanistically driven candidates.

No external validation recorded

64. iron (ferrous sulfate) (H009) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL1200830 · Elo 1499 · confidence 3/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
Low ferritin (iron stores) is associated with premature greying even without anemia. Iron is required for ribonucleotide reductase and the rapid proliferation of melanocyte progeny each anagen, and iron-dependent enzymes participate in melanin synthesis; correcting depleted ferritin restores follicular pigmentary-unit turnover in deficient individuals.
Evidence
The dossier documents the ferritin/iron association with premature canities; PMID 36853923 supplies the framework that greying reflects failed melanocyte regeneration, which iron repletion supports at the proliferative level. No interventional trials for greying exist; expected benefit is confined to confirmed low ferritin. (refs: PMID 36853923)
Safety
Oral ferrous sulfate is cheap and OTC, but iron overload is harmful — supplementation should follow a ferritin blood test. Standard dosing 325 mg daily or every other day (better absorbed, less GI upset) with vitamin C; avoid taking with tea/calcium. Consumer access: pharmacy iron supplements plus a GP-ordered ferritin test; recheck ferritin after ~3 months.
Novelty rationale
Not a hair-drug repurposing story but a targeted deficiency-correction hypothesis; included because ferritin is a frequently missed, cheaply testable contributor and fits the dossier's actionable layer.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 4/10, safety 7/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict revise. Same structural problem as H008: a ferritin-canities association (dossier-only, causality uncertain) dressed up as an intervention, with no interventional evidence that iron repletion restores pigment even in deficient subjects. The mechanistic embroidery (ribonucleotide reductase, 'iron-dependent enzymes in melanin synthesis') is plausible biochemistry but unverified at the follicle level and does not connect iron status to MelSC maintenance, the actual greying lesion per the correctly cited PMID 36853923. Safety is handled responsibly (ferritin-first, alternate-day dosing, recheck), but iron is not benign when dosed blind, and the whole hypothesis hinges on a test most consumers will skip. Novelty is low: ferritin correction for hair complaints is already standard dermatology practice. Acceptable only as part of a mandatory lab-screening bundle alongside B12/vitamin D/thyroid, not as a standalone candidate; as written it overstates a screening item as a repurposing hypothesis.
Validation

  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

65. latanoprost scalp lotion + topical melatonin (dual melanogenesis-stimulation / MelSC-protection regimen) (H027) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL1051 · Elo 1493 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (leave-on) · origin: evolution (round 2)

Mechanism
This regimen merges the run's only demonstrated human repigmentation-adjacent pharmacology with its best-tolerated stem-cell-protection agent, addressing both halves of greying biology simultaneously. Latanoprost, a prostaglandin F2alpha analog, stimulates melanin synthesis in surviving bulb melanocytes via FP-receptor signaling feeding the MITF/tyrosinase program — raising pigment output per anagen where melanocytes remain. Topical melatonin in parallel lowers the follicular ROS burden that drives ectopic MelSC differentiation and niche depletion, slowing exhaustion of the stem-cell reservoir and thereby enlarging and preserving the pool of follicles that can still respond to stimulation. The indication is sharply restricted to early, salt-and-pepper greying with intermixed pigmented hairs — follicles with exhausted MelSCs are explicitly out of scope.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 lays out the MITF/tyrosinase melanogenesis framework; eyelash darkening is an established prostaglandin class effect. PMID 33598985 and 36853923 ground the oxidative MelSC-depletion lesion the melatonin arm targets. Resolves H014's residual critique — that the melanocyte-survival restriction could not be operationalized — by adding a concrete selection and monitoring protocol (below) and by adding the MelSC-protection arm so the regimen no longer depends solely on pre-existing viable melanocytes doing all the work. H001's accept-level caveat (progression-slowing, not repigmentation) is retained for its arm. Remaining honest caveat: lash-to-scalp extrapolation is unproven and no grey-hair trial exists for either agent. (refs: PMID 33320376, PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Microgram-scale topical prostaglandin doses have negligible systemic exposure; the real liability is local skin hyperpigmentation, managed by a part-line dropper/mascara-style applicator confined to hair-bearing greying zones and an explicit stop rule (discontinue if skin staining or irritation appears). Access framed honestly: latanoprost is prescription-only in major markets, obtainable as a cheap generic via a short physician consult; the melatonin arm is OTC. Operational protocol: select a single test zone of early salt-and-pepper greying (minority grey fraction, onset within roughly the last few years), apply both agents nightly, take standardized monthly photographs, and judge response only after 6–9 months of new anagen growth, abandoning non-responding zones. Not for consumers unwilling to see a prescriber.
Novelty rationale
Material changes over H014: a second, mechanistically orthogonal arm (MelSC protection) is added so the regimen targets stem-cell preservation as well as melanin output, and the previously inoperable 'surviving melanocytes only' restriction is turned into an explicit consumer-executable test-zone selection and stop-rule protocol. No existing regimen combines melanogenesis stimulation with follicular antioxidant protection for canities.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 4/10, safety 7/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict revise. PMID check: all three citations (33320376, 33598985, 36853923) exist and are attributed fairly -- 33320376 is a pigmentation-control review covering the MITF/cAMP framework, and 33598985 does demonstrate redox-stress-driven ectopic MelSC differentiation ex vivo in human follicles. Weakest link: the entire regimen rests on a lash-to-scalp extrapolation. Eyelash darkening is a real prostaglandin class effect, but no scalp pigmentation trial exists for latanoprost, and the melatonin arm has zero direct evidence of MelSC preservation in humans -- it is antioxidant inference stacked on antioxidant inference. The 'surviving melanocytes only' restriction is now operationalized as a test-zone protocol, which is the right salvage, but a consumer cannot verify MelSC viability; they can only run a 6-9 month per-zone experiment most of which will fail, because follicles visibly greying have by definition already lost function. Two further frictions: (1) latanoprost is prescription-only, against the run's stated preference for OTC/cosmetic candidates -- the rationale (only human repigmentation-adjacent signal in the run) is defensible but should be weighed explicitly; (2) local skin hyperpigmentation is not a minor cosmetic nuisance here, it is the drug working as intended on the wrong compartment, and blotchy perifollicular staining on a visible scalp part-line could be worse than the greying. Proceed only as a self-experimentation protocol with the stop rules as written; do not grade it above anecdote-adjacent.
Validation
  • ClinicalTrials.gov: 0 alopecia trial(s), max phase 0, statuses: n/a
  • OpenTargets: MoA targets PTGFR; AGA-associated target overlap: none
  • Novelty: 0 PubMed hair co-mentions (novelty score 1.000)

66. acetylcysteine (H002) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL600 · Elo 1485 · confidence 3/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
Oral N-acetylcysteine (NAC) is a cysteine prodrug that replenishes intracellular glutathione, the dominant cellular antioxidant buffer. Grey/white follicles show collapse of the peroxide-clearing machinery (low catalase, low methionine sulfoxide reductase); systemic NAC raises glutathione availability to follicular melanocytes and melanocyte stem cells, counteracting the oxidative burden that drives ectopic MelSC differentiation and niche depletion.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 shows H2O2 exposure reproduces the greying phenotype (ectopic MelSC pigmentation and depletion) in ex vivo human follicles, establishing oxidative stress as a tractable target. No clinical data link NAC to repigmentation; the hypothesis rests on correcting the documented follicular redox defect. (refs: PMID 33598985)
Safety
NAC is an OTC dietary supplement in the US and a cheap mucolytic elsewhere; 600–1200 mg/day is well tolerated (main complaint: GI upset, sulfur odor). Far more benign than prescription antioxidants. Consumer access: standard supplement capsules from any pharmacy/online retailer, taken daily; realistic expectation is slowing progression rather than reversal, assessed over 6+ months of new hair growth.
Novelty rationale
Not a known hair agent. The glutathione-precursor angle for greying is essentially unproposed in the consumer space, which is dominated by (ineffective) oral catalase products; NAC addresses the same H2O2 lesion through a mechanism that actually survives digestion.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 4/10, safety 7/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict revise. PMID 33598985 is accurately cited for the H2O2 lesion, but there is a large unbridged gap between 'systemic glutathione precursor' and 'raised glutathione at the follicular bulge': oral NAC has poor and variable bioavailability, and no data show follicular MelSC redox rescue from oral dosing. The hypothesis also quietly violates the run constraint — it proposes daily chronic SYSTEMIC dosing for a purely cosmetic indication, which the dossier says should be reserved for compelling cases; correcting a redox defect that may be downstream of MelSC loss is not compelling. The swipe at oral catalase products is fair but does not elevate NAC's own evidence above the same 'rationale only' tier. Salvageable if reframed as topical NAC/NAC-ester formulation or explicitly deprioritized behind topical candidates.

67. bimatoprost (H005) Known hair-related use —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL1200963 · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
Bimatoprost is a prostaglandin F2α analog; prostaglandin analogs stimulate melanogenesis in follicular melanocytes (established clinically as eyelash darkening/iris hyperpigmentation), plausibly via FP-receptor signaling that upregulates tyrosinase/MITF activity in the pigmentary unit. Applied to the scalp, it could increase melanin synthesis by surviving melanocytes and promote repigmentation of hairs from follicles that retain viable MelSCs.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 lays out the MITF/tyrosinase melanogenesis program that prostaglandin signaling feeds into. Drug-induced repigmentation with prostaglandin analogs (eyelash darkening) is an established on-label-adjacent effect noted in the dossier; scalp grey-hair repigmentation is anecdote-grade and untested in trials. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
Topical use has a benign systemic profile (ocular doses are microgram-scale); local effects include skin hyperpigmentation and irritation — cosmetically relevant but not dangerous. Access is semi-OTC in practice: bimatoprost 0.03% is sold as the cosmetic eyelash serum Latisse (prescription in the US, cosmetic/online channels elsewhere); a consumer would apply the serum to greying scalp/hairline areas nightly. Cost and off-label status are the main barriers.
Novelty rationale
Known for lash growth/darkening, not for scalp greying; this extends an established follicular melanogenesis signal to the canities indication — flagged as prior hair-related use, but the grey-hair application is genuinely untested.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 5/10, safety 6/10, novelty 6/10 — verdict revise. PMID 33320376 is accurately cited as a melanogenesis-framework review, and eyelash darkening is a genuinely established prostaglandin effect, making this the only entry with a demonstrated human repigmentation-adjacent pharmacology. The fatal-flaw candidate is the target mismatch: bimatoprost stimulates melanin SYNTHESIS in existing melanocytes, but greying is primarily a melanocyte/MelSC LOSS phenotype (36853923) — follicles that have exhausted their MelSCs have nothing to stimulate, so the addressable population is only early/salt-and-pepper greying with surviving bulb melanocytes, and the hypothesis should say so sharply. Second weakness: access is oversold as 'semi-OTC' — it is prescription-only in major markets, expensive for scalp-scale daily use, and scalp skin hyperpigmentation is a cosmetically perverse side effect for a hair-color product. Revise to narrow the target population and address cost/regulatory access honestly.

68. L-tyrosine (H019) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL925 · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 2)

Mechanism
L-tyrosine is the obligate substrate of tyrosinase, the rate-limiting enzyme of melanogenesis (tyrosine → DOPA → dopaquinone → melanin). In follicles that retain viable melanocytes but produce fading pigment, substrate insufficiency could limit melanin output per hair; ensuring tyrosine sufficiency supports maximal flux through the MITF-driven melanogenesis program. Rationale is strongest for marginal-protein diets or high-stress states that consume catecholamine precursor pools.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 details the MITF/tyrosinase melanogenesis program for which tyrosine is the substrate. The dossier grades tyrosine as 'supplementation rationale only (uncertain)' — no trial has shown repigmentation, and substrate is rarely limiting in well-nourished individuals. This is a cheap, low-risk mechanistic bet, not an evidence-backed therapy. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
OTC amino acid, inexpensive, generally recognized as safe at 500–2000 mg/day; side effects (GI upset, headache) are mild. Caution with hyperthyroidism (tyrosine is a thyroid-hormone precursor), MAO inhibitors, and levodopa. Consumer access: standard amino-acid capsules from any supplement store, taken in the morning (mildly alerting); effect, if any, visible only over months of new growth on follicles with surviving melanocytes.
Novelty rationale
Substrate-side support of melanogenesis is orthogonal to the copper incumbent (enzyme cofactor, H004) and to all antioxidant incumbents; it addresses the output side of surviving melanocytes rather than stem-cell protection. Rarely proposed outside grey-hair folklore, and not among the current leaders.
Reviews
  • plausibility 3/10, evidence 2/10, safety 9/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict revise. The substrate-sufficiency argument collapses on its own admission: tyrosine is a non-essential amino acid that is rarely rate-limiting in anyone eating adequate protein, and the dossier explicitly grades it 'supplementation rationale only (uncertain)' with zero repigmentation trials. Melanocyte tyrosinase flux in greying follicles is limited by melanocyte loss and oxidative enzyme damage, not by systemic tyrosine supply; flooding the substrate pool does nothing for follicles whose melanocytes are gone or whose catalase has collapsed. Cited PMID 33320376 is correctly attributed but it is a general pigmentation review — it establishes that tyrosine is the melanin substrate, which nobody disputes, and provides no support for supplementation changing hair pigment. This is grey-hair folklore dressed in pathway language. The only defensible version is targeted repletion in documented marginal-protein or deficiency states, which makes it a nutritional-correction footnote rather than a repurposing hypothesis.
Validation

69. latanoprost (H006) Known hair-related use —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL1051 · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
Latanoprost, the prostaglandin F2α analog glaucoma drop, shares bimatoprost's melanogenesis-stimulating pharmacology (FP-receptor agonism increasing tyrosinase/MITF-driven melanin synthesis in melanocytes). Scalp application could drive pigment production in follicles with surviving melanocytes, and prostaglandin signaling may also support the anagen environment the melanocyte stem cell niche depends on.

Same melanogenesis framework as bimatoprost (PMID 33320376). The dossier lists latanoprost/bimatoprost eyelash darkening as an established repigmentation signal, with applicability to scalp grey hair uncertain. No grey-hair trials exist. (refs: PMID 33320376)

Very benign systemic exposure at topical doses; risks are local (irritation, unwanted skin darkening). Prescription-only as eye drops, but latanoprost is a cheap, widely available generic; a consumer would need an off-label prescription or use ophthalmic solution applied to scalp — weaker practical access than bimatoprost's cosmetic-serum channel, hence flagged. Included as the lower-cost prostaglandin alternative with a compelling rationale and benign profile per the dossier's Rx exception clause.
Novelty rationale
Known hair-adjacent agent (lash darkening) with no established grey-hair use; proposing it as a distinct, cheap generic route to the same melanogenesis target rather than duplicating bimatoprost's branded channel.

Practical delivery is also dodged: using ophthalmic solution on the scalp is an awkward, costly-per-ml off-label hack, and periocular-style risks (periorbital/skin hyperpigmentation, irritation) on visible scalp skin are cosmetic liabilities. Within the dossier's Rx exception clause it is defensible as the cheap generic PG route, but it needs a concrete scalp formulation/dosing plan and honest framing that repigmentation would be limited to follicles with surviving melanocytes.

70. ketoconazole (H011) Known hair-related use —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL157101 · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
Topical ketoconazole is an antifungal with anti-inflammatory and mild antioxidant properties on the scalp. Chronic scalp micro-inflammation and Malassezia-driven oxidative stress add to the follicular ROS burden that drives melanocyte stem cell ectopic differentiation; reducing this background inflammatory/oxidative load could slow MelSC depletion and preserve pigment longer.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 establishes that redox/inflammatory stressors deplete MelSCs in human follicles ex vivo — the pathway ketoconazole's scalp anti-inflammatory action intersects. Direct greying evidence is absent; ketoconazole's hair data concern AGA. This is a background-burden-reduction hypothesis. (refs: PMID 33598985)
Safety

Consumer access: anti-dandruff shampoo (e.g., Nizoral) used 2–3 times weekly with several minutes of scalp contact before rinsing.
Novelty rationale

Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 9/10, novelty 4/10 — verdict revise. PMID 33598985 is real and correctly cited: it demonstrates MelSC ectopic differentiation/depletion under redox and noradrenergic stress ex vivo, so the pathway anchor is legitimate. The novelty framing is also overstated - ketoconazole is a well-worn hair-market ingredient; only the MelSC-preservation rationale is new. 71. methoxsalen (H012) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL416 · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical + UVA phototherapy · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
Methoxsalen (8-MOP) plus UVA (PUVA) is an established repigmentation tool in vitiligo: photoactivated psoralen stimulates melanocyte proliferation and migration from hair follicle reservoirs into depigmented skin, and upregulates the MITF/tyrosinase melanogenesis program. Applied carefully to greying scalp, follicular PUVA could reactivate residual melanocyte/MelSC populations and drive repigmentation of new hairs.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 covers UV-driven pigmentation control and the vitiligo depigmentation model where PUVA's repigmentation efficacy is established; the dossier lists PUVA as a repigmentation tool with uncertain applicability to grey scalp hair. Evidence for greying specifically is anecdote-grade. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
Prescription-only and NOT consumer-friendly: systemic/topical psoralen is phototoxic, requires controlled UVA dosing, and carries cumulative photocarcinogenesis risk — included under the dossier's Rx exception only because its repigmentation mechanism is the most clinically proven in the list. Real-world access: dermatologist-supervised phototherapy clinic with topical psoralen paint + targeted UVA to scalp; not a self-administered option.
Novelty rationale
Established in vitiligo but essentially unexplored for canities; the follicular-reservoir repigmentation mechanism maps directly onto the surviving-MelSC requirement of grey-hair reversal.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 4/10, safety 2/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict reject. PMID 33320376 is real but is a general pigmentation/UV review, not PUVA efficacy data for greying - the citation supports the tanning/vitiligo background, nothing specific to canities, so 'evidence' is anecdote-grade as the hypothesis itself admits. More fundamentally, the vitiligo analogy is flawed: vitiligo follicles retain a viable melanocyte reservoir that PUVA can mobilize, whereas grey follicles are defined by MelSC exhaustion/mis-localization and depletion - there may be nothing left to photoactivate, exactly the failure mode the dossier's NYU McSC finding predicts. Delivery is also dubious: scalp hair shafts attenuate UVA, making controlled follicular dosing impractical outside shaved/short-hair scenarios. The decisive problem, though, is the run's own constraint: this is a cosmetic indication with zero tolerance for systemic side effects, and PUVA carries cumulative photocarcinogenesis risk, phototoxicity, and mandatory dermatologist-supervised clinic administration - the opposite of the dossier's stated OTC/benign-safety preference. The dossier's 'Rx exception' requires a compelling rationale AND benign safety; photocarcinogenesis fails the second leg outright. Novel and mechanistically interesting, but fatally mismatched to the indication's safety bar.
Validation

No external validation recorded

72. lab-confirmed micronutrient repletion panel: cobalamin (B12), ferrous sulfate, cholecalciferol (vitamin D3), with copper only under physician-confirmed deficiency (H015) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1484 · confidence 4/5 · route: oral (test-and-correct repletion) · origin: evolution (round 1)

Mechanism
Premature canities is associated with low B12, low ferritin, low vitamin D, and (more weakly) low copper. Rather than four standalone 'repurposing' hypotheses, this is a single test-and-correct intervention: a blood panel (B12 ± pernicious-anemia workup, ferritin, 25-OH vitamin D; serum copper/ceruloplasmin only if clinically indicated) followed by targeted repletion of confirmed deficiencies. The shared mechanism is removal of metabolic bottlenecks on the rapid proliferation and differentiation of melanocyte-lineage cells each anagen — B12 and iron support DNA synthesis in transit-amplifying progeny, vitamin D receptor signaling modulates melanocyte biology, and copper is the tyrosinase cofactor — restoring the conditions for normal pigmentary-unit turnover in the deficient subset only.
Evidence
PMID 36853923 provides the framework that greying reflects failed melanocyte-lineage regeneration (it is cited only for that framework, not — as the H003 review rightly objected — as evidence that B12 supports MelSCs); the B12/ferritin/vitamin D associations and repigmentation-after-correction case reports live in the dossier and are graded association/case-report level. All four parent critiques are resolved structurally: each reviewer said these were association-grade screening items dressed as interventions and belonged in a mandatory lab-workup bundle — that is exactly what this is. Explicit negative-control expectation: in the non-deficient majority, the panel is a screening recommendation with no expected pigment effect, and blind supplementation is not proposed. H004's standalone rejection is honored by demoting copper to physician-supervised, deficiency-confirmed correction only. (refs: PMID 36853923)
Safety
Replaces unsupervised self-supplementation with a test-first protocol, which resolves every parent safety critique: B12 is benign at 1000 mcg/day orally (injection if malabsorption); iron only after confirmed low ferritin, alternate-day 325 mg with vitamin C, recheck at ~3 months; vitamin D 1000–2000 IU/day titrated to 25-OH-D; copper never dosed blind (narrow window, hepatic toxicity, zinc antagonism) — only via clinician-confirmed deficiency. Consumer access: one GP visit for the panel, then OTC repletion per result. Any pigment effect requires months of new anagen growth and is expected only in confirmed-deficient individuals.
Novelty rationale
The material change is architectural, not cosmetic: four weak standalone association hypotheses (three 'revise', one 'reject') are collapsed into the single mandatory lab-screening bundle their reviewers each prescribed, with citation overreach corrected (36853923 no longer used to imply B12–MelSC support) and copper's unsafe standalone form eliminated. As a unified, deficiency-conditional protocol with an explicit negative-control expectation it is the highest-certainty actionable tier — documented human repigmentation after deficiency correction — rather than four overclaimed interventions.
Reviews
  • plausibility 7/10, evidence 5/10, safety 9/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict accept. PMID 36853923 verifies in the corpus and the hypothesis now cites it only for the MelSC-regeneration framework, which is exactly what that review supports — the prior citation overreach (B12-to-MelSC) is genuinely corrected. This is the highest-certainty actionable item in the set: deficiency associations for B12/ferritin/vitamin D are replicated, repigmentation-after-correction case reports exist, and the test-first architecture eliminates the blind-supplementation safety critique, including the correct demotion of copper to clinician-confirmed deficiency only. The skeptical points are structural, not fatal. First, this is not drug repurposing — it is standard medical screening wearing a hypothesis costume, and its 'novelty rationale' is essentially that four weak ideas were merged into a checklist; novelty score reflects that. Second, the causal arrow remains unproven: the associations are cross-sectional, deficiency correction normalizes labs reliably but repigmentation only anecdotally, and the hypothesis concedes no expected pigment effect in the non-deficient majority — meaning for most users this is a workup, not a treatment. Third, iron repletion details (alternate-day 325 mg) should be clinician-directed rather than consumer-protocol. None of this undermines the recommendation; it should be accepted as the mandatory baseline workup tier, explicitly labeled as screening rather than intervention.
Validation

No external validation recorded

73. ashwagandha (H018) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 2)

Mechanism
Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera) is an adaptogen that reduces perceived stress and lowers cortisol in randomized trials. Chronic psychosocial stress sustains sympathetic/noradrenergic tone, and noradrenaline at the follicle drives melanocyte stem cell ectopic differentiation and niche depletion. Damping the systemic stress axis upstream of the follicle could slow stress-mediated MelSC loss — the behavioral-stress contribution to premature greying that no topical antioxidant addresses.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 shows noradrenaline depletes MelSCs in ex vivo human hair follicles, and PMID 36853923 frames MelSC depletion as the core greying lesion; the dossier notes the stress–greying link is solid in animals, uncertain in humans. Ashwagandha's stress/cortisol reduction is supported by small human RCTs outside the hair field. Direct greying evidence: none — this is a two-step mechanistic inference. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Fully OTC and widely available (standardized KSM-66-type extracts, 300–600 mg/day). Generally well tolerated; rare idiosyncratic liver injury has been reported, and it can raise thyroid hormone levels — avoid in liver disease and use caution with thyroid disorders or sedatives. Consumer access: buy a standardized extract from any supplement retailer, take daily with food; treat as a long-horizon adjunct, not a repigmentation therapy, and expect at most slowed progression over months.
Novelty rationale
Targets the neuroendocrine stress axis systemically rather than follicular oxidative burden — complementary to, not duplicative of, the melatonin/NAC antioxidant incumbents. Not an established greying remedy and absent from the current leaders.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 6/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict revise. A two-step inference where both steps are weak. Step one: the stress-greying link is solid only in mice; the dossier itself grades the human link 'uncertain', and cited PMID 33598985 is an ex vivo surrogate model showing ectopic MelSC differentiation under noradrenaline exposure, not evidence that human psychosocial stress greys hair at achievable follicular noradrenaline levels. Step two: ashwagandha's cortisol reduction comes from small, short, industry-adjacent RCTs measuring perceived stress, and no work connects that systemic endocrine tweak to follicular MelSC preservation. The effect size chain (modest cortisol reduction -> uncertain sympathetic change at the follicle -> unproven human stress-greying pathway) compounds to near-zero expected signal. Safety is not benign enough to excuse this: rare but documented idiosyncratic liver injury and thyroid-hormone elevation are exactly the systemic liabilities this run's cosmetic constraint says to avoid. Citations check out (33598985 and 36853923 exist and are used correctly), but they support a mouse/ex vivo mechanism, not this intervention.
Validation

No external validation recorded

74. PABA (para-aminobenzoic acid) (H020) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 2)

Mechanism
PABA is the historical anti-grey-hair supplement: clinical reports from the 1940s described repigmentation of grey hair at high daily doses, with hair re-greying on discontinuation — a reversible pharmacodynamic pattern consistent with a real (if unexplained) effect on melanocyte pigment output, possibly via support of the melanogenesis program or folate-related one-carbon metabolism in the pigmentary unit. Mechanism remains unresolved; the interest is the empirical human signal, unique in this candidate universe.
Evidence
The dossier documents the old repigmentation reports at high doses alongside safety concerns and grades PABA 'uncertain'. PMID 33320376 supplies the melanogenesis framework any repigmenting effect would act through. No modern controlled trials exist; the 1940s data are anecdote-grade by current standards. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
Available OTC in some countries as a B-complex adjunct. The historical repigmentation doses (multi-gram/day) carried real risks — nausea, hepatic toxicity, blood dyscrasias — so only low doses (roughly 100–400 mg/day) are defensible, which may sit below any effective exposure. Consumer access: PABA capsules are sold online; use low dose only, take with food, and stop on any GI or hepatic symptoms. Honest expectation: weakest benefit/risk ratio of the OTC entries here — included as a flagged anecdote-grade lead, not a recommendation.
Novelty rationale
The only candidate in the universe with actual historical human repigmentation reports specifically for grey hair — a genuinely different evidentiary niche from all incumbents, which are mechanism-only. Presented with full caveats rather than as established efficacy.
Reviews
  • plausibility 3/10, evidence 2/10, safety 3/10, novelty 8/10 — verdict reject. The fatal flaw is the dose-toxicity overlap: the 1940s repigmentation reports used multi-gram daily doses that carried nausea, hepatic toxicity, and blood dyscrasias, while the hypothesis itself concedes that only ~100-400 mg/day is defensible — an exposure likely below any effect. A candidate whose plausible effective dose is its toxic dose has no viable window, and this run's constraint is explicit: cosmetic indication, zero tolerance for systemic side effects. The evidence is 80-year-old anecdote-grade clinical observation with no controlled follow-up and no resolved mechanism; the cited PMID 33320376 merely supplies the generic melanogenesis framework and says nothing about PABA. The unique historical human signal is genuinely interesting as a lead for mechanism research, but as a consumer-actionable hypothesis it fails the safety bar outright. Honest framing cannot rescue an unusable therapeutic window.
