75. tocotrienols (vitamin E) (H021) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL3989727 · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 2)

Mechanism
Tocotrienols and tocopherols are chain-breaking lipid-phase antioxidants that terminate lipid peroxidation in cell membranes. Grey follicles accumulate millimolar hydrogen peroxide with collapsed catalase, and peroxide-driven membrane damage contributes to melanocyte and MelSC death. A membrane-permeant antioxidant protects the lipid compartment that water-phase defenders (glutathione/NAC) and even melatonin only partially cover, preserving melanocyte viability and pigment output across anagen cycles.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 documents peroxide-driven ectopic MelSC differentiation and depletion in human follicles; PMID 36853923 frames MelSC loss as the central greying lesion. The dossier notes antioxidant topicals have a coherent H2O2/catalase rationale but lack direct greying evidence. Flag: oral tocotrienols already have a small AGA growth trial — prior hair-related use, but for a growth endpoint, not pigment. (refs: PMID 33598985, PMID 36853923)
Safety
Fully OTC: oral tocotrienol/tocopherol softgels (keep at or below ~200 IU/day — high-dose vitamin E carries debated bleeding and all-cause-mortality signals) or cosmetic-grade vitamin-E scalp oils. Systemic risk at these doses is minimal. Consumer access: any supplement retailer for capsules, or vitamin-E oil applied to the scalp as a leave-on a few nights per week; expect, at best, slowed progression over months.
Novelty rationale
Covers the membrane lipid phase of oxidative defense — a compartment the melatonin and NAC incumbents (H001/H002/H013) address only indirectly — so it complements rather than rewords them. Its AGA trial history is flagged; the anti-greying membrane-protection angle is new to this run.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 7/10, novelty 4/10 — verdict revise. Delivery is the weakest link: the hypothesis proposes oral dosing for a follicular membrane-protection effect, but there is no evidence oral tocotrienols reach the follicular pigmentary unit at concentrations that alter lipid peroxidation locally, and the token mention of topical vitamin-E scalp oil comes with no penetration or follicular-uptake data. Novelty is also overstated — vitamin E is among the most well-worn antioxidant proposals in the skin/hair literature, the dossier lists vitamin E derivatives in the existing antioxidant-topical landscape, and the hypothesis concedes a prior AGA growth trial, so 'new to this run' is doing heavy lifting. Citations are accurate (33598985 does document peroxide-driven ectopic MelSC differentiation ex vivo; 36853923 is a MelSC/greying review), but both support the disease mechanism, not tocotrienol efficacy against it. The <=200 IU/day framing ducks the debated high-dose vitamin E mortality/bleeding signal rather than engaging with whether effective local concentrations require more. Salvageable only as a topical formulation hypothesis with a stated penetration rationale.
Validation

No external validation recorded

76. selenium (H023) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: n/a · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 2)

Mechanism
Selenium is the essential cofactor of the glutathione peroxidase (GPx) family, the principal enzymatic disposal system for hydrogen peroxide and lipid hydroperoxides alongside catalase. Marginal selenium status lowers GPx activity and would compound the peroxide burden documented in grey follicles, where catalase is already collapsed — the pigmentary unit then depends disproportionately on GPx. Correcting insufficiency restores this second line of enzymatic peroxide defense for melanocytes and MelSCs.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 documents the peroxide-driven MelSC lesion in human follicles, and the dossier describes the catalase collapse that makes the GPx axis the remaining enzymatic defense. The dossier simultaneously warns selenium has a narrow window — both deficiency and excess damage hair. No greying trials exist; this is cofactor repletion with a defined mechanistic role, not a demonstrated repigmentation therapy. (refs: PMID 33598985)
Safety
OTC but the narrowest therapeutic window of any entry here: supplement only 50–100 mcg/day, count dietary intake (one Brazil nut can supply ~50–90 mcg), and never approach the ~400 mcg/day upper limit — selenosis itself causes hair loss and nail changes. Ideally guided by a blood test. Consumer access: low-dose selenium tablets or selenium-yeast from any pharmacy; the test-and-correct framing is mandatory, not optional, for this candidate.
Novelty rationale
Targets the GPx enzymatic axis — mechanistically distinct from direct radical scavengers (melatonin, NAC incumbents) and from the B12/iron/vitamin-D repletion panel incumbent (H015), which does not include selenium. Explicitly framed as deficiency-correction, a different hypothesis class than blind antioxidant dosing.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 3/10, safety 5/10, novelty 5/10 — verdict revise. The GPx-axis reasoning is biochemically coherent and PMID 33598985 is a legitimate citation (it does document hydrogen-peroxide-driven MelSC depletion in ex vivo human follicles), but the evidentiary chain stops at 'selenium is a GPx cofactor' — there is zero evidence that follicular selenium or GPx activity is limiting in greying hair, and the dossier's lesion is catalase/MSR collapse plus McSC loss, which GPx upregulation cannot repair. Weakest link: this is deficiency-correction hypothesized against an unmeasured deficiency; in selenium-replete individuals supplementation does nothing for pigment while actively risking harm, since selenosis itself causes hair loss and nail dystrophy — the one candidate in the run whose toxicity phenocopies the indication. The narrow therapeutic window (~50-100 mcg effective vs ~400 mcg upper limit, with high dietary variability from nuts/seafood) makes unsupervised OTC use for a cosmetic goal hard to defend. Novelty is overstated: antioxidant/cofactor repletion for greying is well-worn supplement-aisle territory, and the claimed distinction from the H015 repletion panel is administrative, not mechanistic. Salvageable only if reframed strictly as test-and-correct for confirmed low selenium status.
Validation

No external validation recorded

77. afamelanotide (H024) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL441738 · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: subcutaneous implant (specialist-administered) · origin: generation (round 2)

Mechanism
Afamelanotide is a synthetic alpha-MSH analog and potent agonist of the melanocortin 1 receptor (MC1R). MC1R → cAMP → MITF is the canonical master switch of melanogenesis: UV tanning works through POMC-derived alpha-MSH engaging this same receptor. Systemic MC1R agonism raises MITF-driven melanin synthesis in every melanocyte, including surviving follicular melanocytes — making afamelanotide the most direct pharmacological melanogenesis stimulator in the candidate universe.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 lays out the complete UV → POMC → alpha-MSH → MC1R → MITF tanning pathway that afamelanotide pharmacologically mimics, and notes MC1R variants' strong pigmentation phenotypes — human genetic proof of the pathway's centrality. Afamelanotide is approved (erythropoietic protoporphyria) precisely because it demonstrably increases human skin pigmentation. Grey-hair repigmentation is untested; effect would require follicles retaining viable melanocytes. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
Prescription-only subcutaneous implant (Scenesse) administered in specialty clinics; adverse effects are mostly nausea, flushing, and fatigue, plus generalized skin darkening — a cosmetic consequence rather than organ toxicity. It is expensive and NOT realistically consumer-obtainable, which under this run's OTC-preferring constraint is close to disqualifying: included only under the dossier's 'compelling rationale AND benign profile' prescription exception, as the mechanistic benchmark for MC1R-agonist repigmentation. No legitimate OTC channel exists — consumers should not source research-chemical melanotan analogs, which are unsafe and unregulated.
Novelty rationale
Direct receptor-level MC1R agonism is orthogonal to every incumbent — the caffeine incumbent (H007) only weakly raises cAMP downstream of the receptor, and the prostaglandin incumbents act through FP signaling. Afamelanotide has no prior hair indication; it anchors the receptor-agonist strategy class for the tournament.
Reviews
  • plausibility 6/10, evidence 5/10, safety 5/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict revise. PMID 33320376 is accurately cited — it lays out the UV/POMC/alpha-MSH/MC1R/MITF axis and the vitiligo/phototherapy context exactly as claimed, and afamelanotide's EPP approval is genuine human proof that systemic MC1R agonism increases skin pigmentation. But the hypothesis commits a compartment error: afamelanotide darkens skin because epidermal melanocytes persist; grey follicles have lost McSCs and often melanocytes entirely (per the 33598985/dossier biology), so the 'most direct melanogenesis stimulator' has no substrate to stimulate in established canities — it would at best darken follicles that were going to pigment anyway. Second weak link: it fails the run's own access constraint on every axis — prescription-only, specialist-implanted, expensive, with systemic generalized darkening (an off-target cosmetic side effect for someone wanting scalp repigmentation) and no legitimate consumer channel; the authors concede this, which raises the question of why it competes against OTC candidates at all. Keep it only as the explicitly-labeled mechanistic benchmark for the MC1R-agonist class (useful for anchoring topical/cosmetic alpha-MSH-mimetic peptides like palmitoyl tetrapeptide that DO satisfy the constraint), not as a trial candidate.
Validation

No external validation recorded

78. misoprostol (H030) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL606 · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical (compounded) · origin: generation (round 3)

Mechanism
Misoprostol is a prostaglandin E1 analog. PGE-series prostaglandins signal through EP2/EP4 receptors, which are Gs-coupled and raise cAMP in melanocytes, feeding the canonical cAMP -> MITF -> tyrosinase melanogenesis switch (the same second-messenger pathway MC1R uses). This is mechanistically orthogonal to the run's FP-receptor agonists (latanoprost/bimatoprost, H005/H006/H014/H027): PGE2 is an established autocrine stimulator of melanocyte dendricity and melanin synthesis in skin, so a scalp EP-axis agonist could raise pigment output per anagen in follicles that retain viable melanocytes.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 lays out the receptor-Gs-cAMP-MITF melanogenesis framework that EP2/EP4 agonism engages. The dossier notes prostaglandin-analog repigmentation signals (eyelash darkening) as a class; direct PGE2-stimulates-melanogenesis data are outside the provided excerpts, so this is mechanism-level plausibility at anecdote-adjacent grade, same evidence ceiling as the FP-agonist incumbents. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
Oral misoprostol is a cheap generic (prescription in most countries, near-OTC in some) with a well-characterized profile; topical microgram-scale dosing would have minimal systemic exposure, but NO commercial topical formulation exists — it requires a compounding pharmacy (e.g., 0.001-0.01% in a leave-on scalp vehicle). Main cautions: local irritation, and strict avoidance of systemic exposure in pregnancy (abortifacient). Consumer access: a short dermatologist consult for a compounded prescription, applied nightly to greying zones; any pigment effect needs months of new anagen growth.
Novelty rationale
No incumbent targets the EP/cAMP prostaglandin axis; all prostaglandin hypotheses so far are FP agonists. Misoprostol is not an established hair treatment in any form, and its melanogenesis rationale rests on a different receptor family and second messenger than the latanoprost line.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 3/10, novelty 7/10 — verdict reject. PMID check: the single citation, 33320376, exists and does lay out the Gs-coupled MC1R -> cAMP -> MITF framework -- but it says nothing about EP2/EP4 prostaglandin receptors or misoprostol; the hypothesis admits the PGE2-melanogenesis data are 'outside the provided excerpts,' so the evidentiary floor here is one framework review plus extrapolation. That is weaker than the FP-agonist incumbents, which at least have a human class-effect signal (eyelash darkening). The decisive problem is safety for this indication. Misoprostol is an abortifacient. There is no commercial topical formulation, so transdermal absorption from a compounded scalp vehicle is uncharacterized -- you cannot assert 'microgram-scale, minimal systemic exposure' for a formulation that has never been studied, on a drug class where uterotonic systemic exposure in women of childbearing potential is a catastrophic failure mode for a COSMETIC product. 'Strict avoidance in pregnancy' is not an adequate control for a leave-on cosmetic used nightly for 6-9 months. Meanwhile the run already contains FP-axis prostaglandin candidates (H027) with a better human signal, better-defined topical dosing, and no reproductive-toxicity label. Choosing a worse-characterized receptor axis with a worse safety label buys novelty and nothing else. If the EP axis is worth pursuing, it is a preclinical PK/formulation project, not a consumer-facing repurposing candidate.
Validation

No external validation recorded

79. folate (folic acid) (H032) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL1622 · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 3)

Mechanism
Folate, with B12, drives remethylation of homocysteine to methionine. Elevated homocysteine is pro-oxidant, and the defining biochemical lesion of grey follicles includes impaired repair of oxidized methionine (low methionine sulfoxide reductase) alongside collapsed catalase — so a folate-replete, low-homocysteine state reduces one specific oxidative pressure on the melanocyte stem cell niche. The dossier lists folate among the deficiencies associated with premature canities.
Evidence
PMID 33598985 demonstrates that redox stress drives ectopic differentiation and depletion of melanocyte stem cells in ex vivo human hair follicles — the lesion the homocysteine-lowering axis targets. The folate-canities link itself is association-grade (dossier); no interventional trial exists. Crucially, both incumbent micronutrient protocols (H015, H028) omit folate, and standalone B12 (H003) does not cover the folate/homocysteine arm. (refs: PMID 33598985)
Safety
Fully OTC and benign: 400 ug-1 mg folic acid daily from any pharmacy or supermarket, pennies per dose. One real caveat — high-dose folate can mask B12 deficiency (hematologically) while neuropathy progresses, so test B12 alongside before supplementing, which fits this run's lab-guided ethos. Consumer protocol: basic blood panel (folate, B12, homocysteine if available), supplement only if low or borderline, recheck at ~3 months; pigment effects require months of new anagen growth.
Novelty rationale
Not a repackaged panel: folate is the one dossier-listed deficiency absent from both incumbent micronutrient hypotheses, and its homocysteine/oxidized-methionine-repair rationale ties directly to the named grey-follicle lesion rather than generic deficiency correction.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 9/10, novelty 3/10 — verdict revise. The fatal weakness is novelty, not safety: this is generic deficiency correction wearing a mechanistic costume. The dossier itself already lists folate among the deficiencies associated with premature canities and already recommends 'correct confirmed deficiencies'; the hypothesis's boast that incumbents H015 and H028 omit folate cuts both ways — it may reflect a deliberate judgment that folate is the weakest association in the panel, not a gap waiting to be filled. The mechanism chain is long and indirect: folate -> homocysteine remethylation -> reduced pro-oxidant tone -> less pressure on methionine-sulfoxide repair in McSCs. Every arrow is plausible and none is demonstrated for greying; there is no interventional evidence that folate repletion repigments or slows greying even in deficient individuals. Cited PMID 33598985 is verified in the corpus and does show redox-stress-driven MelSC depletion ex vivo, but it implicates H2O2/noradrenaline/irradiation, not homocysteine — the citation grounds the lesion, not the folate lever. To its credit the safety profile is excellent, the B12-masking caveat is handled correctly (test B12 alongside), and the lab-guided 'supplement only if low' protocol matches the run's ethos. Salvageable only if repositioned honestly as 'complete the dossier's deficiency panel under lab guidance' rather than a distinct mechanistic hypothesis.
Validation

No external validation recorded

80. CALCITRIOL (H033) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL846 · Elo 1484 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 3)

Mechanism
Calcitriol (1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D3) is the active vitamin D hormone. The vitamin D receptor is expressed in melanocytes and follicular keratinocytes, and VDR signaling modulates melanocyte survival and differentiation; topical vitamin D analogs (calcipotriol/calcitriol class) are used adjunctively in vitiligo repigmentation regimens, where the goal — coaxing pigment production back in a depigmented unit — is conceptually adjacent to grey-hair repigmentation. Direct follicular VDR agonism differs mechanistically from oral cholecalciferol repletion (H008): it delivers the active ligand to the pigmentary unit regardless of systemic deficiency status.
Evidence
PMID 36853923 grounds melanocyte/MelSC loss as the driver of greying that VDR-modulated melanocyte survival would counteract. The vitiligo-adjunct rationale and the vitamin D-canities association (dossier) are outside the provided excerpts and association-grade; no greying trial exists for any vitamin D formulation. (refs: PMID 36853923)
Safety
Calcitriol ointment (3 ug/g, e.g., Vectical/Silkis) is prescription in the US but available cheaply or OTC in several markets; topical use at these concentrations has negligible effect on systemic calcium, with local irritation as the main adverse effect. Consumer access: request a calcitriol ointment prescription from a GP/dermatologist (or purchase where OTC), apply a thin layer to greying scalp zones once daily; judge only after 6+ months of new hair growth.
Novelty rationale
Distinct from incumbent H008 (oral cholecalciferol, deficiency-correction framing): this is active-hormone receptor agonism delivered directly to the follicle, borrowing the vitiligo-repigmentation playbook — a delivery and mechanism change, not a rewording.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 7/10, novelty 4/10 — verdict revise. The vitiligo analogy is the weakest link and it is shakier than presented: vitiligo repigmentation regimens work by suppressing autoimmune melanocyte destruction and coaxing reservoir melanocytes to repopulate an epidermis that still has viable follicular melanocytes; premature greying is McSC pool attrition and mis-localization, a different lesion, and even in vitiligo topical vitamin D analogs are a modest ADJUNCT to phototherapy, not monotherapy with demonstrated repigmentation power. Nothing provided shows VDR agonism regenerates or reactivates a depleted follicular McSC niche. Cited PMID 36853923 is verified in the corpus but is a narrative review establishing MelSC loss as the driver of greying — it supports the problem statement, not the calcitriol remedy. Redundancy risk is real: incumbent H008 already occupies the vitamin-D-axis-for-canities slot, and 'topical active ligand vs oral prohormone' is a formulation delta on a weakly-supported idea, not a new idea; the hypothesis must make that differentiation load-bearing or it is a rewording. Safety is acceptable for limited scalp areas (hypercalcemia risk is negligible at 3 ug/g on small surfaces), but calcitriol ointment is prescription-only in the US, and this run's constraint allows prescription candidates only with a COMPELLING rationale — 'conceptually adjacent to vitiligo' does not meet that bar. Revise to specify target area limits, duration, and a concrete differentiation from H008, or fold into H008.
Validation

No external validation recorded

81. ruxolitinib (H025) Known hair-related use —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL1789941 · Elo 1483 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 2)

Mechanism
Topical ruxolitinib is a JAK1/JAK2 inhibitor approved for vitiligo repigmentation. Premature greying can accompany vitiligo and other autoimmune states in which IFN-gamma/JAK-STAT signaling drives immune destruction of follicular melanocytes. In that autoimmune-overlap subset, JAK blockade lifts the inflammatory suppression of the pigmentary unit, permitting melanocyte/MelSC recovery and repigmentation — as demonstrated clinically in vitiligo skin.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 describes vitiligo as autoimmune destruction of melanocytes — the lesion topical JAK inhibition reverses in that disease. The dossier lists topical ruxolitinib as a vitiligo repigmentation tool with limited/uncertain applicability to ordinary grey scalp hair, and flags that new/rapid greying warrants an autoimmune workup — precisely the subset this hypothesis addresses. No grey-hair trials exist; the rationale is confined to the vitiligo/autoimmune-overlap niche. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
Prescription cream (Opzelura 1.5%); cutaneous absorption is low and the vitiligo program showed good local tolerability (application-site acne, pruritus), though the label carries the class boxed warning extrapolated from oral JAK inhibitors. Not OTC — access requires a dermatologist, which is appropriate here because the hypothesis only applies after a medical workup identifies autoimmune overlap; for that diagnosed subset the risk/benefit is favorable even under the cosmetic-indication constraint. Not for undifferentiated premature greying.
Novelty rationale
The only anti-autoimmune mechanism in the run — every incumbent targets oxidative burden, melanogenesis stimulation, or nutrition. The methoxsalen/PUVA incumbent (H012) is the phototherapy option for the same vitiligo-overlap niche; ruxolitinib is its targeted-immunology parallel. Flag: JAK inhibitors have known hair-related use (alopecia areata) — the greying application is the new element.
Reviews
  • plausibility 5/10, evidence 3/10, safety 6/10, novelty 6/10 — verdict revise. PMID 33320376 is correctly cited for vitiligo as autoimmune melanocyte destruction, and topical ruxolitinib's vitiligo repigmentation efficacy is real — but that is the entire evidence base, and it transfers to greying only through an unvalidated assumption: that premature canities in a given patient is driven by IFN-gamma/JAK-STAT autoimmunity rather than the dominant oxidative/McSC-exhaustion biology. For ordinary premature greying (the run's actual target) there is no rationale at all — JAK inhibition does nothing against millimolar follicular peroxide or McSC mis-localization — so the hypothesis applies to a small, workup-gated autoimmune-overlap subset it cannot itself identify. The safety framing is also generous: Opzelura carries the JAK-class boxed warning, it is prescription-only with no OTC path, and 'benign because topical' rests on vitiligo trial data in limited body-surface application, not chronic cosmetic scalp use. The novelty claim ('only anti-autoimmune mechanism') is fair, and the alopecia-areata precedent shows JAK inhibitors do act in follicles, which keeps it from rejection — but as written the hypothesis must be narrowed to: dermatologist-confirmed vitiligo/autoimmune-associated greying only, with ordinary canities explicitly out of scope.
Validation

No external validation recorded

82. cupric sulfate (H004) Novel candidate —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL604 · Elo 1468 · confidence 2/5 · route: oral · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
Copper is the essential cofactor of tyrosinase, the rate-limiting enzyme of melanin synthesis in follicular melanocytes. Marginal copper status is associated with premature greying; restoring adequacy supports tyrosinase activity and melanin output per anagen cycle. Cupric sulfate is the approved oral/IV copper salt and a common supplement ingredient.
Evidence
PMID 33320376 details the melanogenesis cascade (α-MSH → MC1R → MITF → tyrosinase-driven melanin synthesis) that copper-dependent tyrosinase sits at the end of. The dossier documents the copper–canities association; interventional evidence for supplementation is absent, so this is a deficiency-support hypothesis. (refs: PMID 33320376)
Safety
Narrow therapeutic window: keep to ~1–2 mg elemental copper/day; chronic excess causes GI upset and hepatic toxicity, and high zinc intake antagonizes copper absorption (avoid concurrent high-dose zinc). Consumer access: OTC multimineral or copper supplements (copper sulfate/gluconate) from pharmacies; ideally confirm low serum copper/ceruloplasmin first. Do not exceed label dose.
Novelty rationale
Copper appears in grey-hair folklore, but a mechanism-targeted, dose-bounded cupric sulfate hypothesis tied to tyrosinase cofactor biochemistry — with explicit safety guardrails — is distinct from the unmonitored 'copper pills' marketing angle.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 4/10, novelty 2/10 — verdict reject. PMID 33320376 is correctly cited for the melanogenesis cascade, but it provides zero support for copper supplementation affecting hair pigment — the actual basis is the folklore-grade copper-canities association plus tyrosinase-cofactor biochemistry, with no interventional evidence at all. The safety profile is the worst in the set for a cosmetic indication: a narrow therapeutic window, hepatic toxicity with chronic excess, zinc antagonism, and a proposal that amounts to unsupervised self-supplementation of a metal with no deficiency testing built in. The claimed novelty ('mechanism-targeted, dose-bounded' framing) is marketing dressing on the same copper pills already sold for grey hair — exactly the angle the hypothesis claims to be distinct from. Confirmed copper deficiency is already covered by generic deficiency-correction workup (as with H003); as a standalone candidate this adds only risk.
Validation

No external validation recorded

83. minoxidil (H010) Established AGA therapy —

ChEMBL: CHEMBL802 · Elo 1468 · confidence 2/5 · route: topical · origin: generation (round 1)

Mechanism
NEW ANGLE on a known hair agent: beyond growth, minoxidil has occasional case reports of hair darkening/repigmentation, and as a potassium-channel opener it prolongs anagen and improves follicular perfusion. A longer anagen gives the melanocyte stem cell niche more regeneration cycles before depletion, and improved blood flow may reduce local oxidative stress — together plausibly preserving or partially restoring pigment output in follicles with surviving MelSCs.
Evidence
The dossier notes occasional hair-darkening reports for minoxidil (uncertain, anecdote-grade). PMID 36853923 establishes MelSC depletion as the core greying mechanism that extended anagen and better follicular support could mitigate. No greying-specific trials exist. (refs: PMID 36853923)
Safety
Topical 5% minoxidil is OTC globally with a decades-long safety record; minor scalp irritation and unwanted facial hair are the main issues. Systemic absorption is minimal (this is topical, not oral minoxidil — the oral low-dose form is avoided given cosmetic-indication risk tolerance). Consumer access: any pharmacy foam/solution, applied to greying areas daily; judge over 6–12 months given anagen-cycle timelines.
Novelty rationale
Explicitly flagged: minoxidil is an established hair-loss drug. The new angle is the repigmentation/darkening signal plus an anagen-extension rationale aimed at MelSC preservation — a greying hypothesis, not an AGA rehash.
Reviews
  • plausibility 4/10, evidence 3/10, safety 9/10, novelty 4/10 — verdict revise. The central mechanism may be backwards: claiming that anagen extension 'gives the MelSC niche more regeneration cycles before depletion' is asserted, not supported, and increased cycling/proliferative demand is precisely the sympathetic-hyperproliferation route by which MelSCs are exhausted (per the stress literature and the correctly cited PMID 36853923) - minoxidil could as plausibly accelerate MelSC turnover as preserve it. The direct evidence is admittedly 'occasional hair-darkening reports' that the dossier itself grades as uncertain anecdote, and there are no greying trials despite decades of global minoxidil use - a conspicuous absence of a pigment signal in the largest informal dataset imaginable. The novelty disclaimer is appreciated but does not change that this is the world's most established hair drug with a speculative new endpoint. It survives only because topical 5% minoxidil is cheap, OTC, and safe enough that a self-experiment costs nothing; the anagen-preservation rationale should be deleted or inverted into a testable question rather than stated as mechanism.
Validation

No external validation recorded

Methods

Hypotheses were proposed by LLM subagents grounded in a curated AGA pathway dossier, PubMed excerpts, and the ChEMBL approved-drug universe; peer-reviewed (reflection), ranked by Elo through pairwise tournaments, refined by evolution over successive rounds, validated externally against ClinicalTrials.gov, OpenTargets, and PubMed co-mention novelty, and synthesized by meta-review. The orchestration code (hairloop) is deterministic and calls no LLM.
